 THE CROOKSHAVEN
 MURDER

 ∵

 CHAPTER I

 COMEDY


Gerard King frowned at a folded slip of paper that lay on the floor
at his side. It had fallen from his pocket when he pulled out a
handkerchief. He stooped with an angry exclamation, retrieved and
unfolded it, staring at some figures written across its face. His
eyes slowly dilated; his ire changed to dread, as he peered about
uneasily, glanced behind him into the shadows that encircled a desk
light, the room’s only illumination.

With an effort he shrugged, touched a lighted match to the paper and
watched it burn, scowling to see his fingers tremble as he tossed the
ashes into a tray.

‘I’m a fool. A doddering old fool--afraid of his own shadow. Worse
than one of the shrinking victims of blackmail in one of my own
detective stories.’

His words were scornful, yet his rather gaunt face mirrored their
literal sense rather than the bravado with which he had striven to
utter them.

‘This must be only some clumsy joke--a coincidence.--Yet some one
sent that paper to me--has sent other, similar ones----’

His jaw sagged open and his mutter ceased as something moved in the
darkness before him, a ghostly figure that floated toward him. But as
it approached into the light, it took form, became a young woman in a
white chiffon gown that drifted about her in a restless cloud. King
relaxed with a faint sigh, studying her vivacious, piquant face.

‘Mr. King, what is it?’ Her animation faded abruptly to alarm as she
looked into his eyes. ‘Something’s worrying you. I’ve noticed that
look on your face several times lately. What does it mean?’

The smile that had fled from her face seemed to leap across into his,
wiping away its fear.

‘Nonsense, my dear Miss Hawley. You’ve no right to impute such dire
notions to an old man’s reflections.’

‘Old man--tosh,’ she cut in indignantly. ‘But I know you’re worried.
Tell me what it is--let me help you as you’ve so often helped me.’

‘All right, Sylvia, forget your imaginative foolishness, and you
can--by making my little amateur mystery play a success.’

‘Oh, I’m primed to do my share, Mr. King--or Uncle, pro tem.’ She
smiled as she made this reference to her assigned rôle in the
entertainment he had planned so carefully for the amusement of
his week-end house-party. ‘Yet I’m sure you are bluffing me about
this. Somehow even I feel “all of a jump,” as Mrs. Bascom would
say--nervous, for no apparent reason. I wish you’d tell me----’

‘That must be stage fright, Sylvia.--It’s almost time for my curtain
speech of explanation to my guests, so I must hurry. Perhaps I’ll
tell you--some other time.--Now, then, let’s see. Paper-knife is
ready; red ink, in the drawer. The stage is set for the murder scene,
I guess, and the props all in order. I’ll take one last look just to
be sure.’

King disappeared into the darkness. A light flashed on with a click,
and he strode over to a row of chairs placed at the farther end of
the room. In one of these he seated himself and looked about swiftly.

It was a long room with walls of rough clapboards, ceiling of dusty
rafters, and crude plank floor, covered by threadbare rugs. In
reality it was a part of the hayloft of a barn, converted into a
study, or ‘workshop,’ as he called it, where King spent a large part
of his days writing detective stories that, started as a hobby, had
superseded all other work.

Three French windows had been cut into the walls to give light
and air. The one at the farther end of the room from him was
uncurtained and slightly ajar; the other two--one on his left, one
behind him--were covered with heavy portières which lapped over the
casements, effectually excluding any light from outside. A desk,
placed diagonally between the open window and the one on his left,
together with two straight-backed chairs, completed the meager
furnishings.

‘Just switch off all the lights except on the desk, will you, Miss
Hawley?’ asked King from his seat. ‘Great! You can’t see a thing from
here except the immediate area of the desk and a gray blur from the
open window. Aside from these two spots, the room is pitch black. I
couldn’t have picked a better night for my play. It’s so overcast, no
light can seep in to reveal too many details.’

‘It’ll be the darkest murder of the year!’ chuckled Sylvia.

‘I hope so,’ responded King. He tossed an old dressing-gown over the
back of the desk chair, turned off the light, and led Sylvia away,
discussing some final preparations still to be made.

A few minutes later, Sylvia returned to straighten up the desk and
put a fresh blotter in the pad. Approaching almost noiselessly in
the darkness, she hesitated just outside the door, thinking she had
heard a board squeak under a footstep in the room. But when she
called out--‘Who’s there?’--a faint creak was her only answer, so she
hurried in and switched on the light. The room was empty.

Sylvia was not a timid or imaginative woman normally, yet, as she
changed the blotter, she looked up twice, unable to throw off the
feeling that some one had been, or still was, in the room. Her task
quickly completed, she did not stop to investigate further, plunging
the room into darkness and hastening away, trying not to hear a sound
from behind her that might have been a person breathing or moving
lightly, stealthily.

When she reached the brightly lighted living-room of the house
itself, Gerard King was ensconced before the fireplace, addressing
his guests in a hearty voice.

‘--And I doubt every last one of you. All this talk about the ease
of solving a mystery once you find the clues is the theorizing of
amateurs. Clues, so-called, may be twisted any number of ways. As a
writer of detective fiction, I consider myself an authority on the
matter and challenge your sleuthing powers.

‘Now here’s a practical test for you. We’ll act out a play, a murder
scene--let you watch and hear everything except who actually does
the deed, then see who is clever enough to deduce the truth. I’ll
guarantee to provide sufficient tangible clues that, when rightly
interpreted, will prove the murderer’s guilt. As a spur, I’ll give
a prize to the one who actually proves--not merely guesses, mind
you--who the guilty one is.’

A clamor of applause greeted his words, but he held up his hand for
silence.

‘Of course you must observe certain conditions. You must not
interrupt the play nor move from your seats during it. You, Basil
and Ruel, will play the villains, I’ll be the victim. And we must
have a heroine too. Miss Hawley shall do that rôle.--Now, then,
after the play is over, I shall remain “dead” until my murderer has
been discovered. You can do as you please then, make deductions and
find clues--which you will discover in the room itself; but you must
not touch or disturb anything that might prove vital evidence. That
clear?--Then come along to my workshop. We’ll make that the scene of
the crime.’

When the guests trooped up the narrow, dark stairs to the loft, King,
who refused to have any lights turned on, was in the lead. Beside him
walked a slender, erect little woman who was speaking crisply.

‘You can put it any way you please, Gerard King, but making a play
and entertainment out of death is fool----’

‘Foolish? Of course, but what is any impromptu entertainment at a
house-party if not foolish?’

‘Impromptu?’ she echoed with a shrewd sniff. ‘It’s all a hoax of
yours--concocted well ahead of time, or I miss my guess. You brought
it about entirely too cleverly for an impromptu stunt.’

‘Right--as usual, Mrs. B., but I defy you to solve the mystery when
you witness it, just the same!’

‘Oh, you’ll probably bungle it enough times to make that easy,’ she
gibed. ‘You just watch me.’

They had no time for further conversation as King left his guests to
fumble into their places in the darkness.

Mrs. Bascom picked out a prominent chair in the front row, tucked her
feet neatly under it, and stoically endured being bumped and prodded
rather than relinquish her seat. She had not thoroughly approved
of this idea of acting out a murder--it seemed vaguely wrong, like
tempting Providence; yet, since King intended to carry it through,
Mrs. Bascom intended to miss no detail of it!

When the confusion and murmur of voices had somewhat abated, a light
at the farther end of the room flashed on, and Mrs. Bascom leaned
forward--though she would have stoutly denied any feeling of interest
or excitement then. At his desk sat King, his dressing-gown loosely
tied about his waist, revealing a paper sticking out of his vest
pocket. By squinting, Mrs. Bascom made out the words printed on its
outer fold--‘Last Will and Testament’--before he withdrew it to read
its contents. He was smiling as he did this, but the expression
changed abruptly to consternation, it seemed to Mrs. Bascom. He
frowned, seized the sheet as if to tear it, then shrugged and
obviously shook off this mood of surprise.

The incident passed so quickly that most of the guests accepted it as
a bit of clever acting, but Mrs. Bascom checked off a point on her
finger which she bent down to her palm. That was not all acting, she
commented mentally. At the very outset, something had gone wrong,
some mistake had been made and he was trying to conceal it. Nor
was it an ordinary one to have called forth such an expression of
surprise, almost of--well, dread, for lack of a better term, thought
Mrs. Bascom. Yet evidently he had decided to overlook it and go on
with the play. She wondered----

Her speculations were interrupted by a low, scratching sound like
pebbles thrown against glass. King half turned toward the French
window that stood ajar, and called out gruffly, ‘Come in.’

It flew open with a crash and Major, his ecstatic puppy, bounded into
the room, only to stop short, bracing himself clumsily on all four
paws at the burst of laughter from the guests behind Mrs. Bascom.
His expressive tail drooped, one ear cocked up, and his head tipped
quizzically. Then he hesitatingly sat up to beg, as if it were the
only thing to do under the circumstances! A whispered pleading and
insistent snapping of fingers from outside the window were hardly
necessary to inform Mrs. Bascom that this actor, though effective,
was usurping another’s rôle. She bent a second finger with a chuckle,
wondering if she would have hands enough for all the mistakes that
seemed predestined to occur.

Reluctantly the dog dropped to his feet again and obeyed the summons
from without, at which the expected actor made his anticlimactic
entrance. It was Basil Stacey, wearing a long yellow slicker and
carrying a slouch hat in his hand. His was a jolly, likable face,
yet to Mrs. Bascom the concentrated light seemed to bring out the
weakness of his jaw and lack of strong character.

‘Beastly sort of a night to set a man on a cross-country hike,’ he
began at once as he advanced to the desk. ‘Of all the toe-stubbing,
shin-barking darkness I ever stumbled through----’

‘It was necessary,’ interrupted King, overdoing the severity of his
tone. ‘No one knows you came or shall see you go, understand?’

A bark of remonstrance from outside smothered his answer.

The dialogue and acting that followed were decidedly unimpressive to
Mrs. Bascom. Both men were upset, almost too overcome with mirth to
carry out their rôles, much less make them effective. King demanded
some undefined papers; Basil declined to give them up. An argument
was clearly intended to follow, but Basil cut it short, unable to
play his part longer. With a sound that he tried to pass off as a
sob of emotion, he threw down a packet of papers on the desk, kicked
aside the chair by which he had been standing, and stumbled toward
the window. When he had passed out of the immediate area of the light
so that his expression could no longer be seen, he paused to shake
his fist menacingly, then disappeared into the darkness. But the
point of the scene had been hopelessly spoiled.

Mrs. Bascom gave an audible ‘Hump!’ which she hoped would reach
King’s ears, then fixed her eyes on his face as he thumbed the papers
in gloating silence before muttering, ‘I must put them into the
safe.’ He crossed the room, passing before the dim oblong of light
that defined the window, stooped down to fumble in the shadow beyond
it, swung open the door to the safe she knew stood against the wall,
and thrust in the papers.

Back again in his chair, he took up the will, drumming with his
fingers as he read it.

A footstep sounded from the window, and another figure materialized,
stepping inside and advancing slowly toward the desk.

‘Well, back again? Changed your mind about letting me have ’em?’
sneered King without turning his head.

‘Pardon me, sir. May I have a word with you?’

‘Oh, Ruel Moore. Well, I’m busy, but I’ll listen if you make it
brief. Need another loan, I suppose?’

King’s greeting seemed to confuse Ruel. He stood stiffly by the
side of the chair, and Mrs. Bascom relaxed as if in anticipation of
another scene about to miscarry. Ruel, typical gentleman farmer in
overalls and corduroy jacket, was too literal, too matter-of-fact for
such fooling.

‘No, Mr. King,’ the latter answered as dramatically as he could.
‘I’ve come to pay back what I’ve borrowed--with interest in full.’

King grunted, received the paper Ruel extended and glanced at it.
‘The check is correct.--How did you do it? Some one die and leave you
a fortune?’

‘Pigs!’ answered Ruel laconically. ‘If you’ll give me a receipt----’

He waited, manifestly ill at ease while the other scribbled on a
paper. This he thrust into his pocket without a glance.

‘Mr. King, there’s another matter.’

‘What, more pigs?’

‘No, that is, you see, it’s--it’s not that--er--gender.’

‘Oh, you mean sows--lady pigs?’

‘Yes, the--er--ladies--your niece, to be exact.’

The guests behind Mrs. Bascom laughed, hissed and applauded this
sally, but she suspected his embarrassment was due more to stage
fright or diffidence than deliberate cleverness of acting. At their
clamor, he stiffened, his jaw protruding with determination, his
hesitancy vanishing.

‘Young man, am I to assume you are proposing marriage to me?’

‘Yes--that is, not to you, but to your niece. She’s already accepted
me and we intend----’

‘Intend, do you?--Let me get this straight. Ruel Moore, farmer,
borrows money from Gerard King, feeds it to his hogs, and, through
luck, gets it back again. He pays up his obligations, then announces
his intentions of marrying his benefactor’s wealthy niece, thereby
clinching for himself--and his hogs--a future income.--And if she
were penniless, what then?’

‘Sylvia is, for all I know. Nothing would alter my intentions, for
that matter.’

Some one near Mrs. Bascom interrupted _sotto voce_, ‘You see, my
dear, his intentions are honorable!’ but King paid no attention to
this remark, launching into a tirade against the pseudo-lover,
the ‘brawny hero all aquiver to carry off his romantic--and
wealthy--heroine.’ His anger was well simulated even though his
bombastic words overdid the effect he tried to create. Ruel remained
silent.

‘Damn you, get out!’ roared King in a final burst. ‘My niece shall
never marry a man with a piggery for a home. Sylvia shall marry when
I choose and whom I choose, understand?--Now get out before I throw
you out.’

‘I go, sir, but I shall return.’

King sprang to his feet. ‘If it is the last thing I do, it will be
to prevent such a marriage. Let me find you so much as looking at
Sylvia again, and I’ll----Wait. Though I’ve made a will here leaving
everything I possess to her, before I sleep to-night I’ll add a
codicil cutting her off without a cent if she marries you. Then we
shall see how pressing our swain becomes!’

‘We shall see,’ echoed Ruel as he stalked toward the window.

Mrs. Bascom led the applause heartily. In her estimation, Ruel had
been a most natural actor after all, and her shrewd mind was already
at work surmising which of these men would prove to be the murderer,
as one was obviously cast for that rôle.

Meanwhile King scowled and sat down, muttering, ‘The insolence of
him. Sheer bravado----Why, he--Sylvia----’

‘Yes, Uncle,’ answered a voice from the neighborhood of the door.
‘You want me?’

Mrs. Bascom recognized Sylvia Hawley’s accent, watched the grace of
her silhouette as it passed across the gray of the window and into
the spot light of the desk.

‘I do, Sylvia. Come here,’ replied King sternly.

Even Mrs. Bascom was surprised at the transformation in King’s
secretary. Gone was her severe business dress and manner, the air of
efficiency and briskness. She was animated, provocatively lovely.

‘Something wrong, Uncle?’

‘Decidedly so.--Ruel Moore has just been here.’

‘To pay back his debt? I shouldn’t think that would distress you.’

‘You know what else transpired.’

‘Perhaps I could guess.--But it was nothing “decidedly wrong,” I’m
sure.’ Her averted eyes and hurried words attested to her skill as an
actress.

‘It was ridiculous--utterly impossible--preposterous!’

‘Ridiculous? Not--not our love, Uncle.’

‘Puppy love is always absurd.’ The comparison was not a fortunate
one, and King hurried on. ‘As to this----’

‘This is not--what you just called it!’

‘Words--quibbling. Listen to me, Sylvia. You shall not spoil your
life by a hasty marriage to a common farmer. You are too fine for
that.--No, let me finish.--When I brought you here to act as my
secretary, I had no other thought than kindness toward an orphaned
relative. But you have changed all that.

‘Now you have become a daughter to me. Why, only to-day I made you
heiress to my wealth. And as my niece--daughter, rather--you shall
not throw yourself away for a whim, a romantic passion. You have
scarcely a nodding acquaintance with life, and there are others, real
men, more worthy of----’

‘Social dummies, you mean! No, Uncle, I’ve seen that sort of
successes--men birthmarked with a dollar sign who have never faced
an issue in their lives. I’ve had to fight for a living, and I want
a man who can do the same, one whom I can help. Ruel is that sort of
man.’

‘Nonsense--a yokel. I know the world better than you do. You leave
such matters to me.’

‘The world, yes--but not my love!’

‘Look here, Sylvia, enough of this parrying. I forbid you to speak
of that man again, absolutely forbid you to have anything to do with
him, do you hear? I am your guardian, and while you remain here, you
shall obey me--for your own good.’

‘Very well. While I am here, I shall obey.’

‘Quite right.’

Without further reply, she walked away and left as she had come.

While the guests cheered this bit of the drama, Mrs. Bascom sat
tense. A vague movement from the window had riveted her attention.
It was a movement so slow and indistinct that she scarcely could be
sure it was not her own imagination. She had rather scoffed at the
speeches and acting thus far, but there was something arresting,
pulse-quickening about this furtive shadow that sent a shiver of
expectancy down her erect spine.

King stared after his pseudo-niece as if in doubt as to the meaning
of her last words, took a step and called her name. When she did not
answer or return, he shrugged and settled back in his chair. But
Mrs. Bascom was oblivious to all this. The shadow had taken outline
against the window, became a man, peering stealthily into the room,
though his features were, of course, indistinguishable.

In spite of Mrs. Bascom’s scorn at the idea of the play, now that
she sensed the approach of the climax--the murder scene--she became
excited, clasping her hands tightly, thus wiping out all the errors
she had started to count, and wetting her thin lips in the darkness.
Something made her uneasy, ‘creepy’ she would have described the
feeling. The enacting of a murder, of death by violence, had not
appealed to her as an appropriate subject for amusement, but it was
more than a mere sense of indelicacy now. She was acutely nervous----

‘Stop being so skittish, Ellen Bascom,’ she admonished herself. ‘It’s
nothing but your own imagination--all worked up just because it’s
dark and sort of spooky.’

This seemed to calm her for a while and she watched keenly for some
clue to the identity of the silent intruder and the meaning of his
actions.

Meanwhile, King sat gazing into the light in thought, then blinked
and abruptly snapped off the switch. The room became pitch black,
except for that shadow against the window where a man waited
patiently.

A match flared up as King lighted a cigarette. Its tip made a glowing
point of light as he puffed a few times, then tossed it into an ash
tray on his desk. The whistling sound of his breath became more
pronounced and regular. He was supposedly asleep.

Then the man at the window moved, tiptoed into the room, halting
at each step to listen and peer into the darkness toward the desk.
He did not turn his head enough for his face to be seen in profile,
however, and it might have been either Ruel or Basil for all Mrs.
Bascom could tell.

At last he passed out of line with the dim light of the window,
became invisible. She strained to trace his movements, but in vain.
She heard him shuffle toward the desk. There was a soft scraping,
perhaps when his fingers fumbled over the desk or blotter. Mrs.
Bascom thought she heard a rustle, then a swish as from a quick,
sudden movement of an arm. The gasping sigh that followed was
convincing. She shuddered as King’s stertorous breathing abruptly
ceased.

A thud announced the falling of a body against the desk. Something
metal clattered to the floor and she caught her breath sharply to
stifle an exclamation. It all seemed so vividly real.

Out of the obscurity a voice whispered tentatively, ‘Uncle.’ When
there was no reply, a woman’s figure glided from the doorway into the
oblong of light, advanced toward the safe and stooped down before it.
Mrs. Bascom recognized this was Sylvia.

The knobs twirled and clicked, the door swung open. A sheet of paper
appeared in her silhouetted hand, and she held it close to her eyes
as if to read it in the half light. Satisfied with her scrutiny,
she tore it into tiny bits which she scattered on the floor, then
chuckled, and slipped away through the door as she had come.

Silence--broken by a deep sigh like the exhaling of a breath held
almost to the bursting point. A cautious fumbling, the click of a
ring or finger nail as it rapped against wood, then a shuffling of
footsteps, and the man appeared abruptly before the window. He paused
to wipe his fingers meticulously on a handkerchief which he withdrew
from his pocket. This done, he produced a pair of gloves and slowly
drew them on his hands, just as there was a second sharp sigh and
movement from the direction of the desk.

To this latter sound, the actor paid no attention, yet it had a
curious effect on Mrs. Bascom. She held her breath, peered toward
the desk, and half arose to her feet. King’s first death sigh in the
play had been gruesomely realistic; this one seemed unnatural, out
of keeping with the action. There was no definite reason for her
feeling, yet she became stifled, oppressed. It was too much. She must
relieve her tenseness, cry out, or----

She sank back, flushing.

‘You skittish old fool--as emotional as a school girl! Stop making
a scene of yourself,’ she whispered, forcing her eyes to the man
kneeling before the safe to listen for the fall of the tumblers in
the combination.

At length his efforts succeeded and the door swung part-way open. But
he did not reach in, instead became rigid, listening intently.

Somewhere outside the room a board squeaked under the pressure of
a foot. A woman’s voice hummed a tune, then a chair scraped along
the floor. A typewriter clicked as a paper was inserted into the
carriage, the keys tapped out a brief sentence, then the chair
scratched again as if the woman had stood up. There followed a
smothered exclamation, a curious thump, dying away in a series of
low, bumping jerks as when some bulky object is dragged across the
floor.

The thief sprang to his feet and leaped toward the window, stepped
across its sill and vanished. But his hands were empty, Mrs. Bascom
noted.

She sat listening, still tense. After a brief pause, two men’s voices
came from a distance and the clatter of feet upon an uncarpeted
stairway. They stumbled in the darkness as they neared the door.
A hand fumbled along the wall, a switch clicked, and the room was
flooded with blinding light.

Involuntarily, as she blinked at the sudden illumination, Mrs. Bascom
turned first to look at the motionless figure, fallen forward on the
desk. But under this light, her unnatural fears vanished. After all,
it was only a play!

With a smile, she turned to Ruel and Basil who had just entered the
room.

 CHAPTER II

 TRAGEDY


King’s guests jumped to their feet, moving toward Basil and Ruel and
all talking at once.

‘Of all the ham actors I ever saw----’

‘The trained dog act was quite human----’

‘My dear, if he doesn’t move this minute to prove he isn’t dead, I
think I shall scream!’

‘Come on, let’s lynch the murderer!’

‘All right, but who is he?’ Basil laughingly challenged this last
speaker.

His question precipitated a chorus of shouts, some crying ‘Ruel,’
others ‘Basil,’ and trying to make the reason for their conclusions
heard above the din.

It was a cosmopolitan gathering of men and women, ranging, as King
had remarked earlier in the evening to his secretary, ‘From youth,
almost to senility and back again.’ The former might best be typified
by Mrs. Tenney, vivacious and sophisticate from the tips of her smart
pumps to the remarks that gushed from her carmined lips; the latter
by Mrs. Bascom or ponderous Judge Brant, who said nothing, but smiled
with a tolerant, superior air.

The hubbub continued as they milled about Basil until he held up both
hands in a plea for silence.

‘For the love of crime, you’re making enough noise to wake the dead
there, but you haven’t a scrap of evidence on which to base your
judgments. How about searching for the clues first?’

‘May we question the suspects, you and Ruel?’ asked some one.

‘Certainly not. We’d only deny our guilt, and besides you’re supposed
to know all and more than a detective would when called upon the
scene. Haven’t you just _seen_ the murder committed?’

‘Insinuating I have cat’s eyes?’ laughed Mrs. Tenney.

Basil ignored the remark.

‘Now remember your instructions, and obey them as the last words of a
dying man--Ruel, the papers!’

The latter distributed to each of the guests a pencil and a sort of
handbill on which was drawn a plan of the room with a red ink ‘X’ to
mark the position of the body, as in a newspaper diagram. Below this
were tabulated spaces to be filled in with the name of the murderer,
list of clues found, deductive reasoning, and such details, ending
with a line for the signature of the person submitting it.

When these had been received, they all began to scurry about the
room, utterly disregarding most of the rules that had been imposed by
King, moving the few furnishings and looking in corners and under the
rugs as if a clue might be found like a lost jewel or elusive collar
button. The curtains over the windows were jerked aside until they
hung askew; they jostled one another in laughing eagerness, ran in
and out of the open window looking for footprints--and discovering
their own! Only the realistically crimson-daubed paper-cutter on the
floor and the motionless figure crouching over the stained blotter
escaped their onslaught.

The two actors in the play watched them for a time in smiling
derision. Standing together, they were much alike in height and
general build, save that Ruel’s body was thickset with hardened
muscles, Basil’s with flabbier flesh. But there the similarity
ended. Basil Stacey’s face, upon closer scrutiny, lost some of its
geniality in the weakness of its pointed chin that lacked strength
of character; his cheeks were too flaccid, and his eyes were faintly
underlined with telltale shadows. Ruel Moore, on the other hand,
seemed serious even when smiling, and his features, each in its own
way, emphasized the virile force that his companion’s lacked. Sun and
air had tanned them a healthy brown that accentuated the firmness of
his jaw against his white collar, while his clothes, donned to be in
keeping with his rôle in the play, were shabby and ill-fitting in
comparison with Basil’s immaculate evening dress.

After a brief word or two with Ruel, Basil crossed over to the safe
upon which he stepped for a platform and began to speak.

‘Now that the room looks as if an excavating party had done its
best,’ he shouted, ‘I should hope some one has found the missing
link. If that’s your idea of detectives at work----’

His further words were drowned in a roar of laughing denials, while
the guests, by common consent, crowded closer to him.

‘Every one satisfied he’s seen all there is to be seen?’

There were cries of ‘Yes,’ ‘No,’ and ‘But there wasn’t a darned clue
anywhere!’

‘All right, then who has solved the mystery?--No, don’t answer aloud.
Say it with words. Fill in your blanks and sign on the dotted line.’

So many objections were raised to this long-winded process that Basil
acceded to their wishes.

‘Very well. Those in favor of Ruel as the arch-criminal, say
“Aye”--contrary minded, “No.” The “Ayes” have it and Ruel seems to be
elected. “Ayes” two paces to the right of me, “Nos” to the left.--So.
Now, how did you know Ruel did it? Answer in rotation.’

‘He had the motive. You see, he wanted to kill King before the will
was altered, and naturally thought it would be in the safe, so----’

‘That may be feminine intuition, but it’s no proof. Next!’ cut in
Basil imperiously.

‘Ruel is the stronger of you two and the victim died with scarcely
a sound from a single blow. Besides, you wore a slicker and the
murderer didn’t. That proves----’

‘Nothing, my dear Watson. I would have taken off the slicker before
killing him--if I had done it.’

‘I found a pebble on the floor, but darned if I see how that helps,
though of course I know Ruel did it,’ was Mrs. Tenney’s contribution.

‘In the name of justice, can’t any one produce some real evidence?’
exclaimed Basil in mock despair.

‘I can,’ spoke up Judge Brant confidently, elbowing his way to the
front of the group. ‘The proof is to be found in the characters of
the two suspects. Ruel opposed King openly; Basil was cowed by him.
Now, my experience, if I might say so, has dealt with many similar
cases and I could cite numerous instances to prove my contention. The
man who would take such a chance to kill another is certainly not
the one who cringes in his presence as Basil did, or was supposed to
do, let us say. That’s conclusive, so----’

‘It would never convict him in this court, Judge.--What do you say,
Dr. New?’

The man Basil addressed was thin, almost emaciated, with a narrow
face and jet black beard. It was a keen, alert face, however, and
when he spoke his voice was curtly authoritative.

‘I believe the real clue might be found on the paper-cutter, though
I haven’t examined it closely. There must be finger-prints on it
because he didn’t put on gloves until after he had used it for the
murder.’

‘Now we’re getting somewhere!’ encouraged Basil. ‘Look it over,
Doctor.’

One of the elder women gasped audibly as the latter picked up the
paper-knife by its pointed blade and studied the handle critically.

‘They’re here, but blurred. Can’t make them out enough--too faint.’

‘Then even you fail as a witness.--Who’s next?’

One by one the other members of the group made suggestions,
elaborated theories, only to have them tossed aside by Basil as
unsubstantiated by conclusive facts, hence unqualified to compete for
the prize. At last he turned to those who had selected him as the
criminal. There were only two: demure Mrs. Bascom who looked like
the wife of a country clergyman, and a pert young matron dressed in
a startling costume of cerise and blue silk. The latter spoke first
with the studied carelessness of one used to holding the center of
attention quite facilely.

‘I know you did it, Basil, simply because every one else believes
Ruel guilty! In detective stories, it’s always the one you least
suspect!’

She received a laugh of applause, but Basil shook his head.

‘And you, Mrs. Bascom?--You’re my last hope, I’m afraid.’

The elderly woman flushed slightly as so many eyes fixed on her, but
she spoke in a soft, precise tone, nipping off each word crisply.

‘I have three reasons for knowing you are guilty. In the first
place, the--the murderer put on his gloves before touching the safe
so as not to leave incriminating marks, presumably, in the heat of
his anger, forgetting to do so before. A man like Ruel, if bent on
revenge, would not have thought of this at all. In the second place,’
she checked it off on her fingers unconsciously, ‘you had the only
real motive--to regain those papers. No rational man would murder
the relative of the woman he was about to marry, especially when he
declared money was no object to him.

‘Finally, I noticed that you are left-handed. I saw this before the
play. The paper-knife was on the floor to the left of--of Mr. King,
exactly where it would have fallen after a left-handed man struck the
blow.’

A cheer went up from the guests, but she scarcely noticed the
interruption in the flush of her eagerness.

‘A lot depends upon what those papers were, of course, and I’d like
to look in the safe at them. The murderer left empty-handed, so they
might prove to be a valuable clue----’ She hesitated questioningly,
adding, ‘That is, if they were really part of the play as I think.’

At Basil’s nod of acquiescence, she stepped forward and swung open
the half-shut door which no one had bothered to touch. The others
pressed closer to see what would be revealed.

A thin sheaf of papers held together by an elastic band lay on the
floor of the inner compartment. It was obviously the one that Basil
had given to King in the play. At first glance, the sheets seemed to
be blank, but the central one bore upon its outer fold the words--‘To
Whom It May Concern.’

Mrs. Bascom read this aloud, then faced Basil.

‘It is part of the plan, then, not something personal? Shall I open
it?’

‘Search me! I’m sure I never heard of a detective stopping to ask
such a question, though,’ he countered, stepping down from the
impromptu pedestal as if anticipating the dénouement that would
follow. ‘Open it first and ask questions after, would be my advice.’

There was a breathless silence as she unfolded the paper and began to
read its contents to herself, a smile of satisfaction spreading out
from the corners of her mouth.

‘Read it--read it aloud,’ clamored the others, surging about her in
an attempt to read over her shoulder.

Two men laughingly seized her by the elbows and lifted her to the top
of the safe before she could demur. She flushed again, cleared her
throat, then began to read:

 ‘Be you so keen as you seem
 And so wise as this paper to find,
 Some inkling of truth’s in your mind.
 I’m dead, but you, Sleuth, can avenge me;
 Let others now see why they’re blind!’

‘A cryptogram!’ wailed one of the women in the crowd. ‘That’s not
fair. After we worked so hard----’

Basil snorted aloud at this, but Mrs. Tenney interrupted with an
exclamation.

‘The old plotter! Now I understand about that will! I’ve been
wondering why he picked on me as a witness.’

‘What will? What do you mean?’ cried several in unison.

‘Why, he called three of us to witness a will this afternoon with
great formality. Of course I didn’t read it, but now I doubt if it
was a will at all. Probably some gibberish--part of his scheme to use
it in the play, I imagine.’

‘And that’s why Basil has been flaunting his left hand--doing things
to attract attention to the fact he is left-handed, I mean,’ added
another.

‘But this doesn’t help us to solve the cryptogram.--Have you any idea
what the jingle means, Mrs. Bascom?’

‘Surely. It’s the key to the murder or the corroboration of it,’
answered the latter, smiling down jubilantly upon their upturned
faces.

‘Then you were right? It was Basil?’

‘Of course. See, the first letter of each line, read downward, spells
his name!’ She held the paper facing them to prove her statement.

‘So you think you have me cornered, eh?’ mocked Basil suddenly. ‘Yet
what if I denied it? What if I told you that paper was only a false
scent and not a true clue at all?’

‘Why--why that’s not fair! You’ve got to confess,’ gasped Mrs.
Bascom, her triumph wilting at the very thought of all her careful
deductions being overthrown.

‘I won’t, though!’

‘If you don’t, the--the corpse will rise up to accuse you!’ she
retorted with a flash of inspiration.

The guests laughed and applauded her challenge, turning expectantly
toward Gerard King. But he did not stir.

‘Now, you see? He said he’d not move until the murderer was _proved_
guilty, so you’ll get no help from him!’

Mrs. Bascom became a beet red. She was normally a timid, rather shy
woman in a crowd, though _tête-à-tête_ she could hold her own with
the most agile-witted. A resident of the village of Crookshaven a
mile away, she had always amused King by her good nature and rather
tart common sense. He included her in all his house-parties, both
because she enjoyed them, and because she made herself useful as a
sort of hostess.

The necessity of playing up to her share in the game had, until now,
overcome her timidity, even elated her to such an extent that she
was positively enjoying her triumph. But this doubting and defying
of her proof upset her, brought back a consciousness of her former
diffidence in a devastating rush.

She felt she must say or do something, if only to end this torment
and turn their eyes away from her. Twice she tried to speak, but a
hot lump choked her voice, and she could think of no adequate retort.
Then anger, half at her own foolishness, half at Basil’s insistence
upon prolonging the cross-examination, came to her rescue. She jumped
down from the safe, starting over toward King’s motionless figure.

‘We’ll see if he won’t help me!’ she muttered as she advanced, laying
her hands on his shoulders and shaking him. ‘Mr. King, I----’

Her appeal stopped short. The flush swirled from her cheeks and her
outstretched hands drew slowly back from his shoulders. Her eyes
dilated as she stood there motionless, and her voice quavered as she
whispered, ‘Look--look!’

Her gentle shake had moved King’s head so that now it peered out
above his elbow. The face was bloodless, the eyes glazed and staring.
It was the face of a dead man!

Dr. New pushed rudely forward, halting at the corner of the desk and
turning to face the guests, his expression alertly professional.

‘Some of you men carry chairs into the next room for the ladies.
Escort them there and return immediately.’

His crisp words broke the spell, were punctuated by gasps and indrawn
breaths.

‘Mrs. Bascom, if you----’

‘I--I’m all right. I won’t faint, Doctor,’ she whispered, swaying
slightly, but gaining control of herself and resolutely leading the
way toward the door.

‘Please go there and wait, all of you ladies. Remember, we don’t know
anything certain as yet,’ soothed the doctor, shielding King’s body
with his own while the women, in a daze, moved out of the room. ‘We
will join you in a few moments.’

When they had gone and the men were reassembled, he turned swiftly
toward the body, bending closer to examine it. There was no need to
touch it. Gerald King was dead.

Dropping to his knees beside the chair, the doctor scrutinized the
stain on the dressing-gown, hidden from casual glance by the position
of the body as it bent forward toward the desk. It was a dark blotch
that surrounded a short slit in the cloth just over the victim’s
heart. Gingerly he untied the cord that belted it in at the waist,
folded it back enough to reveal the pleated dress shirt clotted with
blood about a small, deep wound.

His eyes darted from one face to another as he slowly stood up and
spoke.

‘Gentlemen, Mr. King died almost instantly while we were present in
this room. He was killed by some sort of a knife thrust that pierced
his heart. That, and his tight vest, account for the comparatively
small bleeding, for, of course, his heart ceased pumping the blood
almost instantly. The weapon was much the same size and shape as
that paper-cutter, I should say.’ He pointed toward the knife that
he had replaced on the desk a few minutes ago. ‘Obviously that is
not the one, however. Its stains are red ink or paint, certainly not
blood. Aside from that, I would have unhesitatingly named it as the
murderer’s weapon.’

‘You’re sure--he’s dead?’ muttered one of the men, unaware of the
incongruity of his question.

The doctor nodded silently as his gaze still searched their faces,
becoming fixed at last on Basil’s--pale, aghast, its lips gaping in
horror.

‘I am rather a stranger to most of you,’ began Dr. New, choosing
his words with care. ‘Perhaps that is fortunate, in a way, for
it will allow me to ask some questions impersonally--without any
accusation of prejudice. You see, I was King’s friend, and if there
is anything--if any of you can tell me----’

‘What’s that paper?’ interrupted one of the men quickly, pointing to
a sheet which lay just beyond King’s elbow on the desk.

‘The fake will he used in the play, I suppose.’

The doctor was about to continue when Judge Brant gave a startled
exclamation. He had leaned forward to glance at the paper and now
straightened up with a look of incredulity.

‘Fake will?--That remains to be seen. It looks like a formal will
in his own handwriting, duly signed by witnesses, and it makes Miss
Hawley his heiress.’

‘You mean--it’s not a fake? It’s legal?’ The question was Basil’s as
he pressed forward, hardly realizing he had spoken aloud.

‘It appears so. If it was signed knowingly as a will, witnessed by
the three parties each in the presence of the others, it would be
legal. Yet it can’t be! He said nothing to me about drawing such a
document. And no man in his senses would have written such a will
simply to use as a stage prop in a play.’

‘But Mrs. Tenney, I think it was, intimated that was exactly what
did happen!’ said Dr. New, still watching Basil’s face.

‘Then--then it stands. But I can’t understand----’

‘Is her real name and all given?’ asked Basil in a whisper.

‘Yes. On the surface, everything seems quite in order. I know it’s
King’s signature and if the others are _bona fide_----’

‘This all appears to upset you strangely, Mr. Stacey,’ interrupted
Dr. New suddenly. ‘May I ask you why?’

‘Nothing--it doesn’t. The surprise--the thought of all his plans for
distributing his wealth----’ He paused in confusion.

‘Well?’

‘Dr. New, I--It was because I am an interested party to King’s
estate. He had told me I was to receive a small share in it.
Naturally, I was--astonished.’

‘So? Well, tell me about your part in the play,’ replied the doctor
levelly.

‘Of course. I was so stunned for a moment, I’d forgotten that.’

‘Yet not so stunned as to wonder and ask questions about the will,
eh?’

‘I’ve explained that already.’

‘Go on, then, about the play.’ Dr. New sounded skeptical.

‘It was this way: Ruel and I----Where is Ruel now?’

For the first time the men noticed Ruel Moore was the only one absent
from the group.

‘He was here a moment ago, I’m sure,’ said one.

‘I saw him go out of the room just before Basil started to----’

‘Never mind that. He’s not here now,’ broke in the doctor curtly.
‘Some one see if by chance he is outside anywhere.’

He was not found.

‘Let us hear the rest of Mr. Stacey’s story. We’ll make a more
thorough search in a moment. Go on, please.’

‘Why, he did it--the pseudo-murder, I mean. You see Mr. King
concocted the scheme of the play, mapped out the parts and so on,
though some of the details, such as the will, the note in the safe,
and such clues, he kept to himself. I was supposed to act the--the
murderer just as Mrs. Bascom deduced. My finger-marks were all
planted on the paper-cutter in advance as one of the main clues--that
and the way I was to drop it on the floor. But Ruel and I planned a
little joke of our own. We decided to switch rôles just to confuse
things and fool King himself. Ruel was to drop the knife on King’s
right instead of----’

‘But it was found on his left!’

‘That’s what I can’t understand. We thought----’

‘Keep to what you did, please.’

‘I’ve told you all I know. We did as planned.--That’s why Ruel’s
fingers confused the prints of mine, why I challenged Mrs. Bascom
and all. But of course he can explain. He wouldn’t--couldn’t have
done--this,’ Basil added in defense of his absent friend.

‘Then who did?’ The judge’s voice unconsciously snapped. ‘We saw with
our own eyes--that is, we--er----’

‘We must find Ruel Moore at once,’ Dr. New interrupted the judge’s
floundering. ‘Have you any idea where he might have gone, Stacey?’

‘Perhaps he--No!’

‘Well?’ prodded the doctor as Basil closed his lips firmly.

‘I don’t know where he went.’

‘Come, Stacey. This is not the time for hedging. Where do you think
he went then?’

Basil looked away in indecision, then answered slowly, ‘I was only
guessing, but it occurred to me he might have gone after----’

‘Basil--Basil!--Oh, there you are. I want a word with you.’

The men whirled about to face Ruel who had just entered by the open
window. He was breathing rapidly.

‘Why--Is anything the matter?’ he called tensely as no one spoke. His
face was already pale under its tan; his muscles were taut, and he
seemed to be under some strain.

‘Mr. King is dead--murdered!’ cut in Dr. New sharply. ‘He was stabbed
in the heart.’

‘King--dead--murdered?’ muttered Ruel, swaying forward and gazing
blankly from one to another. ‘But it’s impossible! I scarcely touched
him at all. I----’

‘Then you admit you acted the part of the murderer in the play?’

‘Of course. Basil and I----’

‘Stacey told us about that.’

‘But King was alive then. He whispered to me.’

‘Before you struck the fake blow?’

‘Yes.’ Ruel did not seem to understand the purport of these
questions. ‘He can’t be dead!--If this is another joke----’

‘See for yourself!’ The doctor stepped aside abruptly, and Ruel’s own
eyes confirmed his doubts.

‘You don’t think--you don’t mean _I_ did it?’

‘You were the only one near him by your own confession,’ pointed out
the doctor.

‘But I tell you, I didn’t kill him! It’s preposterous.’

‘Words, Mr. Moore--your denials against the evidence of our own eyes.’

‘It was dark. You couldn’t actually see what happened.’

‘All the more reason the burden of proof rests upon you.’

‘Proof? How can I prove it, except to give you my word! I never
killed King. I swear it.’

He was about to deny his guilt vehemently again, when it suddenly
seemed to dawn upon him that the ladies were not in the room. This
recalled the matter about which he had been seeking Basil. He became
still more agitated as he spoke.

‘Basil, have you seen Sylvia--Miss Hawley?’

‘Why, no, not since the play.’

‘She’s disappeared--vanished!’ muttered Ruel in a flat, lifeless
voice. ‘I’ve been looking for her everywhere. That thumping noise--as
if something were being dragged along the floor just after she
stopped typing in the play. It sounded queer. I looked for her to ask
about how she made it. I couldn’t find her--anywhere. The servants
were below in the barn, preparing the supper King had planned to
serve there, but they swear she did not come down the stairs. She
didn’t come out the window, so----’ He gestured vaguely, hopelessly.

But no one followed his gesture. All eyes automatically turned to
rest upon the will--the will that made Sylvia Hawley an heiress!

 CHAPTER III

 SUSPICION


It was Mrs. Bascom who took the initiative and carried out the
suggestion Dr. New made; that is, to proceed to the house and there
await the men in more comfortable quarters. She was still pale from
the shock of having been the one to discover King’s murder, but her
lips were set in a firm line as she marshaled the women down the
stairs. The very fact that there was something to be done stimulated
her.

The doctor nodded to himself as she took charge. He had rather
expected she would be the one to rise to the emergency. Heroism,
leadership, bravery--these are not necessarily the prerogatives of
greatness: more often they are the results of circumstance, and the
demand for action in a crisis frequently produces unexpected strength
in an otherwise meek and drab figure. So with Mrs. Bascom, necessity
bred courage.

The first rustle of wind whined through the trees, bearing with it
the cool moisture of an approaching storm, as they hurried down the
gravel drive and into the house, a massive bulk against the metallic
sky. One by one Mrs. Bascom switched on all the lights in the lower
rooms as if to dispel her own mental gloom by the friendliness of the
glare. Yet the women, silent and shaken by what they had so recently
witnessed, stood about the living-room apathetically, huddled closer
together for mutual comfort and shuddered as they heard Mrs.
Bascom’s voice calling up the police station in the village.

When she returned from this errand, she sensed their dread and began
to bustle about with an air of briskness, assumed to cloak her own
emotions.

‘Do sit down--every one,’ she urged, pulling up chairs into a
friendly circle about the huge open fireplace. ‘I think a little
blaze would help, don’t you? It’s going to rain any minute now, and a
big house like this gets so damp and chilly these spring nights.’

Suiting her actions to her words, she struck a match and touched
it to the kindlings beneath the birch logs, then dropped into the
nearest chair and spread out her hands toward the blaze. As the
crackling tongues of flame licked the whiteness of the bark, changed
it to golden brown, then charred black, the others followed suit,
pulling up the chairs closer or sitting down upon the low, cushioned
bench that stood before the brass fender.

‘I can’t seem to realize the--the awful tragedy of it all,’ began one
of the women. ‘To think that he was laughing and planning----’

‘Let’s not think of that part,’ interrupted Mrs. Bascom. ‘No one
feels the tragedy more than I, but what I want to consider is,
whether we can be of help now.’

‘There’s no way we can that I see.’

‘Obviously not. Yet let’s think--get behind the obvious.’

The idea impressed them, created a thoughtful silence until Mrs.
Tenney spoke hesitatingly, staring into the fire. Her smooth chin was
propped up in her hands; her face no longer was animated and smiling,
as she drew her penciled brows into a frown of concentration.

‘I wonder--Wouldn’t it help if we each tried to recall exactly what
we saw, or thought we did?’

‘Excellent, my dear. You mean one of us might have noticed some
little action or detail the others had overlooked?’ exclaimed Mrs.
Bascom.

‘Yes, that’s it.--You think it was Basil who acted that part in the
play?’

‘I’m sure of it. I think he denied it only for effect, to confuse
me and prolong the--the game. But I’m just as certain he did not do
it--commit murder, I mean.’

They all nodded in agreement, and Mrs. Bascom deliberately spun out
the discussion to draw their thoughts away from the horror of it all
into more constructive channels. She reiterated that she and all of
them had known Basil for some time, had seen evidence of his sincere
friendliness with King, hence knew he could have no motive for
murder. Moreover, he did not impress her as the type of man either to
plan such an act deliberately or to do it in a sudden burst of anger.

‘Yet who else could have done it?’ asked Mrs. Tenney. ‘_Some one_ was
there in the darkness and he----’

‘Perhaps more than one was there! Still, that’s useless speculation,
as we haven’t been told a thing yet, beyond the fact that King was
killed.--Wait, I’ve an idea--two of ’em, in fact. First, we must be
searched.’

‘Why? What would you expect to find?’

‘I don’t know! I mean, of course, nothing will be found, but we
ought to do it just the same--to prove we’re innocent of concealing
anything, at any rate. I’ll be first. You search me, Mrs. Tenney, and
then I’ll be responsible for the rest of you. Don’t hesitate to be
thorough. You see I’ve more opportunity than you have for concealing
things!’ She smiled without embarrassment at her longer and more
voluminous gown of modest gray silk.

The search was carried out in an orderly fashion, but without result.

‘There, that proves we’re not accessories, at least. Now for my
second inspiration. We still have those pencils and sheets of paper
with the plan of the room. Let’s each write down her impressions,
make a record of them while they’re still fresh in mind.

‘You’ve all kept the papers? Good.’ She procured some magazines from
the table and distributed one to each. ‘Use these for lap pads to
write on. I suggest we answer the questions given here first--the
ones about clues and so on, not thinking of them in connection with
the play, but more in the light of what happened in reality. Then we
can add anything else we recall that might help; what we saw, who sat
next us, and such points.’

‘But we seated ourselves in the dark! I didn’t notice who was beside
me, in the excitement.’

‘I did. When the lights went up, Bob was on my right and----’

‘Write it down, don’t confuse us by telling now,’ suggested Mrs.
Bascom, already busy with her answers.

They were still thinking and writing when the rattle of an automobile
stopped outside the house and Mrs. Bascom arose to admit the local
police force of two, augmented by the village doctor and one other
man. She directed them to the barn where the men guests were waiting.

A gust of wind and driving rain swept into the hall, and Mrs. Bascom
shivered as she pushed against the door to close it. Mechanically
she stepped over to the mirror and paused to tuck up a lock of black
hair which had blown loose from her modestly sleek coiffure. As she
regarded the reflection of her pale face, she closed her eyes for a
moment, whispering to herself, ‘Play up, Ellen Bascom, an’ the Lord
give you strength an’ courage.’ Then she straightened her narrow
shoulders and returned to the other women.

Their accounts completed, Mrs. Bascom collected the papers and began
to read the answers aloud, discussing each with its author and
occasionally jotting down an additional note which their talk served
to bring out. There was little of apparent value in their statements,
however, the most noteworthy fact being the wide divergence of
inference which these eye-witnesses had drawn from the same scene
they had all witnessed.

Just as they concluded the last statement, the men filed into the
room with the two officers bringing up the rear, Ruel walking between
them. At a nod from the shorter one, who seemed to have assumed the
authority, his companion closed the door and stood guard before it.

‘Now then, ladies,’ began the former, taking up a position at one
end of the fireplace, his rather guileless blue eyes seeming to look
everywhere except at them, although he was already trying to fix a
picture of each face in his mind. ‘It kind of looks as if this case
wasn’t so ballin’ up as you might think.’

His voice was pleasantly drawled and conversational in tone. At first
glance, he might have been taken for a healthy, middle-aged farmer,
dressed in seedy working clothes. His face contained little to single
it out from any of a dozen men such as are seen in a village street,
ready leisurely to pass the time of day or swap the latest titbit of
local gossip.

‘It ain’t exactly a pleasure, me comin’ here to do what I have to do,
but murder ain’t pleasant anyways you look at it. What I mean to say
is this: there’s one here as is pretty strongly suspected, you might
put it, only before I do anythin’ about him, I want you all to tell
me what you saw, like the men here have done.

‘I don’t know any of you intimate, ’cept Mrs. Bascom an’ Ruel, but
I’ve seen some of you kitin’ through the village in autos an’ things,
so I’ll just ask some questions, friendly like.’

‘Might I interrupt your concise explanation for a moment, Mr.
Sewell?’ asked Mrs. Bascom with an asperity that plainly indicated
some personal feeling toward him that was not exactly flattering to
his intelligence.

‘Certain sure, Mrs. B. Fire ahead, only I guess I’ll kind of be
“Inspector” Sewell to you all, if you don’t mind.’

‘Hump!--Well, it might save you a good deal of useless trouble if
you’d read these papers before asking your questions, Ezra Sewell.’

‘Papers? What’s in ’em?’ he grinned, ignoring her use of his name.

‘The answers to your questions, probably.’

‘But I ain’t asked ’em yet, ma’am.’ He put on a pair of bone-rimmed
glasses that sat quite incongruously on his stubby nose. ‘Oh, tells
about what you saw an’ all.--I might have known you women-folks
wouldn’t just set here doin’ nothin’. That’s mighty helpful.’

‘We searched each other, too,’ added Mrs. Bascom shortly.

Sewell nodded, shuffling through several of the sheets and looking at
one item in particular on each. He slowly wagged his head at what he
found.

‘You all seem to make the same mistake on one thing sure enough,’ he
ruminated, placidly continuing to scan the accounts or painstakingly
puzzling out some illegible handwriting.

‘He probably means about Basil exchanging rôles with----’

‘Hold on a bit, Doctor. I wasn’t goin’ to tell that just yet. Seein’
as how you’ve started unloadin’ the cart, though, you might as well
finish the job, I guess. You sort of worked it out first, anyway,
near as I can make out.’

Briefly the doctor outlined the cause of King’s death and the
true account of the play, adding meticulously, ‘It’s all very
involved--Mr. Moore playing a joke on King by switching rôles with
Mr. Stacey, then reversing the joke on the latter, so he claims, by
placing the knife on the left instead of on the right. Like most
practical jokes--but that’s irrelevant. He also swears he knows
nothing of the actual murder, declaims his innocence----’

‘And quite naturally too! Any person with an atom of sense could see
he is innocent,’ said Mrs. Bascom hotly, springing to her feet.

‘Far as I can make out, Mrs. B., it don’t seem to be much a case of
seein’ anythin’, because it happened in the dark,’ suggested Sewell
calmly.

She sat down again with an audible sniff, her cheeks red, her eyes
snapping at this clever rebuff. But Ruel threw her a look of thanks.

‘Understand me, ma’am. I’ve known Ruel Moore about as long as you
have, an’ as Ezra Sewell I’d take his word quick as--quicker than
most. But as _Inspector_ Sewell, takin’ words for things is a gray
mare of another color. Evidence is evidence, an’ there’s got to be a
mighty lot of explainin’ to this thing yet.’

‘But, Ez, I told you I didn’t even touch King,’ pleaded Ruel.
‘Heaven knows I had no motive. He’s been so kind to me I’d rather
die for him than kill him. I’ve explained everything to you exactly
as it happened, and I swear it’s the whole truth--everything I
know. Suspect me if you have to now, but for God’s sake let’s do
something--find Miss Hawley before--before anything more happens.’

At his outburst, Mrs. Bascom sat up straight and looked about her at
the women. In her agitation she had not missed Sylvia before.

‘What do you mean, Ruel? Where is she?’

‘God knows, Mrs. B. She’s disappeared!’ groaned Ruel, sweat breaking
out on his forehead.

Thoughtlessly he raised the back of his hand to wipe it away, then
seemed to recall he was not out in the fields so drew out his
handkerchief instead.

‘Yes, and the will King made and left on----What’s that?’ finished
the judge sharply, pointing to Ruel’s hand.

Stupidly Ruel stared at the handkerchief he held. It was stained with
great spots of red.

‘It’s blood, Ruel. Where’d it come from?’

‘I--I don’t know, Ez.’

‘He wiped his hands on it in the play just after the murder! We all
saw him do it,’ interrupted the judge.

‘So? Well, it just seems, Ruel, as if that was one of them last
straws they talk about. I guess I’ll have to ask you to come along
down with me a spell.’ Sewell’s words were delivered in a tone
that made them sound more like some pleasant invitation than an
official arrest. ‘You go along with Pete there, an’ I’ll see you
later.--’Course, it ain’t exactly legal, arrestin’ you without no
warrant this way, but you can sue me for false arrest if you’re a
mind to. I figure it’s best you go peaceable, anyway, an’ we’ll tend
to the rest later.’

The man at the door stepped forward obediently, but he did not touch
Ruel, merely stood by his side until he turned to move away.

‘I’m sorry, Ruel, old man, but don’t worry. It’ll come out all
right,’ sympathized Basil. Ruel did not seem to hear.

‘Ezra Sewell, the Lord forgive me for callin’ you a fool!’ sputtered
Mrs. Bascom. ‘Of all the idiotic, preposterous----’

‘There now, Mrs. B., save all them dictionary words for a more
understandin’ critter than me, who’s just doin’ a duty as plain as
the nose on your face--not speakin’ personal, I mean to say.--Get
along, you two.’ But his suggestion was unnecessary; Ruel and the
officer had gone.

‘Now, then, folks, there’s lots to be tended to yet. First off,
what’ll I do with you all?’ He addressed the question to them as if
seriously awaiting an answer.

‘Of course we’re at your mercy--your command, Inspector,’ suggested
one of the men, not too seriously in view of the latter’s rather
casual methods. ‘We want to do everything we can to help, but what
about to-morrow? I, for one, must get back to business, and, besides,
this house--after to-night----’

‘I was just considerin’ all that, mister. Now seems to me kind of
useless, keepin’ you all here, though, on the other side of the
fence, seems as if I ought to. Well, since you’ve been searched an’
with the women-folks’ statements an’ names, I guess you can go. You
leave me your names too an’ where I can get you if needs be, an’ I
guess that’ll be enough.’

‘I’m sure I can arrange for a few days’ leave. I’ll stay,’ said Judge
Brant quickly. ‘Naturally I want to help, and there’s the question of
the will to investigate.’

‘That’s fine.--An’ you’ll stay too, Doctor, at least till after the
inquest?’

‘Of course if you really need me I’ll manage it somehow,’ answered
the latter, a little reluctantly.

‘Good.--I do, ’cause I’ll need your testimony, professional like.
Now, if you other gentlemen will give me your names an’ addresses an’
telephone numbers, please, I’ll be much obliged.’

One by one they gave the required information while Sewell
laboriously wrote it down in a tattered notebook he drew from
his pocket, using a borrowed pencil which he wet with his tongue
frequently. This done, he made a stiff, awkward bow and walked out of
the room after requesting the doctor, the judge, and Basil Stacey to
accompany him.

‘Well, of all the unprepossessing, comedy “constabules” I ever saw!’
exclaimed Mrs. Tenney in a voice that certainly must have reached
that dignitary’s ears.

‘Fool!--I will say it--fool!’ muttered Mrs. Bascom. ‘Oh, Basil, wait
a minute.’

She detained him at the door, questioning him about the mysterious
will and Sylvia’s disappearance. When he had answered briefly, she
seized him by the arm excitedly.

‘Somethin’ has happened to her. She’d never go off like that of her
own free will.’

‘I’m sure of it, Mrs. B., and I mean to start on a search for her
right away. This--this Sewell dismisses her disappearance by saying
that if she’s abducted, she’d be far away and well hidden by now,
while the murder is more important to investigate first. But I’m
going to tell him I’m off to find her--this minute.’

‘You will--you must,’ whispered Mrs. Bascom to herself as he hurried
down the hall after the other three men.

Rain was pelting down in drenching streams as he ran the few steps
between the house and barn, struggling into a raincoat he snatched up
from the hall. The others had made a dash for it unmindful of the
storm, and were just ascending the stairs when Basil called out.

‘Inspector Sewell, you don’t need me here, do you? I’m going to find
Miss Hawley.’

It was more an assertion than a question, and he started out into the
rain again before Sewell slowly answered, ‘I calculate that’s a good
idea, young man. Go to it.’

Continuing up the stairs, Ezra led the way directly to King’s
workshop and switched on the lights. The room was now empty, for the
village doctor and his helper had removed King’s body to the house
while the inquiry was taking place in the living-room. Yet there was
a feeling of tragedy and confusion about the room still. The curtains
hung drunkenly before the rain-dripping windows, the chairs, which
had not been removed for the ladies’ use in the next room, were
pushed about in uneven rows; King’s dressing-gown, taken off for an
examination of the wound, lay draped over the back of the desk chair.
Everything either directly or indirectly heightened the lingering
horror of the drama which had so recently taken place there.

The wind whined above the streaming rain, lashing the trees and
whistling around the corners of the barn like a banshee.

‘It sounds almost as if some one were calling,’ shuddered the judge.

‘Yep, it’s a funny thing, about winds. Seems like they always follow
your mood; sigh restful when you’re happy, an’ mournful when you’re
sad,’ commented Sewell, motioning the two men to remain by the door.
Then his voice became crisp, dropped its drawl, though his easy-going
manner did not change. ‘Just let me take a look about before you come
in, though there’s not much use, since every one’s mussed about so
already.’

Slowly, almost indifferently, it seemed, he walked once about the
room without stopping, his eyes flitting from one object to another.
This done, he examined each of the windows in turn. The one which
had been used as a door in the play was now closed to shut out the
storm, and he muttered as he gazed out of it, ‘No chance for marks on
that glass or sill after that washin’.’ The other two were locked and
equally uninteresting.

‘Let’s see now. This barn’s built into the side of the orchard hill,
ain’t it, so instead of this bein’ the second-story floor as it
should be, all them windows open right on the ground.’

‘That’s right,’ approved the judge, ‘except that, due to the
irregularity of the slope, two of them are about a foot above the
ground. The opened one is on the ground level, though.’

‘Um, that don’t help any. The other two were locked.--You said only
the desk light was on?’ Sewell lighted it, then walked over to one of
the empty chairs and sat down in it. ‘Switch off the rest so I can
see how it looked.’

When this had been done, he studied the desk in silence.

‘Now one of you go over there an’ turn off the desk light.’

Dr. New did this.

‘Move around a bit.--You’re movin’ now?--Hmm. That’s enough. The
floor’s squeaky an’ it’s black as a bat so I couldn’t see you.’

‘That’s exactly the way it appeared during the play, except for the
opened window.’

‘What do you mean by that, Judge?’

‘It wasn’t raining then, so was a little lighter. We could see
whatever moved in front of it--as when the men entered the window or
when Miss Hawley came in the door between us and it. There was only
light enough to make out silhouettes, not details, of course.’

Sewell advanced toward the desk once more and stared down at the
stained blotter.

‘Pretty positive that ain’t blood, Doctor?’

‘Sure of it. It’s red ink from the bottle you saw in the desk drawer.
King probably spilled it while the room was dark to carry out the
illusion. Besides, the very way the body leaned, the wound and blood
were below the level of the desk.’

‘Like this, eh?’

Sewell sat down in the chair and bent forward, though refraining from
touching the blotter.

‘That, or possibly a little farther forward.’

Sewell held his position without replying, for he had noticed certain
indentations in the otherwise smooth blotter--marks very indistinct
due to the dampening it had received from the ink, yet clear enough
to show some one had written at the desk since the fresh blotter had
been placed there. It looked like a single word.

‘Happen to know if this was a new blotter or when it was last
changed?’

The men shook their heads in the negative.

‘Wonder if any one else could tell me that?’

‘Probably Miss Hawley or one of the men in the play.’

‘Sure. Guess that’s a point I’ll have to find out about.--King didn’t
do no writin’ durin’ the play, did he?’

‘No, unless it was in the dark. Why do you ask?’

‘I was just wonderin’.’

Sewell took up the dressing-gown to examine it closely. He measured
with his eye the rent that the knife had made, estimating it was less
than two inches in length. Something rustled in one of the pockets as
he handled it, and he drew out a small slip of paper. It was slightly
wrinkled and torn on one edge, while on it was a single row of
penciled figures--‘2 4 5 7 8.’ The numbers were unevenly spaced and
irregular as if hastily done, each approximately an inch in height.

He was about to replace this in the pocket as unimportant for the
present, when his eyes fell again upon the marks on the blotter.
Bending forward as if to hold the paper nearer the light to study
it, he compared the figures with the indentations. Obviously they
coincided! This information he kept to himself, though he showed the
figures on the paper to the men.

‘Make anythin’ out of that?’

‘About the right length for an auto license,’ suggested the doctor.

‘It ain’t King’s, ’cause I’ve seen it often. But that’s an idea. What
do you say, Judge?’

‘Might be anything--or nothing,’ responded the latter absently,
shuddering at the sound of the wind. ‘What a night!’

Inspector Sewell nodded, carefully folded the slip and placed it in
his worn notebook.

‘This secretary, Miss Hawley, maybe she’d know that too, leastwise
she would if any one, I guess. Seems like she might know lots of
things, only she ain’t here to tell ’em. Hope maybe Basil finds
somethin’ about her.’

‘That reminds me. What do you make of this will affair?’ questioned
the judge. ‘I don’t like it. I was in King’s confidence and certainly
he mentioned nothing about such a radical change in his will to me.’

‘Don’t make nothin’--yet. The point of law is up to you, an’ the
rest--well, we’ll see later.’

‘But she’s disappeared, whether by force or----’

‘Hold on, there. I ain’t exactly worryin’ about her--now. Got enough
else on my mind. If she’s been took off, she’s hid by now, an’ if
she’s took herself off, she’s either hid or will come back when she’s
a mind to. My assistant, Pete, is startin’ things along that line an’
so is Basil. An’ that’s all we can do for the present.’

His fat thumb emphasized his words by pressing the switch button
and plunging the room into darkness. As they passed into the other,
unfinished part of the loft, Sewell turned on a second light and
paused to look at the small, dust-covered windows. They had no locks,
and one contained streaks in the dust of the sill, but it was useless
to raise the window for further examination, as the storm would only
destroy them.

Just at the right of the door leading into the workshop stood the
typewriter desk and chair which had been moved there for purposes of
the play when Miss Hawley was supposed to frighten away the intruder.
Over this Sewell bent in passing, reached behind the machine and
lifted out a paper which had fallen there. With this in hand, he
turned to the men.

‘If I recollect right, you said she wrote somethin’ on that machine
durin’ the play? Well, now, would it be much--a few words, or what?’

‘Not more than a line, I should say. She rattled it off very swiftly.’

‘I heard the bell ring only once,’ nodded the judge.

‘A few words, then. About this long, maybe?’

Sewell extended the paper which he had just picked up, and the men
read--‘Now is the time--2 4 5 7 8.’

‘Good Lord--that number again! You don’t suppose----?’

‘Queer, ain’t it?--An’ now she’s gone off somewheres,’ muttered
Sewell as he led the way to the stairs in the farther corner of the
room. But to himself he recalled that in the sequence of the play,
she had appeared at the safe just after the pseudo and the real
murder must have been committed! Which way had she entered, then,
from the loft or from the room itself?

 CHAPTER IV

 EZRA MAKES A PROFESSIONAL CALL


‘Feel sort of confidin’ now, son?’

Ruel ceased his pacing and whirled on the balls of his feet to face
Inspector Sewell.

‘You’ve found her?’

Sewell shook his head sorrowfully as he unlocked the bars and entered
the cell. Ruel continued his pacing without a glance at the door
which the inspector had left half open.

‘Nope, nothin’ to tell about that, yet.--You got anythin’ to tell me
though, Ruel?’ His voice was almost wheedling as he perched on the
edge of the cot, drawing up his knees and resting his chin upon them.
‘No?--Then it ain’t done no good, has it?’

‘What?’

‘Solitary confinement--sunlight makin’ patterns of the bars on the
floor--remorseful thoughts, an’ such,’ replied Sewell, tapping one
palm with the bulky key to the cell. ‘Now that you’re a formal
prisoner, arrested good an’ proper an’ all, seems as if you ought to
be feelin’ different, ’cordin’ to these here psychologists!’

‘The only remorseful thoughts I have are that I ever came here so
meekly in the first place, didn’t break away to look for Sylvia
myself before you had me arraigned before the local judge late last
night.’

‘Any particular reason why _you_ should feel that way?--No, don’t
answer, bein’ as it’s personal an’ unnecessary. Besides, I guess
them as know you an’ her like I do, can dope out a pretty close
answer, eh?’

‘There’s--there’s no definite reason--yet,’ stammered Ruel.

Sewell nodded, turned his head toward the corridor and called out,
‘Pete, soon as that Basil Stacey gets here, step him along in, will
you?’

A voice from the office answered laconically, ‘Yep.’

It was nine o’clock in the morning of the day after the tragedy.
The night had passed agonizingly for Ruel. He had not slept at
all, spending the time either pacing his cell or hunched with
his head in his hands, reliving each thought and action of that
fatal play with its subsequent revelations, striving to recall
something--anything--that might give a key to these events. But the
process had been in vain--only served to bewilder him more than ever.
His brain ached with the exertion, while his body suffered from the
lack of it. His face was pallid, his hair and clothes disheveled,
and his eyes seemed to have receded into his head. Yet all this
only added to the strength and determination of his face, the weary
dejection of his expression.

Ezra Sewell also showed the effects of his all-night search for
Sylvia Hawley, yet his eyes were alert and keen. A sparse cowlick
over his forehead made a sleek contrast to his otherwise tousled
hair, and as he perched there, his arms encircling his drawn-up
knees, he seemed a rather whimsical, weather-beaten sort of elf.

‘Look here, Ruel, it ain’t doin’ no good ragin’ round like a loco
pig. Set down now, so we----’

‘Pigs?--I’d forgotten mine. I suppose now they will----’

‘I ain’t. Pete’s tendin’ to them an’ feedin’ ’em all right.--But what
I mean to say is this: we’re doin’ all we can to find Miss Hawley,
an’ don’t mistake me neither. We’ve got to find her.’

‘I know, but I can’t stay here doing nothing. Let me help, Ez.’

‘No, son, can’t do that. Me--I’d turn you loose in a shake, only
don’t you see I ain’t me, now. I’m an inspector. I’m the law, you
might say, an’ if people saw you runnin’ loose after this, they’d
never respect it no more. It’s little enough they do, as it is.’

‘Oh, I appreciate that, Ez, but it’s hard----’

‘Most things as are right, is. But I’m doin’ all I can to make it
easy, Ruel.--Now let’s talk sensible. You been thinkin’ over things,
eh?’

Ruel laughed jerkily. ‘Yes, but it’s no use. All I know is that I
didn’t kill King.’

‘Of course you never, but all the evidence is against you, an’ it’s
embarrassin’. Take that blood, for instance, how’d it come to be on
your handkerchief?’

‘I don’t know unless----Well, you see I wore these old clothes to
carry out my part in the play. The handkerchief was in them, not a
fresh one. I suppose I cut myself--I’m always doing so--and stopped
the blood with it, then stuffed it into that pocket and forgot it.’

‘When did you do that?’

‘That’s the trouble, Ez. I can’t remember any specific time, and I
heal quickly.’ He spread out his calloused hands; they were scarred
in several places, but no very recent cuts were visible.

‘Not very convincin’ to a jury.--That play now, Ruel, seems kind of
foolish an’ far-fetched in a way. Was----’

‘But not so much as you think, Ez. That was the reason I hated
to take part in it--so much of it was near the truth. It made me
uncomfortable, especially when King played his part so--so literally.
I hadn’t expected him to stick to actual facts, you see.’

‘You mean usin’ your own names an’ you--er--you an’ Sylvia like?’

‘Yes, and more. King had actually lent me money for my farm, and I
still owed it to him. When he brought that into the play, gave me
that receipt and all, I----’

‘You still got it?’

Ruel took a paper from his pocket and handed it to Sewell.

‘Hmm. How much did you owe him, Ruel?’

‘Five thousand--about.’

‘I thought so. This looks like a genuine receipt for that amount in
full!’

‘What? Let me see! I hadn’t looked before.--But of course he didn’t
mean it seriously. Here, let me tear it up.’

‘Hold on. We ain’t destroyin’ nothin’. You see it might prove he was
expectin’ somethin’ to happen--preparin’ for it, like, though it’d be
just like him to do it on purpose, he was that kind an’ generous.’

‘You mean he might have been forewarned? But then you must suspect
some one else. Who is it?’

‘There now, I knew you’d ask that pretty soon, only I was sort of
hopin’ you wouldn’t, in a way. Maybe it will kind of make it simpler
if I tell you right out plain--plough right along into it, ‘stead of
only furrowin’ round the edges. ’Course I s’pose no matter how easy
like I put it, you’ll get all riled up, so here it is. You see, I
suspect Sylvia Hawley----’

Ready as he was for Ruel’s protests, Sewell was not prepared for the
sudden onslaught that followed. Without a word, Ruel charged like an
angry bull at the mention of Sylvia’s name, his muscles bulging, his
hands reaching out as if to tear away that word from the inspector’s
lips. But when he bent forward to seize and shake him, Sewell
suddenly straightened his knees, driving his feet straight forward.
It was not a very dignified way to end an imminent fight, but it was
an effective one. His feet landed neatly against Ruel’s chest. The
force of the blow sent the latter reeling backward until he sprawled
against the wall which saved him from falling. Then Sewell calmly
reached over with one foot, swung shut the door with a slam, and
resumed his former position.

‘Guess it would be better, shuttin’ that door--keep the bull in the
pen, eh?’ he drawled. ‘Too bad you didn’t give your head a crack
against the wall--knock some sense into it maybe. Such a temper would
most make me believe you might have killed King in anger, if I didn’t
know you better.--Suppose you didn’t sleep much, though. Don’t know
as I blame a young fellow like you--might have done the same thing
myself, if I felt that way about a girl.’

He was talking against time to allow Ruel to compose himself. This
the latter did abruptly, crossing over to the bed and sitting down
beside Sewell. He still twitched nervously, and dropped his head into
his hands.

‘Sorry, Ez. I--I won’t interrupt again.’

‘Hope not--at least, not so violent. You’ll feel better, gettin’ it
out of your system too. Now I can tell you the whole thing, includin’
