the fact she ain’t the only one I suspect of knowin’ a lot about
this. Understand me, too, I ain’t said I suspected her of guilt,
just of knowin’ somethin’ as might help. I suspect every one right
now--except you!’

Sewell’s manner changed as he began to recount the discoveries he
had made after Ruel’s arrest. He spoke in short, concise sentences,
clipping his words instead of drawling them, dropping his voice
from its rather high pitch to an emphatic, low staccato. Intently
he watched Ruel’s face and followed his expression of surprise and
incredulity carefully. It was too sincere to be mere acting. Beyond
a doubt, Ruel knew nothing about that mysterious number. This much
accomplished, Sewell relapsed into his former conversational manner.

‘There’s a lot she’s got to explain, certain sure, d’you see? But
that don’t mean she can’t set things to rights in a jiffy, far as
she’s concerned. It don’t stand to reason she’d disappear without
some reason. She’ll probably turn up with some simple explanation
when the time comes.’

‘And probably some one else typed that line, left it on purpose to
cast suspicion on her.’

‘It’s possible--so’s anythin’ for all we know yet,’ assented Sewell
gravely. ‘But I ain’t surmisin’ things. I just told you the facts as
I found ’em, so as to sort of prepare you. If you should remember
anythin’ about that number----’

‘I don’t, Ez. It means absolutely nothing to me.’

‘Well, let’s try somethin’ else. About Sylvia, now. You don’t suppose
she just went off for some reason of her own? A relative, maybe,
or----’

‘She hadn’t any. She was an orphan, Ez. She said she hadn’t a real
friend--outside this village. Mr. King met her at Basil Stacey’s
office and brought her here when he bought that house a year ago.
She’s been here ever since, even when he went on trips to New York or
anywhere.’

‘Hmm. I’d forgotten she worked for Stacey.--Suppose you know all
about her, where she came from an’ so on? All I know is she’s a
mighty nice, interestin’ woman.’

‘Why--no. I never asked her, and she didn’t seem to like to talk much
about her past. You aren’t insinuating----’

‘No, I ain’t. Guess I can tell good folks from bad without no
autobiography, young man. But you let somethin’ like this happen,
an’ sure as I’m alive, of all the cussed complications an’----Why,
I’d have said I knew Sylvia Hawley from A to Izzard before this.
Ask me now, an’ I’m as bad as you, sayin’ she’s mighty sweet an’
all, without knowin’ nothin’ to drive a spike into. Seems like that
reminds me of some others too. You take----’

‘Step right along in. He’s waitin’ for you,’ interrupted Pete’s nasal
voice from the office, followed by Basil’s appearance at the bars.
Sewell handed him the key and Basil unlocked the door and entered,
leaving it open behind him.

‘The last of the guests have gone, even the doctor and judge, to
arrange their affairs. Any news?’

He nodded to Ruel, but they did not bother to shake hands or greet
each other.

It was useless to ask him the same question, for his eagerness
betrayed his own lack of it. Unlike Ruel, though he had been up most
of the night in his fruitless search for Sylvia, his face seemed
as fresh, his manner as jaunty as if he had just awakened from a
refreshing sleep. It was the contrast between a man used to regular
hours and outdoor life, as opposed to one accustomed to the rush and
irregularity of city life.

‘We’ve been mullin’ things over a bit, but ain’t arrived no place in
particular,’ said Sewell. ‘You might tell us about lookin’ for Miss
Hawley.’

‘I searched everywhere, and there’s no trace of her. There was no
train out of here after she was last seen in the play, and I checked
up on all the known automobiles and conveyances in the village or
house. No one saw her after the play and she couldn’t have come down
the stairs from the loft without being seen by Mrs. Dobson who was in
the barn below.’

‘That only leaves two ways, then. Number one is to have sneaked out
that window----’

‘Impossible, Sewell, if you mean the one we used in the show. In the
first place, she’d have been seen against the light; in the second,
I’d have seen her, for I was waiting just outside there for Ruel to
finish and come out.’

‘As far as I can figure out, nobody saw nothin’ important an’ did see
lots as was unimportant. That’s always the way with human critters in
a crisis, seems like.--You was standin’ close to the window?’

‘At the right of it as you face it from outside, wasn’t it, Ruel? I’d
been waiting there in the shadow for some time.’

Ruel nodded absently.

‘Then she didn’t come out there, that’s certain.’

Ruel opened his lips suddenly to speak, but as neither of the
men appeared to be looking at him, he changed his mind, scowled
thoughtfully, and glanced sidewise at Basil, then remained silent.

Sewell noticed this expression of doubt, but made no comment on it
just then. ‘She must have climbed out one of the little windows then,
the one where the dust was streaked on the sill an’ dropped--let’s
see--nigh on to fifteen feet to the ground. Hump! That don’t seem
logical, not to me it don’t. I dunno why she’d try such a thing.’

‘Her disappearance is all illogical,’ echoed Basil. ‘She couldn’t
leave that room--yet she did. Even if some one tried to force her
away, I don’t see how it could have been accomplished.’

‘Nor me, neither.--Hold on.--Now kick me if I ain’t the fool Ellen
B. says I was! An’ me livin’ around farms most all my life! Why, look
here. There’s a door in the end of the barn there an’ the hoistin’
tackle an’ rope for pullin’ up hay to the loft. She might have slid
down that neat as a whistle an’ no one the wiser.’

‘Not Sylvia,’ said Ruel firmly. ‘Sliding down a rope is no easy task
for a woman in evening clothes. Besides, there was no rope there, I
know. King had taken it down.’

‘I s’pose you’re right,’ grunted Sewell, nodding his head to
emphasize his words, ‘but just the same it’s possible.--Say, you
haven’t heard all of it--what I found after you left, Basil.’

The inspector turned to the latter and again rapidly told of the
papers with the numbers on them. Basil was obviously incredulous,
vehemently denying any suspicion of Sylvia’s guilt, but his vehemence
did not take any physical form as Ruel’s had done. Instead he scoffed
at the idea of her knowing anything about the numbers, and dismissed
them as unimportant.

‘This Mrs. Bascom,’ continued Basil, changing the subject abruptly,
‘she is certainly shrewd--the way she worked out the play and took
charge afterward.’

‘Shrewd? Hump! Don’t need to have no one tell me about that critter.
She’d be up to most anythin’.’

‘I wonder!’ murmured Basil, as if to himself.

‘What’s that? You insinuatin’ Ellen B. had somethin’--? Say, young
man, what gave you such a fool notion?’

‘I don’t know. She seemed to be the only level-headed one--she solved
the mystery and was pretty calm about things mostly. She saw what no
one else seemed to take in, and after all she was there in the room.’

‘So was quite a few others--all of ’em as much suspected as her.’

‘I was just theorizing, Sewell, thinking out loud and trying to help
somehow,’ replied Basil apologetically.

‘Sure, I need help, but none about them as I know as well as Ellen B.
I guess I know her, all right--darned well!’

For several minutes thereafter, under Sewell’s prodding, Basil gave
miniature characterizations of each of the guests, whom he knew quite
intimately, save for Dr. New. In this latter case, however, he was
able to repeat King’s own eulogy of his friend and physician. The
result was not illuminating beyond the fact that they seemed a gay,
likable set, devoted to Mr. King and without exception feeling the
loss of his friendship as a decidedly personal one.

This naturally brought the conversation to King himself and rapidly
developed the fact that, although he was accepted everywhere, admired
and looked up to, comparatively few intimate details were known
about his life. A year ago he had bought a large farmhouse about a
mile from the center of Crookshaven and spent a small fortune on
repairing it. He had moved in at once, bringing Miss Hawley with him
and establishing Mr. and Mrs. Dobson in charge of the grounds and
household.

The repairs and modernizing of the house had been skillfully executed
so as to make it thoroughly comfortable and livable, yet not offend
the charm of its age. Externally, wherever repairs were made, a
replica of what had to be removed was put back in its place, then a
coat of stain, rather than paint, applied, so tempered and tinted
that its protective coating enhanced the illusion of boards grown
gray-green with time. Inside, rooms were thrown together in some
instances, restored and redecorated with the same general plan in
mind. Electric lights were added, the plumbing modernized, but
otherwise the effect was of comfortable antiquity.

King had taken an eager, personal interest in all this, spending
lavishly and refurnishing the house with antiques. Two stipulations
were made with regard to the furniture: each piece must be a genuine
antique, but also it must pass a test of comfortableness in the case
of chairs and such appointments. Miss Hawley’s enthusiasm and good
taste had been a material assistance in the work.

At first regarded somewhat in awe because of his wealth and power,
Mr. King had rapidly overcome the village’s reserve and ingratiated
himself with its inhabitants, sharing their hopes, plans, and
sentiments as if he had always lived among them. His generosity
became a byword with them, whereas normally such matters as money
or personal problems were seldom discussed openly; his approval and
advice were sought on many occasions and never proved wanting. In a
year’s time, he called most of them by their first names, could ask
after their children both present or absent, discuss their affairs
and encourage them in periods of stress like a relative.

Yet now that Sewell began to look farther back, he abruptly
discovered little or nothing was known of King himself before his
advent into the village.

Basil was able to trace his history one step farther, yet without
adding materially to it. He had first met King in New York in a
matter of business which had resulted in their friendship. King had
proved himself a shrewd business man, honest, yet capable of matching
wits with the keenest brains and singularly able to get his own way
once his mind was made up on any matter. He had quickly gathered a
circle of friends about him, a somewhat cosmopolitan group, to be
sure, but one that was at bottom sincere in its relations to him
and each other. He enjoyed having them about him, took a special
pleasure in entertaining them without thought of reciprocation or
those formalities of social life that can prove a bane as well as a
blessing.

For two years King had lived alone, enlarging his income by clever
ventures into business where everything seemed to turn to his profit.
Then his hobby--the writing of stories and intricate mysteries--grew
from a pastime to a major work, and he retired to Crookshaven to
gratify it. Yet he could be reticent, and always was, concerning his
past life. He referred to it in generalities, such as to mention some
city or country he had visited or some personal reminiscence that
had apparently been the basis of one of his stories, but he avoided
specific details. Some accused him of having been a detective at
one time because of his knowledge of their ways and methods, but he
smiled without affirming or denying the fact.

‘Seems funny,’ commented Sewell mournfully as Basil completed his
scanty account of King’s city life. ‘It don’t seem right as I should
be the one to play detective for him. He was smart along those lines
himself, an’ it ought to have been the other way around, like.--But
I guess it ain’t the first or last time life’s mixed things up all
wrong. One thing’s certain sure, the person that killed King has got
a lot of enemies in this village an’ a lot of eyes workin’ against
him.’

‘But that hasn’t solved the mystery--or found Sylvia yet,’ said Ruel.

‘No, and I say let’s _do_ something, get busy. This talking will
never get us anywhere.’

‘Don’t be too sure of that, my man. There’s times to act an’ times to
talk, an’ most of us do a whole lot more talkin’ than actin’, so it
stands to reason discussin’ ain’t always a waste of time. Personally,
I guess I’ve learned lots since I came here to-day.’

Sewell got slowly to his feet and yawned, kicking out one leg after
the other to increase the circulation after his cramped position.

‘Basil, you go crank up my auto out there an’ we’ll amble up to the
house for another look around by daylight. There’s a couple of places
I want to see now it ain’t rainin’. I’ll be along soon as I hear the
thing goin’.’

The inspector fumbled with the key, inserting it in the outside of
the lock and delaying until Basil had gone. Then he turned back to
Ruel.

‘Just what was you thinkin’ back there a spell when he said he was
standin’ waitin’ outside the window? I saw you had somethin’ on your
mind.’

For a moment Ruel hesitated, then looked Sewell straight in the eyes
as he answered: ‘I thought it was unimportant--kept still rather
than--well, than appear to be throwing suspicion on a man’s word. I
guess he forgot, but he was not on the right of the window and not
standing still. I know because I took a couple of steps down the
slope on the left when I came out. He met me there and he’d been
running or something, as he was breathing fast.’

‘Hmm. You did right, tellin’ me, son. Now why should he have said he
was standin’ still waitin’?’

‘I don’t know, but I don’t think he meant it as an attempt to cover
up some--some guilty act.’

‘Maybe not, but he kind of made a point of it, seems to me.--Well,
don’t forget, Ruel, I expect to hear everythin’ like that as may
come to your mind, see? Little details turn out big, you know, quite
often.’

At that moment the telephone bell rang and Pete answered it, received
some message evidently, for the receiver clicked down again after a
brief pause without his making any remark. Then Pete shuffled out of
the office with a chuckle and addressed his superior familiarly.

‘Message for you, Ruel,’ he grinned again, ‘an’ one for you, Ez.’

‘Not--not Miss Hawley----’

‘About her, yep. She’s back, Ruel.’

‘Thank God. Where was she? Is she all right?’

‘Yep, so Mrs. B. says.’

‘Ellen B.?’ interrupted Sewell. ‘What did she say? Give it plain an’
literal, Pete.’

‘She says--“Tell Ruel Moore I’ve found Sylvia. She’s shook up a bit,
but she’s safe an’ all right now.”’

‘But where--where was she?’

‘Don’t know, Ruel. All else she says was--“An’ if Inspector Sewell
wants to know where she was, tell him for me it was where any one
with sense, especially an inspector like him, would have looked first
thing.”’

‘Hump. I notice she didn’t look there herself--first thing,’ grunted
Sewell testily. ‘Why didn’t you hold on to the wire, Pete?’

‘’Twasn’t no use when she hung up, Ez. Guess I know Ellen B.!’

A loud report and chugging from the yard announced that the auto was
ready to perform its duty. Sewell slammed the cell gate and, ignoring
Ruel’s pleas to be allowed to accompany him, stamped hurriedly away.

 CHAPTER V

 MRS. BASCOM LAYS A GHOST


As the last automobile load of the guests turned into the road
toward the village, Mrs. Bascom heaved a sigh of relief and leaned
back against the piazza rail. She enjoyed them usually, but her
self-appointed task of hostess had not been an easy one this time.

Gradually the hum of motors faded into the distance, giving way to
the chatter of birds and the gentle rustling of leaves in the cooling
morning breeze. The smell of warm oil and gasoline fumes dissipated
slowly, routed by the clean odors of freshly dampened earth, flowers,
and hayfields filled with clover. The sun, poised in a deep blue sky
washed clear by the night’s rain, was comfortably warm and friendly,
softening the lines on Mrs. Bascom’s face, bringing out the healthy
color of its skin, the benignity yet firmness of its expression, and
adding lustrous highlights in her rather tightly drawn, dark hair.

Scarcely had her slender body relaxed, however, when she straightened
up and admonished herself audibly.

‘Ellen Bascom, you’re a lazy, idlin’ woman. Where’s your gumption?’

As if this self-imposed taunt had goaded her to action, she walked
halfway down the curved drive, then swung about to survey the house
and grounds. She knew this was the best spot from which to view both
house and barn together, for it afforded a clear vista between the
elm trees that shaded the lawn.

Her eyes rapidly swept over the familiar house with its air of
sturdiness and age. It was only two stories high, the peaked gable
adding an unfinished attic, but it gave the impression of being a
spacious house. Originally square, two wings had been added some time
before King bought it, thus almost doubling the floor space. One of
these additions remained exactly as it had formerly been, but in the
other, rooms had been thrown together on the lower floor to make the
large sitting-room and smaller den behind it. A square hall with
narrow, yet not ungraceful, stairs leading straight up to the second
floor, opened on the left into the sitting-room, on the right into a
library, back of which lay the smoking-room, dining-room, and kitchen.

Mentally reviewing this house plan and studying its exterior as if
it might hold some secret, she remembered how children had always
called it the ‘Smiling House,’ pointing to the broad piazza across
its entire front as the mouth, its second-story windows as eyes, and
the chimney as a rakishly cocked hat. Now its smile seemed to become
a leer, and the ells and wings stretched out, not in their former
friendliness, but as if to shrink away from the main body itself.
What had been skillfully counterfeited age seemed too vividly old,
about to fall apart with the passing of its master.--But such fancies
were not for practical minds.

Mrs. Bascom deliberately turned her attention to the barn. It stood
about ten yards to the rear of the house and was built into a low
hill that formed a background for both buildings. Although two full
stories in front, the second floor was on a level with the hill at
the back, thus bringing King’s workroom almost flush with its slope.
Small windows in double rows punctuated the sides of the barn,
terminating in the larger French window at the back.

The huge doors stood open now, giving a glimpse of the small box
stalls, some of which King had converted into places for his own and
his guests’ automobiles. They were still hung with bits of harness,
and the disused implements of the farm of which they had once been
a vital part. Her eyes rested for a moment on the short length of
frayed rope swinging from the wooden crane over the smaller door on
the second floor where hay had once been hoisted for storage, passed
on to the stone wall that cut a line across the hill just back of the
barn, returned to the open doorway just as a man hurried out of it
almost fearfully.

It was ‘Jake’ Dobson, King’s man of all trades and husband to Martha,
the housekeeper. He was bareheaded and clad in overalls, though the
shoulder straps had not been adjusted and trailed behind him as he
hurried toward the house. They caught on a stone and jerked him
backward with a startled cry. As he stooped to free himself, Mrs.
Bascom hastened toward him.

‘’Morning, Jake.--Sakes alive, you look as scared as if you’d seen a
ghost!’ she remarked.

‘I have!’ replied Jake unexpectedly, his red face now crimson as
he straightened up and adjusted the suspenders fumblingly to his
shoulders.

‘Stuff and nonsense.--Where?’

‘Well, I ain’t exactly seen a speerit, ma’am, but it’s a-moanin’
terrible in the barn. I--I was just a-goin’ to tell Marthy about it
before we--before I tried to find it.’

Mrs. Bascom snorted and made a clicking sound with her teeth which
seemed as expressive as a whole volley of sarcasm.

‘You come along with me, Jake Dobson, an’ let me listen to your
ghost,’ she called scathingly as she started toward the barn. He
hesitated, then followed sheepishly.

‘I was just puttin’ on my jeans when it began to moan awful bad. It
wasn’t wind either. I’m used to that. It was more like----’

‘Keep still. How do you expect me to listen to spirits with your
voice jabberin’ in my ears.’

Jake halted, abashed, keeping near the open door while Mrs. Bascom
walked firmly into the friendly muskiness of the barn.

‘Where were you standing, by the peg where you hang your things?’

He nodded silently, his eyes rolling about as if in search of some
dread shape.

At that moment a low moan, faint, but more human than ghostly,
reached Mrs. Bascom’s ears. It seemed to come from one of the stalls,
and she listened breathlessly to locate which one. The moan changed
to a muffled call, a cry of ‘Help--help’ followed by a slow tapping
sound.

Without hesitation, Mrs. Bascom scurried toward one of the box
stalls, disappeared inside, only to reappear and hurry toward the
stairs.

‘It’s Sylvia,’ she called out to Jake. ‘Get Martha--quick.’

‘Sylvia Hawley? Where--What--?’

‘Don’t stand there gapin’ like an idiot. Bring Martha up here.’ Her
final words floated down from above as she disappeared up the narrow
stairs.

Bewildered, Jake obeyed, stammering so badly that his wife thought
something had happened to Mrs. Bascom and came on the run. When she
had reached the loft, she stopped short in amazement and relief. By
the farther wall knelt Mrs. Bascom, bending over a grain chute in the
floor and calling little inarticulate words of sympathy down it. At
Martha’s approach she turned her head for a quick explanation.

‘It’s Sylvia. She’s been down the chute all this time.--Tell Jake to
fetch up a rope.’

This Martha did, and there was no hesitation in Jake’s movements when
she issued instructions!

Then began the somewhat awkward rescue. Martha held one end of the
rope, her husband the other, while Mrs. Bascom lay flat on the floor,
reaching down the chute to help as best she could as Sylvia worked
the loop behind her shoulders and up under her arms. Realizing this
would cut into the flesh, for Sylvia’s gown was a sleeveless evening
dress, Mrs. Bascom halted proceedings long enough to procure two
burlap bags to use for padding.

The rope adjusted, she joined Martha on her end of it and all
three began to pull. At the first loosening movement, Miss Hawley
groaned in pain, yet called up pluckily, ‘It’s my ankle. But I can
stand--anything--to get out of here.’

Slowly they raised her. Her face appeared, pale and streaked with
dust, her teeth clenched to stifle the pain. When her knees were on
a level with the floor, her rescuers moved back a step or two, thus
gently seating her on the edge of the chute. She smiled wanly, then
fell back in a faint.

‘Look, her ankle is sprained!’ cried Martha pityingly as she drew up
Sylvia’s feet from the hole and pointed to her left leg. ‘Give me
your knife, Jake.’

When he had opened and handed it to her, she cut the straps of the
dainty pump over which the swollen ankle bulged painfully, then ran
her fingers delicately over it to see if she could detect any broken
bones.

While she was doing this, Mrs. Bascom gently smoothed back the wavy,
bobbed hair from Sylvia’s face. Though drawn, dirty, and streaked by
tears of pain, it was still a delicately fresh, youthful face upon
which cosmetics and such artifices were unnecessary. Now that her
eyes were closed, it was expressionless, save for a hint of suffering
about the puckered lips and firmly closed jaws. Her dress, once
white, was wrinkled and blotched with dirt from the sides of the
chute as it outlined the slender curves of her body, lying back on
the floor.

A draped cascade of chiffon that hung from her shoulder had spread
out on the floor behind her, and this suddenly riveted Mrs. Bascom’s
attention. Something had cut a neat slit down part of its length, and
the edges along this slash were stained a dark red-brown that looked
like blood! Glancing down the folds of the dress, she discovered
another slit, but here the cloth was unstained.

Quickly Mrs. Bascom placed her foot upon this bit of drapery, then
stooped to raise Sylvia to a sitting position, thus ripping it away
from its fastenings on her shoulder.

‘There now, see what I’ve done!’ she murmured to Martha. ‘But her
dress was ruined anyway, poor dear.--She’s such a slender little
thing I’m sure Jake can carry her to the house.’

The latter did as he was bidden, picking up her limp body easily and
moving toward the stairs with many adjurations and comments upon his
clumsiness from his wife.

Mrs. Bascom lingered behind, ostensibly to pick up the bit of chiffon
and the mutilated slipper, but as soon as she was alone, she peered
down the chute. It was a square slide, tapering downward to a narrow
opening as it neared the little barred grill through which the horse
could munch his hay in the stall below. With the light filtering in
through this opening, she could just catch a glint of something on
the floor of the chute. What it was, she suspected and meant to make
certain.

Hesitating until she heard Jake’s footsteps on the gravel drive, she
hurried down the stairs and into the stall, pressing her face against
the grill. There, upright, its point stuck in the soft wood, was a
slender knife! Covering her hand with a thickness of the chiffon,
Mrs. Bascom reached in and secured it, hastily wrapping it around
with the rest of the cloth, being careful not to touch it with her
fingers. Then, with a shudder, but none the less resolutely, she
thrust it into the bosom of her dress and ran to catch up with the
others just as they were ascending the stairs in the house.

Once in Miss Hawley’s room, Jake was summarily dismissed, and the two
women undressed her and put her to bed.

‘If only Dr. New hadn’t gone to town,’ remarked Mrs. Bascom. ‘But
that’s just like a man--never on hand when he’s wanted. You call up
Dr. Smith in the village, Martha, then make some malted milk for
Sylvia in case she comes to. She’ll need nourishment after being
cooped up so long in that trap.’

‘How do you suppose she came to----’

‘We’ll know--when she tells us,’ answered Mrs. Bascom, opening the
window and procuring a bottle of smelling salts.

Martha Dobson swiftly left the room to do as Mrs. Bascom had
instructed, leaving the latter to revive her patient. As she bathed
Sylvia’s face with cold water, her mind raced with questions.

Had Sylvia been pushed or merely accidentally fallen into the chute?
Who had removed, then replaced the cover to it again? How had that
knife come to be in the chute? How had her dress been stained and
cut? Would she be able to answer all these questions or----

So absorbed had she been mentally, Mrs. Bascom did not notice
Sylvia’s eyes open and rest upon her for a moment inquisitively. But
when Sylvia sighed, Mrs. Bascom abruptly ended her cogitations.

‘There, now, I am thankful to see you come to yourself again. Does
the ankle pain much?’

‘Enough, Mrs. B. But I’m so much more comfortable than squashed in
that smelly hole! It was foolish of me to faint that way when you----’

‘My dear, a woman is never foolish to faint. It’s a God-given
privilege of the sex,’ rebuked Mrs. Bascom with her familiar toss of
the head. ‘And if ever any one had a right to a comfortable swoon, it
was you.’

‘Perhaps--though I’d already indulged in one or two before you found
me!’ smiled Sylvia rather wanly. ‘It was the pain and stuffiness.’

‘Now don’t say another word until after you’ve had a bit of hot milk
to drink,’ commanded Mrs. Bascom sternly as Sylvia seemed about to
add something else. ‘You lie still while I go poke up Martha who is
preparing it. The doctor will be along soon to look after you and
then you can talk all you feel like.’

She bustled out of the room without waiting for an answer, returning
shortly with some malted milk that Sylvia sipped gratefully.

‘I’m really perfectly all right now, Mrs. B. You’ve been a dear
to look after me so nicely.’ Sylvia paused, a hesitant, doubtful
expression in her eyes as they met those of the older woman.

She was troubled, perplexed about something, decided Mrs. Bascom at
once.

‘I want to talk now--to ask some questions and tell you things.’

‘You really shouldn’t, my dear, but if it will ease the pain by
taking your mind off it----No, that’s not honest, not what I’m
thinking at all! I’m really dying with curiosity, of course, and I
wouldn’t be human to make you keep still if you feel like talking.’

Sylvia smiled briefly, and any lingering hesitation vanished. Mrs.
Bascom took both Sylvia’s hands in hers and noted they were cold and
trembling slightly.

‘Did the--the play go off well, Mrs. B.?’

‘It was too gruesomely realistic,’ answered the latter truthfully.

‘You don’t mean--that is----’

‘My dear child, you really are upset. Hadn’t you better wait a while
and rest?’

‘Upset? No--yes. Let me tell you exactly what happened. Then you’ll
see why I’m so--so disturbed.

‘You saw the play, so there’s little I need add to that. There were
several bungled spots in it, one in particular. I saw that Mr. King
had forgotten to place that mock will in the safe, so I carried in a
piece of blank paper which I pretended to take out and tear up. Then
I waited just outside the door until I heard Basil at the safe, after
which I played my invisible part of writing on the typewriter and
moving about as if to enter the room. What I really did was take a
few steps backward, intending to move over toward the window to see
when he went out, thus ending the play.

‘My heel caught on something in the darkness and I twisted my ankle,
then, in an effort to regain my balance, I stepped back and fell
into space. Of course, it was down that chute.--I can’t understand
who could have removed the cover from it or why. Although no one
used that part of the loft now, Mr. King had warned me about the hay
slides and always kept them covered.--Anyway, down I went.

‘The shock of the fall must have stunned me, for the next thing I
knew, I heard you all laughing and moving around the room. I tried to
call out, but the dust got into my throat, and I knew you couldn’t
hear me then, anyway, so kept still, waiting an opportunity to call
when the confusion quieted down.

‘It was just then something suddenly rapped me on the shoulder. I
tried to scream, but my throat seemed to close up tight. I thought
of rats----Oh, it was awful--awful. I could scarcely breathe for a
moment. Then a board creaked, and I realized some one had replaced
the cover to the chute. I hadn’t heard a sound, but the faint light I
could see before was cut off, so I knew what had happened. I should
have screamed then, but--I fainted.

‘When I regained my senses again, my foot pained terribly and I
was so cramped that I could scarcely move. I wriggled, but that
only wedged me tighter--made my position worse. I thought I heard
something drop to the stall below with a little thud.--You--you
didn’t see anything there?’

‘I’ll go look--in a moment,’ muttered Mrs. Bascom evasively, as the
knife in her bosom seemed to press accusingly against her heart.

‘The rest is all confused--a sort of waking nightmare. Once I dimly
heard voices and I’m sure I moaned loudly, but no one seemed to hear
me. Then I dozed or fainted and dreamed--horrible things--that Mr.
King was really dead--that I was in some way respon----’

‘Hush, my dear,’ soothed Mrs. Bascom, scarcely realizing what she
was saying. ‘You were hurt, frightened. No wonder you dreamed such
things.’

‘But they were so vivid, so real. The cover being replaced so
furtively--I couldn’t get rid of the feeling that something was
horribly wrong. Once before, when I was alone in that room just
before the play, it seemed as if some one was there--something
sinister----Oh, it was only fancy, wasn’t it?’ pleaded Sylvia half
hysterically.

‘There now. Don’t bother your tired brain with such----Ah, that’s
the doctor’s car,’ said Mrs. Bascom with heartfelt relief at the
interruption, for she did not know what to say or how to conceal her
own agitation longer.

With an air of brisk authority, she smoothed the bedclothes, caught
up Sylvia’s hastily discarded garments and modestly thrust them
into the closet, then hurried into the hall to usher in the doctor.
Calling Martha to the room to assist him, she stumbled away to be
alone and think. Her feet carried her automatically toward the barn,
her hand clutching at the bosom of her dress.

Straight to the box stall she went, then drew out the knife to
examine it. Unwrapped from the cloth, she held it flat in the palm of
her hand, one fold of the cloth between it and her fingers. It was
a slender knife with a carved hilt, made to represent a sword. Its
tapering silver blade was clotted toward the tip with blood, and
looked exceedingly sharp on both edges. She was sure she had seen it
before, somewhere in King’s house, and obviously it was the weapon
used to murder him.

Wrapping it up in the cloth, she tucked it into her dress again and
stood motionless, tense with her own speculations. She did not doubt
Sylvia’s rather disjointed story, appreciated some of her vague fears
and dreams while penned in the chute. Yet why should they have taken
so definite a form--a premonition of tragedy? Some one had dropped
the knife down there--it was the touch on the shoulder Sylvia had
felt, accounted for the cut, smeared cascade on her dress. But had
this person also removed the cover to the chute purposely to trap
her? No, for then he would not have tossed the knife there, unless
he meant to cast suspicion on her directly. But who--why had any one
wanted to kill Mr. King?

Mrs. Bascom shuddered as the knife weighed against her heart like an
icy finger. For a moment she stood gazing blankly at the wall of the
stall, then shook herself as if to shuffle off by some physical act
her mental doubts and fears.

‘The Lord willin’, we’ll do it, Ellen Bascom,’ she muttered rather
ambiguously, took a few deep breaths to calm her heaving breast,
then hurried back to the house to telephone her message to Inspector
Sewell. Its very words attested to her recovered equilibrium and the
resumption of her former spirit!

It was well she was calm again, for the doctor, when he came down the
stairs, was solemn and perturbed.

‘It’s nothing serious?’

‘Merely a sprain and, of course, the nervous strain. I left a mild
sleeping powder in case it’s needed.--Why didn’t you tell me she did
not know about King?’

‘Do you mean to say you blurted out _that_?’

Before he could answer, she was halfway up the stairs. She hurried to
Sylvia’s room and sat down beside her on the bed, impulsively seizing
both her hands. Sylvia was pale, her eyes fixed in a vacant stare on
the opposite wall. Yet when they turned to meet Mrs. Bascom’s, they
seemed more thoughtful than irrational, and her voice was quite low
and steady.

‘I know now that Mr. King is dead. Tell me--everything.’

In a flash Mrs. Bascom made up her mind to do so. This was no time
for evasions or half truths; only the whole of it would ease the
confused questioning in Sylvia’s mind and enable her to get the rest
she required. Without preamble, therefore, she retold the facts of
the murder as they were known to her, including the switching of
rôles in the play, the dilemma of King’s will, and Ruel’s detention.
At this latter fact, Sylvia’s eyes closed suddenly. She had received
all these details in silence, her eyes never leaving Mrs. Bascom’s
face, her own blank of expression. But the mention of Ruel’s
imprisonment seemed to affect her in some way--just how, Mrs. Bascom
could only guess.

‘Did you look--in the stall?’ Sylvia’s eyes flew open once more. ‘Was
anything there in the chute?’

‘I looked. There was nothing there,’ replied Mrs. Bascom steadily.
‘Now you really must rest, my dear. We must all keep our wits about
us and be ready to do our share--for Mr. King.’

It was exactly the right note to strike, and Sylvia took the opiate
willingly while Mrs. Bascom talked soothingly and smoothed the pillow.

‘Of course the will was just part of the play,’ she concluded. ‘Judge
Brant will tend to all that for you, do whatever is necessary along
legal lines. He is keeping it now, I believe. There’s absolutely
nothing for you to worry about, so----’

She paused. Sylvia’s eyes were closed. A few minutes later the drug
and the physical strain had overcome her resistance and Sylvia was
asleep.

The sound of a motor, grinding up the drive, announced the arrival of
Inspector Sewell and Basil whom Mrs. Bascom met at the door.

‘She is asleep now, so we can talk here, _Inspector_,’ she accented
the last word meaningly.

‘Then she isn’t hurt?’ asked Basil.

‘A sprained ankle--from her fall.’

‘Fall, eh?--You’ve talked to her some, eh? Well, did she by chance
say anythin’ about figures?’

‘Figures? What do you mean, Ezra Sewell?’

Briefly he explained, showing her the penciled sheet.

‘Not a word.’

‘Didn’t calculate she would, but I wanted to know right off.--Now,
Ellen B., let’s get down to facts. Where’d you find her?’

Succinctly Mrs. Bascom told him, but omitting any reference to the
closing of the chute, Sylvia’s premonitions, her stained dress, or
the finding of the knife. Her first intention had been, of course,
to reveal everything, for after all Sewell represented the law,
and this was certainly valuable information. But when he implied a
suspicion of Sylvia by his reference to those figures, Mrs. Bascom
bridled at his stupidity, realized that the story of the knife and
Sylvia’s fears might only add to his case against her. Right or
wrong, Mrs. Bascom decided to protect King’s secretary from such a
contingency--at least for the present or until she could work out an
explanation that could not possibly involve Sylvia in any way.

‘Then the mysterious disappearance was sort of an accident like,
almost comic as you might say.’

‘As _you_ might say,’ echoed Mrs. Bascom scathingly. ‘Personally, I’d
consider such an accident--’

‘There now. You know I ain’t speakin’ literal, ma’am.--But it don’t
seem to explain much, as I’d kind of hoped it might.--Well, Basil,
you get along now an’ make up some of that lost sleep. I’ll have a
look around, an’ if I need you, I’ll sing out.’

He moved away at a shuffle, kicking up the pebbles in the driveway
thoughtfully. His mild eyes had noticed one peculiar fact. Mrs.
Bascom’s left breast had seemed curiously enlarged and her hand had
twice strayed to touch it while she spoke. He noted both these times
were also when her story became less detailed, was spoken rapidly
as if to cover up something. This might have been coincidence,
imagination on his part, yet he shrewdly suspected Mrs. Bascom knew
more than she had revealed.

 CHAPTER VI

 ROBBERY


‘I’m not intruding, am I, Inspector?’ asked Mrs. Bascom as she joined
him in the workroom of the barn.

‘Intrudin’--meanin’ forcin’ your way in?--Well, I ain’t seen no one
stoppin’ you, Ellen B.,’ said Sewell with a grin. ‘If you ask me, I’d
say you’re welcome--always stimulatin’, sort of, to have you helpin’
me.’

He chuckled softly to himself as she stalked over to one of the
chairs and sat down with an audible ‘Hump!’ He had expected a more
violent retort than that, but when she remained silent, he continued
his study of the room, examining the desk with a grotesquely large
magnifying-glass he took from his pocket, then using it on the safe,
the chairs, even along the carpet. At length he returned it to his
pocket and shook his head as their eyes met.

‘It ain’t no use. The rain’s cleaned up any marks outside, an’ there
might as well have been a cyclone in here. Finger-marks blurrin’ all
over everythin’ an’ not a real clue left. Kind of stumps me, all
right. You ain’t got any ideas, I s’pose?’

‘Of course I have. Some one simply walked in that window over there
and killed King in the dark.’ She pointed to the curtained window
near the desk.

‘But it was locked, every one said so, an’ I saw it for myself.’

‘That doesn’t make any difference. Obviously no one could get in
there--so naturally some one did!’

Sewell stared at her incredulously, then a slow flush began to mount
into his cheeks as he realized she was only jeering at him.

‘All right, Ellen B., that squares up for the time bein’. For a
moment I thought you really meant--’

He broke off as an idea struck him. Several of the guests had
examined the windows that had been curtained and found them locked,
according to their statements. They had proved so when he too
examined them, yet he now decided to look a little more closely,
especially at the one near the desk.

Without a word of explanation, he walked over to this latter window
and studied its lock. It was an ordinary bolt affair, securely
fastened and impossible to move from the outside. A drop of water
seemed to have seeped in somehow from the storm, and he absently
touched it with his finger, then wiped it off on the white paint of
the woodwork. It left an oily smear. He bent closer, discovered the
catch had been recently and thoroughly oiled!--Why? To prevent a
squeak--?

The bolt slid noiselessly under his touch and he slowly pushed the
French window open. Something gritted on the sill and he stooped down
to pick up a pebble which had caught under the door frame. Beside it
on the white paint was a scratch and a faint heel mark which had been
partly under the door, hence was not washed away by the rain.

He looked up to find Mrs. Bascom just behind him, intently watching
every move he made.

‘Seems as if you weren’t so far off, after all,’ he said mildly. ‘If
I remember rightly, this window wasn’t used much, was it?’

She shook her head, stepping back a pace to hide her curiosity.

‘Well, them marks look kind of fresh to me!’

She bent closer to look at the blur. Sewell was watching her, and
the movement confirmed his suspicion. Something was concealed in the
bosom of her dress, for it bulged out as if from a weight. It might
be a purely personal article, and yet----

‘Looks like this might be one clue they didn’t mess up.--Ha, it ain’t
a pebble, it’s cement--an’ cement’s on that stone wall there.’

He pointed to the wall which ran so close to the barn that a person
might easily have stepped from it to the window sill. A glitter on
one of the hinges caught his attention then. It proved to be another
drop of oil, and when he tested the doors, they moved noiselessly.

‘Guess you must be quite some detective yourself, Ellen B. I’m kind
of glad you intruded!--’Course, you just guessed it, I suppose;
woman’s intuition, eh?’ His glance at her face was swift but piercing.

‘Yes, I--I was only getting back at you for what you said to
me.--But, Ezra, do you mean he--the murderer--came in this way? Then
it must have been an outsider!’

‘Seems so--him or her. Looks like he was well prepared for the
murder, too. Either he was a pretty good guesser or knew just when to
come an’ go in the dark.’

‘But that would mean he knew all about the play!’

‘You’re a pretty good guesser, ma’am, or else you know somethin’ I
don’t, Ellen B.,’ he shot at her suddenly.

‘What do you mean?’ Involuntarily her hand moved toward her breast,
but she stopped it midway.

Sewell did not answer. He crossed over to the window behind the
chairs in which the guests had sat to watch the play. A brief
examination of this showed it also had been well oiled, but there
were no marks on the sill. When he turned to face Mrs. Bascom again
his expression was calm, though he was inwardly excited.

‘We’re gettin’ somewhere now. I got a hunch it was one of the guests
as did it, came out of here an’ in that other window in the dark,
then locked ’em again durin’ the confusion afterwards, see?’

‘What do you mean about me--knowin’ something?’ reiterated Mrs.
Bascom, refusing to be side-tracked.

‘Oh, nothin’, only you seem to be a pretty good guesser.’

‘You’re accusing me of holding back guilty knowledge, Ezra Sewell!’

‘Nope, certain not. It just struck me queer for a minute--how you
knew about that window.’

‘I didn’t know, I tell you. It was merely a coincidence. If you
appreciated anything about human nature and psychology----’

‘Well, I apologize, ma’am, so don’t get all riled up over it.--Let
me think.--It must have been some one as knew all about the play, eh?’

‘Yes, it might have been one of us,’ acceded Mrs. Bascom eagerly
as an idea occurred to her. ‘It was so dark in the room after King
turned off his desk light that some one might have done as you say.
Any one passing before that other window would have been seen, but if
he came in by the curtained window and kept to this side of the room,
he would have been invisible to us, of course.’

‘Him or her!’

‘Why do you keep adding--“her”?’ snorted Mrs. Bascom. ‘Just because
of that paper with the numbers on it--Why, how do you know Sylvia
typed it at all? How do you know it wasn’t planted there on purpose?
Why do you suspect Sylvia of such an imposs----’

‘I ain’t mentioned no names, have I?’ he interrupted. ‘I merely
remarked it might have been a he or a she, for all we know yet.’

‘But you meant Sylvia, you know you did. And you know perfectly well
she wouldn’t----’

‘’Course I know she wouldn’t, nor Ruel, nor----But some one did.
Might have been you! Far as I’m concerned, me an’ the law suspects
every one around here--’cept myself.’

This silenced her as he had intended it should, and Inspector Sewell
abruptly led the way from the room. When they were outside the barn,
he asked a few questions quite briskly.

‘When can I talk to Sylvia, do you think?’

‘To-morrow, perhaps.’

‘An’ the inquest’ll be then too. You’ll be there for it?’

‘I’ve decided to stay here in the house for a few days. I may be able
to help.’

‘Good idea. Better not say anythin’ about what we just found,
understand?’

‘I won’t. How could I, when commanded not to by--by the law?’

Sewell chuckled appreciatively. ‘Well, I’m kind of glad you’ll be
handy to keep an eye on things. Hope you’ll call me if you need
anythin’ or find anythin’.’

‘Such as?’

‘Oh, the weapon.--Huh, you look as s’prised as if you’d never thought
of that. Seems queer to me it ain’t turned up--’specially if one of
the guests did it. I’m goin’ to have a look for it outside here now.’

Mrs. Bascom watched him walk away with a queer fluttering of her
heart beneath the knife that still rested over it. His sudden
reference to the weapon had startled her. Had he suspected anything?
Had his question about her concealing something contained a double
meaning? She could only wonder as she watched his leisurely progress
toward the rear of the barn.

He stooped to pluck a long grass stem which he stuck in his mouth,
then pushed his hat farther back on his head and stood staring at the
window just beyond him.

As he paused there, feet apart, hands clasped behind his back, an
idle thought crossed Mrs. Bascom’s mind. His was not a prepossessing
figure, average in height, not as rugged as many of his neighbors.
Yet there was a virility, a latent strength about him which he never
seemed to call into being. The same applied to his character which
was friendly, likable, yet rather impressive.

This being true, why should he always arouse an antagonism in her
which was quite obviously reciprocated? She was not ill-tempered
by nature. She could ‘get along’ with almost every one, and the
fact that she was a widow, living alone in moderate comfort, did
not hinder her social standing with the other men and women in the
village. Yet it was certainly true that she and Sewell were always
sarcastic, even caustic, with each other for no apparent reason.
Sometimes she felt he deliberately taunted her, and occasionally she
had tried to overcome this spirit, but always with the same result.
The mood was only short-lived, resulted in a spirited resumption of
hostilities. She had done her best; the result was, of course, his
fault, not her own--just like a man!

With this comforting self-justification, she watched him disappear
around the corner of the barn, then hastened into the house. Sylvia
was still asleep, so she went to the kitchen to inform Martha of her
decision to remain in the house for a few days, after which she set
off at a brisk walk to close up her own home temporarily and bring
back in a battered, straw suit-case a few necessary articles for her
stay.

When she returned, the knife, still wrapped in the chiffon, lay
safely hidden in her own attic. Before putting it away in a trunk,
she had studied it with a magnifying-glass from her parlor table. The
blade showed no marks and the hilt was so carved and etched that no
finger-prints could have been retained on it. Aside from the fact
that it was undoubtedly the weapon the murderer used, it seemed to be
of little value as a clue, she comforted her conscience.

Once established in the bedroom next Sylvia’s in King’s house, Mrs.
Bascom felt comfortably lazy and picked up a book she found there
to read. It proved to be one of King’s novels, written under a
pseudonym, and she began it with interest. The tale was an absorbing
mystery of crooks and detectives, murders and counter-plots which
quite thrilled her. Two hours passed as a few minutes, and only when
the front door bell rang did she look up with a start to find it was
almost dinner time. After changing her dress, she descended to greet
the doctor and judge who had just returned.

Eagerly they listened to her story of Sylvia’s rescue, pressing her
for details which she gave much as she had done earlier to Sewell and
with the same reservations.

‘I suppose this inspector person has made a more thorough search
to-day. Did he find anything tangible--the weapon, for example?’
asked Judge Brant when she concluded.

Mrs. Bascom glanced at him quickly, but his face revealed nothing but
interest and curiosity.

‘No--unless he found it after I left him.’

‘And Miss Hawley. How is she now?’ asked Dr. New, professionally
interested in that angle of the case.

‘Resting comfortably and as well as could be expected!’ gibed Mrs.
Bascom.

The doctor grinned. ‘You’d make a good nurse, Mrs. Bascom.--But I
gather she’s been looked after--her ankle?’

‘By our local doctor, yes. He said nothing was wrong with her except
the sprain--that rest was all she needed.--But I wish you’d give your
opinion too. I’d feel so much more comfortable, with you, right here
in the house, looking after her.’

‘Of course, while I’m here I shall, if you wish.’

‘Does that mean you have to go soon, Doctor?’

‘I’m afraid so--after the inquest.’

‘But Dr. New, I think you ought to stay on a few days--just to make
sure she’s all right.’

‘So, you don’t trust your local physician?’ smiled the latter
banteringly.

‘Yes, but--well, the nervous shock must have been severe. And I know
Mr. King would insist upon the best of care for her, if he were still
alive.’

The doctor hesitated. ‘Put that way, Mrs. Bascom, I can hardly
refuse. I’ll stay--a day or so, at least. You don’t think I ought to
see her to-night?’

‘She’s asleep, so I guess she’d better just rest. There’ll be so
much for her to do when she can--go over King’s papers and answer
questions and all. Incidentally, I’m going to stay here too for a
while.’

‘Quite right, Mrs. Bascom. Just call on me if necessary.’

The two men left to prepare for the evening meal and Mrs. Bascom,
observing the inspector outside the window, hurried out to accost him
as he shuffled toward his car.

‘Find anything new, Ezra?’

‘Not a durned thing, Ellen B.,’ he replied cheerily. ‘Guess I need
your guidin’ hand when it comes to findin’ things.’

He bent over the crank of his car and puffed audibly as he spun its
handle several times before the engine snorted noisily. His face was
red with the exertion as he climbed into the driver’s seat without
bothering to open the door. Tossing off his hat, he bent closer to
her with a twinkle in his eye and shouted above the pounding of the
engine--‘Mighty becomin’ dress, that. Seems to fit you better than
the one you had on this mornin’, Ellen B.!’

With a flourishing rap on his chest, he threw in the clutch and
started with a bounding jerk, leaving Mrs. Bascom gasping.

So he had noticed that bulge in her dress! But had he guessed what
it was? Did that explain his questions, his first reference to the
missing weapon? Or was it merely a coincidence, a product of her own
imagination?

A sort of panic seized her. Had she done the right thing to conceal
that knife? If he suspected her knowledge, might not he get her into
trouble?--Her ideas were vague. She saw a vision of herself in jail,
held for--what was the term?--contempt or accessory to something.
She wavered, might have confessed it all if Ezra had only remained.
Then Sylvia’s anxious, wan face seemed to float before her eyes,
strengthening her resolve. Sylvia would have enough to face without
that. Come what might, Mrs. Bascom would guard her secret, do a
little sleuthing of her own before she gave up that knife and its
story. And she must warn Sylvia to keep silent about certain phases
of her confinement in the chute--for the time being, at least.

The evening meal was given over to a discussion of the crime with
the doctor, the judge, and Basil, but it was all fruitless and
brought out nothing new. After it was concluded, the men retired to
the piazza to smoke and Mrs. Bascom withdrew to her room where she
eagerly took up King’s story once more, reading it with avidity.

About ten o’clock she went to Sylvia’s room, placed some more malted
milk at the side of her bed, then, as she was a light sleeper, left,
knowing she would hear if Sylvia should call or need her.

King’s book enthralled her. She heard the men retire to their rooms,
but still read on, unable to put down the story again until she had
concluded it. So it was nearly midnight before she closed it with a
little sigh of relief, undressed, and went to bed. But she could not
sleep as she had expected to do. For what seemed hours, she lay awake
going over and over the details of King’s murder. Even her caustic
admonitions to herself proved unavailing; her brain refused to cease
its activities.

At last she fell into a doze, only to be awakened by some sound which
ceased as soon as it had startled her to an upright position in bed.
She sat there, tense, for a moment, but no repetition of the sound
came to her ears. She must have been mistaken, she decided. It was
nothing but her own nerves.

Disgusted at herself for being so foolish, Mrs. Bascom lay back
again, deliberately closing her eyes and counting sheep jumping
over the wall. She had just reached the fiftieth hurdler, when an
unmistakable crash brought her to her feet by the side of the bed.
Somewhere in the house a chair or some heavy piece of furniture had
been tipped over and fallen upon an uncarpeted floor. The sound
seemed to have come from one of the rooms on the lower floor.

Mrs. Bascom groped for her dressing-gown, only to realize she had
neglected to bring it with her. She crept to the door, opened it a
crack, but modesty prevented her from stepping into the hall.

Modesty is an illogical emotion. Dress Mrs. Bascom in a flannel
wrapper and she felt quite competent to face any masculine eye--under
such provocation as the present situation--but clothe her in an even
more voluminous flannel nightgown, and even her curiosity shrank
back in righteous terror. Some one might see her, one of the men,
if the sound had awakened him too. If only she had brought that
dressing-gown----

A door opened, then another, and she heard whispered voices, the
muffled shuffling of slippered feet. Peering out through a crack in
the door, she could just discern two figures hastily descending the
stairs. Undoubtedly they were the doctor and the judge whose rooms
were at the farther end of the corridor. Opening her door a little
wider, she listened eagerly until footsteps close by made her close
it again quickly. That must be Basil coming from his room farther
down the wing.

A blur outside her window announced the arrival of the men in the
study below and the turning on of its lights. Mrs. Bascom remembered
the window would probably be open--it was a needless precaution to
even close it this far out in the country--so leaned out of her own
to try to catch any of the sounds or voices from below. A word or
broken phrase became intelligible as the men moved about in the room,
enough for her to gather that some one had entered with the obvious
intent of robbery.

She had listened there for several minutes before the thought of
Sylvia came to her mind. Perhaps the noise had awakened her and she
might be frightened. Mrs. Bascom hurried to the door between the two
rooms and softly pushed it open, glancing quickly at the bed. It was
empty!

Scarcely believing her eyes, she looked again, opening the door wider
and almost gasping aloud with surprise. The covers were thrown back,
and Sylvia was nowhere to be seen. Her eyes swept the vacant room
questioningly, paused on the hall door that stood ajar. She took a
step into the room, then shrank back again. That door was slowly
moving open!

Mrs. Bascom cringed back into the shadows, but held her door open a
crack so that she might watch.

A figure stumbled into the room and leaned against the wall. It
was Sylvia in a dark dressing-gown, supporting herself on a cane!
Painfully she drew herself toward the bed, favoring her sprained
ankle, but stepping on it occasionally and breathing rapidly. Once
there, she thrust the cane under it, then threw herself down, too
exhausted to remove her wrapper or get beneath the covers.

Mrs. Bascom rattled the knob of the door and entered.

‘My dear, what’s the matter?’ she called softly.

‘That noise--I tried to get to the door--I’ll be all right now.’

‘You should not have gotten up,’ reproved Mrs. Bascom sternly. ‘Why
did you do it?’

‘I--I had to----But the doctor said I could--advised me to step on my
foot all I could, you know. It’s bandaged--’

‘There now. Let me help you and tuck you in. You’re still weak.
Everything is all right. Just rest and sleep.’

Sylvia sighed deeply and relaxed, seemed to fall asleep almost
instantly, so Mrs. Bascom left her, hearing the men approaching
up the stairs. Modesty or no modesty, she must find out what had
happened! Wrapping the puff about her shoulders, she thrust her head
into the hall and called out, ‘What is it? Was it a robber?’

‘Yes--at least, an intruder,’ answered one of the men. ‘He broke into
King’s desk, but of course we don’t know whether he took anything or
not. The window was open--he must have come in there. We’ve locked up
again now, so don’t worry. We’ll search the house, then call Sewell,
so you’ll be perfectly safe.’

It sounded like the judge’s voice, but as he spoke he was moving
toward his room so that Mrs. Bascom had no chance for a reply or to
ask any of the questions that arose in her mind. For a moment she
stood still listening. Thus it happened she overheard still another
brief conversation.

Scarcely had the two men closed their doors, when one of them opened
again and Mrs. Bascom, by leaning immodestly into the hall, saw Judge
Brant step from his lighted room and knock on the doctor’s door. When
the latter opened it, the Judge spoke in what was supposed to be a
whisper, but was so loud and excited that Mrs. Bascom clearly heard
his words.

‘Doctor, some one has been in my room while we were downstairs. Come
in and I’ll show you. My wallet----’

The rest was lost as the two men disappeared from Mrs. Bascom’s view,
but she had heard enough. She knew that ‘some one’ was Sylvia!

But why? What was she doing there? If she was after some special
thing----The will! That was the only article the judge possessed that
seemed to bear on the case. She was after the will--why?

Shivering slightly with the excitement and the chill of the night,
Mrs. Bascom got back into bed to think. She intended to straighten
out these questions in her mind before sleeping again, sure that rest
could not come until her perplexities were settled.

Yet the human brain is a contrary organism; given a problem which
must be solved, it often plays the truant, closes up shop and refuses
to function, while if it has no duty to perform, it creates one
out of thin air. Thus, now that Mrs. Bascom had a real reason for
remaining awake, she almost instantly dropped off to sleep!

 CHAPTER VII

 A LIE


‘Much better, Mrs. B.,’ Sylvia Hawley answered her inquiry. ‘I’ve
slept for hours. I don’t think I’ll attempt getting up just yet,
however. If Inspector Sewell wants to ask questions as you say, bring
him up now and I’ll answer them.’

It was mid-morning. Sylvia had just finished the breakfast Mrs.
Bascom had solicitously brought her and now sat propped up in
bed. Beyond its pallor, her face showed scarcely any traces of
the ordeal through which she had so recently passed. A becomingly
ruffled negligee had been thrown over her shoulders and she seemed
even younger than her twenty-six years, the slight disarray of her
bobbed hair adding to the effect of careless youth and softening an
expression which, to the observant Mrs. Bascom, was a trifle too
tense and nervous.

Tactfully, Mrs. Bascom set about to relieve this strain as well as to
caution Sylvia about certain matters, such as any reference to the
presentiments she had felt while in the chute.

‘What it is to still have youth!’ she began cheerfully. ‘One would
scarcely suspect you’d even been up to a late party, much less a
harrowing adventure.’

‘I do look--normal?’ responded Sylvia, quite too seriously.

‘And bewitching, my dear, to say the least. You certainly do not look
as if you needed any help, especially mine, but I’m going to offer
it, nevertheless. Not only do I want to help you, but also Mr. King.
He was my friend and I----Oh, I suppose it’s all quite foolish of me,
but it seems as if I had a personal responsibility to discharge.

‘You know I was the one to solve the pseudo-mystery of the play as
well as find he was really dead. In the cryptogram jingle he left,
telling it was Basil who was supposed to have killed him, there was a
line--“I’m dead, but you can avenge me.” I can’t get that out of my
head, somehow.’

‘Why, Mrs. B., I didn’t know you felt--that way.’ Sylvia hesitated,
seemed about to continue with some important remark from the
seriousness of her expression, but instead she patted the older
woman’s arm affectionately.

‘I know. You think it’s foolish of me and sentimental--to take those
words literally--but I do.’

‘I’m sure you’ll help us--me, Mrs. B. It’s not going to be easy, but
I----’

‘That’s what I wanted to speak to you about, Sylvia dear. There are
some things you don’t already know and I want to advise--to help you
about them. For instance, when you used the typewriter during the
play, what did you write?’

‘I don’t remember--some jumble or other that occurred to me there in
the dark. It was quite mechanical and--Yes, I do recall part of it.
I started to write off that practice line all stenographers learn,
about being the time for all good men, and so on. But I wrote only a
few words, as I remember, then tapped whatever keys happened to be
under my fingers to finish out the line.--But why do you ask that?’

Briefly, Mrs. Bascom told of the finding of the similar numbers on
that sheet to those on the paper in King’s dressing-gown pocket.

‘But I never heard of them before!’ exclaimed Sylvia. ‘I didn’t even
know I was making figures at all.’

‘You’ll have to explain that coincidence to Inspector Sewell,
however. Frankly, he suspects you of--well, complicity!’

Sylvia closed her eyes suddenly, thus robbing her face of an
expression which Mrs. Bascom was trying to catalogue. It seemed
almost as if a look of relief had showed briefly in her eyes, but
that was impossible under the circumstances. It should have been fear
or anger at being suspected. Yet Sylvia’s mouth, instead of drooping,
was set into a misleading willfulness and satisfaction.

‘Of course, he is only a muddle-headed fool to believe such a thing
about you--or Ruel, for that matter, but----’

‘I’ve changed my mind. I shall get up and see Sewell and the others,
since you say they are here, at once.’

Sylvia’s eyes flew open again, but they were expressionless and only
added strength to the determined set of her mouth, almost a bitter
hardness. Her voice cut into Mrs. Bascom’s soothing murmur with an
abrupt rudeness.

‘But, my dear, you don’t seem to understand. He suspects you, and I
want to warn you about certain----’

‘Thank you, I don’t need your warning.’

Startled at this brusque dismissal of her aid, Mrs. Bascom again
protested, but Sylvia did not listen, throwing back the bedcovers and
awkwardly swinging her feet to the floor. Seeing this determination,
Mrs. Bascom closed her lips sternly and did not offer to assist
Sylvia as she dressed. The latter seemed in a nervous haste to put on
her clothes, so much so that she fumbled with them and bit her lip
in annoyance as her fingers seemed to become all thumbs. When she
was ready, she stooped to secure the cane which she had thrust under
the bed after the disturbance of the night, and, as she straightened
again, their eyes met and held for an instant. Mrs. Bascom’s showed
her hurt and resentment, yet Sylvia’s did not change or appear to
take notice of this obvious appeal.

In spite of her pique, Mrs. Bascom could not endure the sight of
Sylvia’s halting progress toward the door, so drew the latter’s free
arm over her shoulder and thus bore much of her weight as they moved
toward the stairs. Here she called to Basil and Dr. New for aid. The
two men hurried up the stairs and, learning her wish, carried Sylvia
between them to the smoking-room where the judge and Sewell were
talking together.

It was an oddly comfortable room, which had formerly served as the
kitchen in the old-fashioned house. A huge brick fireplace with a
built-in oven filled its longer wall. This had been kept intact even
to the utensils which still hung about it, including a more modern
corn popper and toast rack. A variety of low chairs mutely beckoned
with lazy allure to any man who desired to smoke, spill his ashes
carelessly into the fireplace, or even prop up his feet on the low
brick oven for sheer comfort.

When Sylvia had been established in a winged chair and her injured
foot placed on another before it, Dr. New bent for a brief,
professional examination of the ankle. Running his fingers over the
surgeon’s plaster that encased it, he nodded to show his approval,
then resumed his seat.

‘Well, Miss Hawley,’ said Sewell with a cordial smile of greeting,
‘I’m right glad to see you again. I was kind of beginnin’ to worry
about you, till Mrs. B. here decided to show up the whole durned
constabulary by listenin’ to a ghost an’ takin’ him--or rather
her--serious.’

‘At least I can use the common sense the good Lord gave me,’ retorted
Mrs. Bascom tartly.

‘Yep--only you took your time about it,’ murmured Sewell slyly.

Mrs. Bascom understood this reference to her telephone message and
began to bridle with another retort, but she did not get a chance to
use it.

Sylvia had not smiled at this brief exchange of amenities, though
normally she had always been the first to appreciate and even abet
their word battles. Her attitude confirmed her serious expression and
Ezra Sewell, responsive to a mood, though apparently taking no notice
of it, met it squarely after having thus tested it.

‘Now, Miss Hawley, let’s get right down to the road-bed, as you might
say. There’s some interestin’ things you can tell us an’ if you’re
feelin’ up to it--’

‘Of course I am.--Shall I tell you what I know about--about the
play and all, or do you prefer to question me first?’ She spoke in
a level, quick tone as if it were some sort of a formal business
interview with strangers.

‘You go ahead, an’ I’ll pick up the leavin’s afterwards.’

‘There isn’t much I know. Of course you’ve been told all about the
entertainment Mr. King planned. My part in it was quite obvious up to
the time I was supposed to frighten away the intruder by writing on
the typewriter. I wrote what was on that paper you found, according
to Mrs. Bascom. Then I stepped back, wrenched my ankle, and fell
into that hole--the feed chute. I heard the confusion, but could not
make myself heard. Then some one replaced the cover to the chute and
I fainted. I was in a sort of stupor from then on until Mrs. Bascom
found me.’

‘Land o’ Goshun! An’ they say a woman can’t be brief!’ chuckled the
inspector when she stopped speaking. ‘Why, you ain’t told us nothin’
yet.’

Sylvia sat silent, waiting for him to continue and totally
unresponsive to his efforts to draw her into a more freely
conversational mood. Desisting in this attempt, Sewell began to ask
questions in rapid succession.

‘Before the play, now. Mr. King seemed happy, not scared or anythin’?’

‘Apprehensive? Not at all. He’d worked up the scheme of the play
carefully and was anxious for it to get over successfully.’

‘Anybody know about it, except you ones that was in it?’

‘Not to my knowledge. He swore us to secrecy to insure its success,
so I doubt if he told any one else.’

‘The blotter on his desk, was it a new one?’

‘Yes, I changed it just before the play. But why do you----’

‘Had the room been cleaned recently?’

‘That afternoon. I helped Mrs. Dobson air it of tobacco smoke, dust
and generally clean it.’

‘So she said.--An’ all the windows were opened?’

‘For a time. I was the one who closed them before Mr. King hung the
draperies.’

‘Did you lock ’em?’

‘I did.’

‘Did you happen to notice the sills?’

‘Not particularly, though I swept them just before closing the
windows.’

‘In the show, now. Could you see anythin’ when you came in to open
the safe?’

‘No, but I did not look specially. I knew Basil, or Ruel as it turned
out to be, was there in the darkness, of course.’

‘An’ when you did that writin’, why did you choose them words an’
figures?’

‘I wrote without thinking. It was part of a sentence we typists used
to practice on for speed. “Now is the time for every good man to come
to the aid of the party.” I started that, then tapped a few keys
haphazardly, just as my fingers happened to be above them. I don’t
even remember how much of that sentence I wrote. I mean, I didn’t
until I was told about that paper.’

‘Perhaps you’d seen those figures before somewhere an’ they came to
your memory automatic, eh?’

‘Possibly, but I don’t recall ever having seen or heard them.’ She
looked away in an obvious attempt to avoid his eyes, and her fingers
plucked at her dress nervously.

‘Certain sure, Miss Hawley? It’s kind of important, you know.’

‘I know. Mrs. Bascom explained that to me.’

He waited for a more direct reiteration of her denial, but when
none came, he did not press the question, glancing sidewise at Mrs.
Bascom’s face. She was intently watching Sylvia, her mouth gaping
slightly in astonishment, a look of incredulity in her eyes.

‘Now, then. When this cover to the chute was put on again, what’d you
hear?’

‘Nothing. I was waiting for the confusion to stop so I could call
out, when suddenly it was muffled and I saw the cover had been
replaced, that’s all.’

‘No footsteps, like?’

‘Possibly, but very faintly.’

‘An’ that was all you knew then?’

Sylvia nodded in the affirmative, her eyes darting a swift look at
Mrs. Bascom. The latter exhaled her breath with a whistling sound
as if she could hold it no longer, then, meeting Sewell’s gaze, she
flushed and spoke with an asperity that was not altogether genuine.

‘What would you expect an injured person, shut up in that chute, to
hear, I’d like to know?’

‘Dunno. I was just askin’ that question to find out,’ retorted the
inspector dryly. ‘Now, about yourself, Miss Hawley. If you’d tell
us----’

‘My life history?’ she interrupted listlessly. ‘I’m an orphan,
working for my living and have since I was twelve. I can give you the
usual references, of course, but there’s nothing much to say.’

‘I’d like to have ’em later, just for form, sort of.--Where’d you
meet Mr. King?’

‘In Mr. Stacey’s office where I was working at the time.’

‘I see. An’ you left him to become King’s secretary?’

‘I can answer that,’ broke in Basil, speaking for the first time. ‘It
was the only thing I have against King. He stole my one competent
secretary.’ Basil smiled at Sylvia, but she did not respond to his
flattery. ‘She’d only been with me a few weeks--just long enough
to be indispensable--when I did some work for King. He spotted her
ability even when I tried to hide her, and bribed her away.’

‘It was a generous offer I could not afford to let pass,’ added
Sylvia stiffly.

‘So you didn’t know King before Basil did, eh? But you must have
heard somethin’ about his life, King’s, I mean, before he came to New
York?’

‘He never mentioned it directly. Our arrangement was strictly a
business one, of course, though he treated me like a daughter. I
took his dictation and helped him with social affairs, but never
inquired into his life. He was reticent about that--quite naturally,
I thought. His friends can tell you----’

‘No, they can’t, seems like. No one appears to know much about
him--before he came to New York--unless you gentlemen do, eh?’

‘Not I,’ responded Dr. New, ‘though I’d never thought of it before. I
attended him as his physician, though I gave rather nominal advice,
medically speaking. He was sound as an oak. I liked him from the
start and we simply dropped in upon one another quite naturally. I
admired him and accepted him at face value, but as for his past----’
He spread out his slender fingers in a deprecating gesture.

‘It’s extraordinary,’ added the judge. ‘I met him when he came to me
as a client, but even as his lawyer, he told me nothing. It was never
necessary or pertinent to ask, you see. As a judge of character, I
considered him a----’

‘Oh, I ain’t castin’ no slurs on him. Guess we all know he was an
exceptional man. But it’s queer, though it’s human. We accept some at
face value, an’ require nothin’, an’ others we just naturally never
accept, for no reason at all except likes an’ dislikes.--Anybody know
where he lived before New York?’

They all shook their heads.

‘Well, now, comin’ to this burglary business of last night. He was
kind of well off, I guess?’

‘More than “kind of,”’ said Basil to whom the question seemed
addressed. ‘I handled some of his business--bonds, you know--but even
that was enough to insure a moderate income. Besides, he did quite a
bit of writing and publishing, I believe.’

‘Keep any cash here as you know about--or valuable papers an’ things,
Miss Hawley?’

‘I should say so, although I don’t know accurately. He attended to
all money matters himself. He was generous and liberal, and always
seemed to have money on hand when it was needed.’

‘Guess I know he was generous an’ so does most of the village.--Well,
probably ’twas thievin’, then. Of course when you, as knows most
about his things, get a chance to check up, we can tell for sure.
Where did he keep his valuables?’

‘In a safe behind a panel in the library.’

‘Not in his desk, hey? Then seems like the thief didn’t get much
after all.’

‘There were some important papers in the desk,’ volunteered Sylvia,
looking away pointedly. ‘I’m not sure what they were, but he spoke of
attending to them after the house-party.’

‘Well, let’s quit for a while an’ let you look ’em over,’ decided
Inspector Sewell. ‘Guess you’ve told us about everythin’, Miss
Hawley, an’ helped a bit, leastwise about some things.’

‘May I ask a question or two?’ interrupted the judge.

Sylvia nodded, fixing her eyes on his face and bracing herself as if
for something she had dreaded and expected. Inspector Sewell noticed
that her hands clenched until the knuckles showed white as the dress
that almost concealed them.

‘It’s about that will. You’ve been told about the one King used in
the play?’

Sylvia moistened her lips and nodded slowly.

‘There’s one thing that has occurred to me in that connection. In the
sequence of the play, you tore up something you took from the safe.
Was it supposed to have been the will?’

‘Yes. He’d planned----He forgot to put it there as he’d planned. I
saw this--from the door--so carried in a blank sheet--tore it up.’
Her words jerked as if forced out by an effort of will power.

‘Then but for a chance mistake, it would have been destroyed!’
muttered Judge Brant. ‘Did you know it was a real, legally executed
will?’

‘No--that is, not until Mrs. Bascom told me.’

‘Well, I’ve interviewed the witnesses to it, and it was quite in
order. Yet I can’t understand it. I know the contents of his previous
will, made less than a month ago, and he said nothing about altering
it so radically. This one is his last, hence stands, unless----’

‘I want it to be probated!’ interrupted Sylvia in a small, dry voice.

‘No, no, my child, that’s the term you mean for putting it into
force. Now I’m sure you----’

‘I do understand, and that’s what I shall insist upon!’

‘What?’

They all stared incredulously at her, the judge leaning forward and
cupping a hand behind his ear as if he had not heard her words.

‘The will in my favor--I want you to probate it.’ Her voice steadied,
her eyes met his inflexibly.

‘But I can’t do that! I’m sure it was somehow just a mistake--a part
of his plan for the play. I thought of course you’d understand and
help us show that----’

‘He told me he would provide for me in his will,’ interrupted Sylvia.

‘And he did, handsomely, but naturally he did not mean to give you
everything.’

‘He has left me everything. You yourself said it was legally drawn
up.’

‘Sylvia, you don’t know what you’re saying!’ cried Mrs. Bascom,
suddenly recovering from her astonishment. ‘You know Mr. King had
other plans. He’d told us all about them--many times. You know there
must be some mistake about this will.’

‘Let’s hear what she has to say,’ suggested Basil. ‘She must have
some foundation for all this. I think I’ve a right to say this, as
I’m an interested party, you know. He read the clause in his previous
will that included me.’

‘One thing at a time,’ said Sewell slowly. ‘Just explain a bit, Miss
Hawley.’

‘There’s nothing to explain. I knew about that will. He told me he
was going to make it--in my favor.’

‘When?’

‘A--a few days ago. I--I don’t remember exactly,’ stammered Sylvia,
her assurance suddenly collapsing, her pale face working, her eyes
restless and avoiding theirs. It was scarcely a tone of conviction or
the manner of one who told the truth.

‘What did he say, exactly? Were there any other witnesses to the
interview?’ snapped the judge.

‘I don’t remember--No,’ she answered both questions at once.

‘But, Miss Hawley, think. If you insist upon this, it will
seem--people will think----’

‘I don’t care what people think,’ she cried wildly. ‘The money is
mine--mine, and I intend to have it.’

She broke down completely, sobbing and wringing her hands. It was
then Dr. New interrupted quietly but firmly.

‘Wait. She’s not strong enough for this sort of thing yet, Sewell. We
must get her to bed again at once, or she’ll become hysterical and
have a bad relapse.’

At a gesture from him, Basil assisted, and together they carried
her to her room, Mrs. Bascom following in a daze, shaking her head
from side to side and moving automatically, though she carried out
the doctor’s instructions quite capably. When Sylvia had been put to
bed, he procured a sedative and watched over her until she had become
calm and fallen into a doze. Then he arose to leave, and Mrs. Bascom
followed him into the hall, stopping him with a touch on his arm.

‘You don’t suppose--the shock and all, I mean--could that have
affected her--made her temporarily forget things?’
