‘It’s quite possible. Of course the shock and strain were severe,
yet she did seem rational enough when she first started answering
questions,’ he replied, not suspecting all that prompted this
question.

It was not only Sylvia’s determination about the will that troubled
Mrs. Bascom. She had heard her story the night before, had just
listened to her answers which omitted several details and avoided
other pitfalls with evasions that amounted almost to lies. True,
she herself had not been entirely straightforward with Ezra Sewell,
but that was different. At least she had not told direct lies,
while Sylvia----Yet what was right? Had Sylvia been so confused and
bewildered that she had not realized what she was saying? No, not
entirely, at least. The presence of the knife, the cut and stained
dress proved the account of the tap on the shoulder. And if that much
was true, it seemed evident the rest was also clear.

Mechanically she heard what the doctor was saying to the other men as
she followed him into the smoking-room once more.

‘Miss Hawley has been walking on that foot, Sewell. I think in view
of that fact you should tell about what occurred in your room,
Judge,’ he said sternly.

‘I’ve already done so. But are you sure she’s walked on it?’

‘I can tell by the bandaging--yes.’

‘You went into his room, Doctor? You saw that wallet was lyin’ on
the floor an’ its papers scattered about as if some one had been
searchin’ ’em?’

‘Yes, Inspector.’

‘But she stepped on her foot before I called you up to help her down
the stairs!’ interrupted Mrs. Bascom, coming to Sylvia’s aid without
stopping to think of her words. ‘I don’t see what you are trying to
insinuate--why you----’

‘There now, Ellen B. Let’s not argue about such things now. Wait till
we kind of digest like what everythin’ means. What looks simple,
ain’t, and t’other way round. There’s Ruel in jail with enough
evidence to make him out guilty, an’ there’s her with a lot of queer
goin’s on. Seems like Dr. New, the Judge, an’ me are the only ones
that can tell straight stories, so far.’

‘You left me out of that,’ grinned Basil.

‘So I did!’ answered the inspector quizzically. But he did not amend
his statement.

His eyes, half mocking, half serious, met Mrs. Bascom’s and held them
for a moment. No one noticed, however, that she did not try to add
her name to the list of omissions!

 CHAPTER VIII

 THREAT AND PROMISE


The inquest was over, and King’s body quietly laid to rest. A verdict
of death by person or persons unknown had been duly returned, then a
postponement had been ordered to sift the evidence and investigate
the clues, at some later date to be reconvened for further testimony.
It had been a purely formal ceremony to satisfy the letter of the
law, for Sewell had taken care that nothing of real importance had
been presented. He was far from ready for that contingency as yet.

To Mrs. Bascom the proceedings had been a decided disappointment, for
she had attended them fully confident of learning what Sewell’s real
suspicions might be, sure that she would hear any facts or details he
had gathered which had not been discussed in her presence. Instead,
only the bare outline of the case had been given out, a brief
recapitulation of what she already knew.

She was not at all sure now that Ezra Sewell had any definite theory
as to the murder. His face was as placid as ever, his manner as
leisurely and unperturbed as if nothing of importance was being
discussed. Yet that did not deceive Mrs. Bascom. She knew that
underneath his assumed air of nonchalance, his eyes were watching
every one, his brain receiving and cataloguing their expressions
and reactions or noting details that might have any bearing on
the case. It was these characteristics which had made him come out
victorious in so many deals or trades with shrewd farmers, had gained
him a hearty respect and reputation for wisdom among his fellow
townspeople. But these same traits led many strangers to pass him by
as a quite ordinary, none too keen farmer!

All this Mrs. Bascom sensed without ever having put it actually into
words or even concrete thoughts, and it made her realize still more
forcefully that her own dilemma was precarious. That Sewell suspected
her of concealing some knowledge was certain, but whether this was
merely a vague impression or a definite suspicion, she could not
decide.

In spite of these thoughts, however, she had definitely decided upon
a course of action for herself. Sylvia must be protected until such
time as she regained her normal senses. There was no explanation
possible, it seemed to Mrs. Bascom, for Sylvia’s actions and words
other than an imminent nervous breakdown or mental lapse. Whether
this be temporary or not remained to be seen, but until it was past,
Mrs. Bascom would keep her knowledge of certain things to herself.

This would mean concealing the knife and the stained bit of Sylvia’s
dress, would require her to choose her words with care in discussing
certain phases of Sylvia’s temporary disappearance, but she did
not shrink from the task, once her mind was fixed upon a definite
policy. Perhaps also there was a certain perverse pleasure in keeping
a secret from Sewell, even though she ran a very real risk of
complications with the law for her deeds. That only added a spice to
the affair!

The inquest over, Mrs. Bascom went up to Sylvia’s room and found her
just awakening.

‘Feeling better and rested?’ she asked, the sight of Sylvia’s drawn
face putting to rout any lingering doubts as to her duty in shielding
her.

‘Yes. I’ll try not to be foolish again, Mrs. B.--Tell me, has
anything happened since they--they questioned me?’

‘The inquest has been held, but only the usual vague verdict was
rendered. Nothing else has occurred at all.’

‘They did not bring out any new facts?’

‘Not even all the old ones!’

‘And was anything said--about me?’

‘No. The whole thing was postponed until the evidence against Ruel
could be sifted, or at least that’s what I gather they meant.’

‘If I could only do something,’ muttered Sylvia tearfully. ‘If only
it weren’t for this ankle----’

‘There’s nothing better you can do than to rest and get well
speedily,’ soothed Mrs. Bascom. ‘When things get straightened out and
clear in your mind again----’

‘What do you mean? Everything is perfectly clear now. I know exactly
what I’m doing, if that’s what you infer.’

‘Not--not when you insist upon that will, not when you tell the very
details that involve you most suspiciously.’

‘You mean I’ve made my position--suspicious?’

‘Yes.’ Mrs. Bascom was moving about the room, so did not notice the
curious expression on Sylvia’s face at that moment. ‘If you’d only
listened to me first--let me help you----It was not like your usual
clear-headed way of doing things, Sylvia.’

‘I knew you’d say that, though I’d hoped----Never mind,’ she finished
flatly, and the buoyancy went from her face, leaving it cold, aloof,
and determined.

‘Mrs. B.,’ she said after a moment’s pause, ‘I suppose I told you a
lot of queer things about--about when I was in the chute, didn’t I?’

‘Yes, you did. And that’s another reason I could not understand some
of your answers.’

‘I don’t wonder. But don’t you see I was still dazed then and didn’t
know what I was saying? If I contradicted myself to-day--well, what I
said to the inspector is--what I shall stick to, so please forget the
rest.’

‘It sounded quite convincing as you told it to me,’ challenged Mrs.
Bascom.

‘Did it?--But won’t you forget it now?’

‘You mean your premonitions--that tap on your shoulder?’

‘You said there was nothing at the bottom of the chute, so that
proves I was only imagining things,’ interrupted Sylvia quickly. ‘It
was all fancy, you see.’

Mrs. Bascom could not reply to this. Caught in a web of her own
weaving, she did not deem it wise to confess about the knife she had
found there nor the way she had evaded lying about it. Robbed of any
retort, she merely studied Sylvia’s expression in silence, trying
to pierce beyond its blankness to find some emotion. Certainly her
words were clear-headed enough now.

‘Is Basil about the house?’ suddenly asked Sylvia.

‘I think so.’

‘Then would you mind asking him to come here? I want to talk with
him--alone.’

‘Surely. But you don’t mean alone--while you’re in bed, my dear, and
in your nightgown!’

‘Oh, I’ll slip on a negligee, though I guess Basil wouldn’t be
shocked.’

‘But, Sylvia, it isn’t proper.’

‘Why not?--That sounds like his step now.’

She raised her voice, calling his name, and when he answered, told
him to enter. This he did in the midst of Mrs. Bascom’s frantic
efforts to envelop Sylvia in her negligee! The result was more
revealing than concealing for a moment, but it was Mrs. Bascom who
blushed. Basil, as Sylvia had remarked, did not seem to mind.

‘Hello, Sylvia. Up and at ’em again, eh?’ he called cheerily.

Her stolidity broke into a gay smile of welcome.

‘Not up, but decidedly at ’em,’ she laughed. ‘Pick out an easy
resting-place, I want to talk to you.’ She signaled to Mrs. Bascom
to retire, but when the latter did not move as Basil drew up a chair
close to the bed, Sylvia added flippantly, ‘You’ll promise to be a
good boy? You see, Mrs. B. doesn’t seem to trust us--alone.’

Basil laughed heartily and was about to cap her remark with another,
but Mrs. Bascom did not stay to hear it. With outraged dignity, she
strode from the room, slamming the door obviously behind her.

In the hall she paused to gasp for breath. Decidedly she was
learning things about Sylvia! Until now she had seemed a quiet,
self-respecting young girl, but turning on Mrs. Bascom as she had,
becoming so suddenly secretive, and inviting a bachelor into her
bedroom--Either she was wholly out of her senses or--she was not the
sort of woman Mrs. Bascom had supposed.

The door to her own room stood open, so she stepped inside, then
halted abruptly at the sound of voices.

Her room was next Sylvia’s and the communicating door was ajar so
that she could hear every word Sylvia and Basil were saying. With all
due justice to her, Mrs. Bascom remained in her room not with any
idea of eavesdropping, but merely to satisfy her sense of propriety.
It was not in her code for young women to entertain men alone in
their bedrooms--under any circumstances. Hence it was her plain duty
to act as chaperon, even though unwelcome and unsuspected. She picked
up a book and began to read conscientiously.

For a few moments the two voices from the other room kept up a
battery of light chatter and Mrs. Bascom read a page or so. Then,
unconsciously at first, a phrase here and there seeped into her
ears as the voices lowered somewhat and became serious. Before she
realized what she was doing, the book slipped to her lap unnoticed,
and she bent toward the door to catch their words. And when she did
realize her attitude, she was deliberately listening, her conscience
ceasing to prick her, for the revelations she heard were too
astonishingly important to her knowledge of affairs.

It was Basil who first brought up the subject of the will.

‘Sylvia, what was the idea in saying King had told you he meant to
leave everything to you?’

There was a strained pause before she answered.

‘Why do you ask that? You heard what I said to the inspector.’

‘And I also know it wasn’t true.’

‘How?’ Her voice was sharp, frightened.

‘Because he discussed his financial affairs with me that morning,
told me of one alteration in his will he was going to make, but
decidedly it was not all in your favor.’

‘Then he must have changed his mind.’

‘I don’t think so,’ replied Basil levelly. ‘Besides, you said he told
you this a few days ago. If he had changed, it was obviously not in
your benefit.’

‘Then I--I must have confused the time.--You insinuate I was--lying?’

‘Come, Sylvia. Don’t get huffy about it. All I want to know is,
what’s the game? I’m not accusing you of--er--anything. You know I
admire your clever little brain and want to help you. I’ve told you
that before and it still goes.’

‘I--Let’s not discuss that, please.’

‘Why not?’ reiterated Basil calmly. ‘You know I like
you--tremendously. I’d been trying to get you alone all day before
the play to tell you----’

‘Don’t Basil, not now,’ pleaded Sylvia in a panic.

‘Why not now? It’s just the time you need help and advice. I only
want to understand your game so I can play up too.’

‘Play up?’ exploded Sylvia. ‘You accuse me of scheming and playing a
game, do you? You dare to talk that way to me--you, who sneak about a
dead man’s house like a thief--no, _as_ a thief!’

‘What do you mean?’ His voice was surprised, but not alarmed.

‘I saw you tiptoeing down the stairs just before the--the burglary.
Oh, I know what was in that desk--what you took from it. You knew
well enough where he put those unlisted securities and bonds. I don’t
have to look there to see what’s gone or guess where it is now.’

‘So you saw me, eh? And why were you up at such a late hour, might I
ask? Why didn’t you tell all this to Sewell?’

‘Because I wanted to hear what you had to say first. Also I haven’t
made sure yet----’

‘Ah! Two minds with but a single thought!’ laughed Basil a trifle
harshly.

‘What do you mean by that?’

‘Why, you expressed exactly the reason I kept mum about your little
prowling in the dark!’

‘About my--about me----’ Real fear choked her voice now, and Mrs.
Bascom involuntarily stood up, ready to act if necessary.

‘Yes, about your little trip down the hall. Oh, you didn’t know I
saw you hobbling down to Judge Brant’s room, did you? Well, I did. I
came up here to search about for the burglar’--he snickered at the
word--‘and paused on the stairs while you entered his room. I suppose
it was the creaking of the stairs that scared you back again, eh? Of
course you were after the will, but you didn’t get it. He sent it
off to his office for safe-keeping this morning.--Well, what did you
mean to do with it?’

‘Destroy it,’ whispered Sylvia desperately.

‘Quite likely, my dear, when you insisted on probating it to-day!’

Sylvia did not answer. Mrs. Bascom could not stand the suspense
any longer. Shamelessly she pulled her skirts up so they would not
rustle, and crept over to the door. Slowly she moved her head until
one eye could look through the crack.

Basil was on his feet beside the bed, his hands thrust into his
pockets, his whole attitude one of insolent victory. Sylvia crouched
back against her pillow breathing rapidly, her hands clutching at
the bedclothes and trembling, her eyes staring with horror. Her lips
moved as if in speech, but no words came from them.

That one glimpse was enough for Mrs. Bascom. Quickly she retreated,
intending to gain the hall, then enter Sylvia’s room and interrupt
this painful scene. She might have done so more easily by entering
through the communicating door, but instinctively she avoided
exposing her presence there, her mind groping to explain what she had
overheard.

Before she reached the hall, Basil spoke again.

‘That’s hit you where you live, eh? Well, think it over, Sylvia, my
dear. You’ve no need to fear. There’s an easy way to fix everything,
you know, for both of us. If you don’t discover it, I’ll tell
you--to-morrow.’

Mrs. Bascom took a few steps down the hall, then turned as if just
coming from the farther wing of the house. Thus Basil saw her, nodded
quite composedly, and moved down the stairs with some pleasantry
that she neither understood nor even heard. She had just made a
discovery. She did not like Basil, had always mistrusted him somehow,
now that she came to look back upon their meetings!

When Mrs. Bascom entered Sylvia’s room, the latter had not moved,
still cringed, staring blankly at the spot where Basil’s face had
been. Mrs. Bascom’s first impulse was to take her in her arms,
soothe away her abject terror and comfort her. Yet, as she started
to move toward the bed, reason conquered impulse, and she paused.
There was little she could say without revealing that she had
been eavesdropping, and nothing to be gained by such a course.
Moreover, she hesitated to approach Sylvia in the latter’s strange,
unsympathetic mood that had rebuffed her so sharply a few moments ago.

Steeling herself with these resolves, she went to the window
and began fussing with the curtains and shade to give Sylvia an
opportunity to make the first overture if she so desired. Evidently
she did not, so Mrs. Bascom raised the shade rather noisily and
whirled to face her.

‘You look very weary. You must rest, Sylvia.’

At the sound of her voice, Sylvia seemed to notice her presence in
the room for the first time, caught her breath sharply, and closed
her eyes before the light that flooded in the window.

‘Rest--rest?’ she whispered in a dull voice. ‘Yes, I must think.’

Her tired, helpless tone almost broke down Mrs. Bascom’s resolve,
but before she could move or speak, Sylvia abruptly sat up in bed,
slapping her cheeks to bring back their color, and tossing back her
hair with little impatient jerks of her head.

‘Mrs. B., I’m acting like a--a fool. You must think I’m
utterly--utterly----’ She could not find the right word, so left the
sentence unfinished with a fluttering gesture of impatience.

‘I think you’re utterly worn out, scarcely recovered from your
experience in the barn before you went through that ordeal of
questioning,’ finished Mrs. Bascom for her.

‘No, I’m behaving like a helpless infant, not a normal, healthy
woman. But it’s all so sudden--so hard to make out. And I must carry
on--alone.’

‘Tell me what’s troubling you, Sylvia,’ pleaded Mrs. Bascom,
coming closer and seizing her hand. ‘You know you can trust me. I
know--perhaps more than you think. Don’t you see, I’m as anxious to
find out the truth as you are? I’m a woman. I can understand, and
help you, if you’ll only let me, Sylvia.’

‘I wish--But you don’t understand, you can’t.’ Sylvia, with an
irrepressible shudder, leaned closer for a moment. ‘I can’t tell you.’

‘Because you’re afraid I won’t hold my tongue? Because you think I’m
just an inquisitive, gossipy old woman?’ Mrs. Bascom bridled in spite
of herself.

‘Oh, no--no, not that. It’s just you wouldn’t believe--you’d insist
I----Mrs. B., don’t fail me! Help me to do--what’s to be done. Trust
me--if you can!’ The last words were spoken in a whisper.

Mrs. Bascom did not know the first principles of psychology, but
she did know human nature. There was something Sylvia wanted to
tell her, yet dared not, some hint she was trying desperately to
convey, yet conceal. Mrs. Bascom could not fathom her purpose, yet
the appeal was genuine, sincere, and those whispered words seemed to
portend some further trial, some future event which would tax her
credulity even more. Whether these words had been impulsive or chosen
with forethought, Sylvia’s plea could not have been better planned
to bring back Mrs. Bascom’s determination to protect her. Somehow
they made her believe in Sylvia’s innocence in spite of the web of
circumstance that was tangling about her.

‘I do trust you, my dear, though I think you are mistaken, trying to
carry some double burden alone. You have the confidence of youth, but
that’s a deceptive strength without the knowledge of experience.’

‘I know, I know. It’s what you’d say, of course, but this isn’t the
first time I’ve had to--to carry a burden. If you’ll only trust me
and help me.’

‘Trust makes trust,’ answered Mrs. Bascom simply. ‘Now you must
stop talking. You’re wearing yourself out without cause. Save your
strength--if you must fight.’

Obediently Sylvia lay back, allowing Mrs. Bascom to smooth the
bedclothes and tuck her in comfortably. In a few minutes she dropped
off to sleep like a weary child. Then Mrs. Bascom tiptoed out of the
room and thoughtfully descended the stairs.

In the living-room, she saw Dr. New who came to meet her with a
question.

‘How is Miss Hawley now?’

‘Asleep, Doctor. She seems to be quite played out, poor dear.’

‘And well she might be. Young nerves may be made of steel, but
like piano wires, they are tightly stretched and ready to vibrate
too easily to any note, or even to snap. That experience in the
chute--it’s a wonder she didn’t have all sorts of hallucinations.’

‘She did.’

‘Ah, I thought so. And she told you about them? Then undoubtedly they
are still recurring to her mind, helping to keep her nerves upset.
There was no specially vivid one she told you about?’

‘Why, yes, a premonition of King’s death and--er--rats.’

‘So?--Well, that’s not so unnatural, though bad enough. I suppose the
confusion we made moving about gave her the former idea?--We must try
to clear her mind of them somehow, help her to forget them. The mind
is a delicate organism. In her nervous state, Mrs. Bascom, if she
broods over some one thing, she might become--well, she might suffer
for it, shall we say.’

‘That’s what I wanted to ask you about. Is it possible all this has
upset her so that she’d be perfectly normal about some things and
queer about others?’ asked Mrs. Bascom.

From the turn the conversation had taken, a scheme had sprung into
her mind and she acted upon it at once. If she could deliberately
plant the suspicion that the balance of Sylvia’s mind was temporarily
upset by the strain of her experiences, it might soften the
impression she had made about the will as well as prepare for the
indefinite fear of the future she had expressed.

‘I think I see what you mean, Mrs. Bascom,’ answered the doctor, his
eyes narrowing speculatively. ‘It’s quite possible, almost probable.
Is there anything definite you can point to that gave you this
impression?’

‘Woman’s intuition, perhaps, more than definite facts,’ evaded Mrs.
Bascom. ‘But I hope I’m wrong.’

‘Of course. You must watch her closely, however, and if you find a
basis for your fears, let me know at once. It may prove vital in the
treatment, you see.’

‘I certainly will, Doctor. I’m so glad you are taking charge, will be
right here when needed.--You do intend to stay a while yet?’

He replied in the affirmative and switched the conversation to
Inspector Sewell, about whose ability he questioned Mrs. Bascom
plainly and seriously.

‘Oh, Ezra’s all right--in his way. This is the first murder he’s ever
had to handle, but I guess he’s capable of it,’ she acknowledged.

‘You think he is really competent, then? You see, I’m anxious to see
something done, to call in a real detective if necessary. Mr. King
was my friend and----’

‘And mine,’ interrupted Mrs. Bascom. ‘That’s why I would agree to
getting an outside man if I thought it necessary. But I don’t--yet.
I’m sure Ezra Sewell is doing his best. He’s keener than he looks in
some ways, and I’d trust him anywhere as far as I was concerned.’

‘Thanks for the kind words, Ellen B.,’ said the inspector’s voice
with a chuckle, as he suddenly appeared in the doorway of the room.
‘’Bout time I interrupted, I figured, as things was gettin’ sort of
personal.’

‘You mean you’ve been guilty of eavesdropping?’ laughed the doctor as
Mrs. Bascom sniffed in humiliation that her words should have been
overheard. ‘Guess I’ll have to watch my speech from now on!’

‘Yep, you’d better. Inspectors an’ such ain’t supposed to have no
scruples, you know, Doctor. But then, I ain’t learned much for my
pains’--he turned to Mrs. Bascom with a grin--‘’cept Ellen B.’s
opinion of me, which is highly flatterin’, considerin’!’

She took a step away from him, but Sewell followed her.

‘Just a minute, ma’am. There’s somethin’ I’d kind of like to say
to you, if you don’t mind--sort of private like,’ he added for the
benefit of the doctor.

In silence they walked out of the house and down the lawn toward the
road, until Sewell suddenly stopped and spoke.

‘I’m goin’ back to have a little confab with Ruel about now. I
was just thinkin’ maybe you an’ me could talk things over a bit,
confidential like.’

‘What things?’

‘Well, I kind of figured we might compare notes in case I should
find you’d thought of somethin’ I hadn’t, or found somethin’ as
you’d--er--forgotten to tell me about.’ He paused suggestively, but
she did not take the hint.

‘Whom do you suspect?’ she answered evasively.

‘Huh. That’s a mighty big little question, Ellen B. There’s lots of
queer notions runnin’ round in my head,’ he drawled. ‘One of ’em
perhaps you’d be interested in hearin’ an’ helpin’ me with.

‘Say we was wonderin’ about some woman.--I’ll put it the way you do
one of those things you try to prove in geometry.--Given this woman,
let’s say she was one of the guests, an’ a right smart one too. She
sort of had her finger in things from the start, talked to lots of
’em, an’ had a chance to find things out an’ cover up some facts, if
she’d happened to want to.

‘Well, let’s say she did, but little things kind of showed her up.
She was so terrible honest, she wouldn’t tell a lie, so told things
so true it gave her away. Then again, she always knew just where
to look for things--clues that were sort of blind an’ might be
misleadin’. She answered some questions right cute an’----’

‘Ezra Sewell, are you accusing me of--of this murder?’ cried Mrs.
Bascom sharply.

‘Murder--you? Certain not. Whatever put such an idea in your head? I
was just talkin’ about this “suppose woman,” an’ I was just sayin’,
if there had been such a female, how’d you go about it to find out
why she did these things an’ what they were?’

Mrs. Bascom returned his gaze with snapping eyes and defiantly poised
head.

‘If I were a man, Ezra Sewell, I’d use what brains the good Lord gave
me. Being only a woman, I’d--I’d----’

Her retort rather failed in letter, though not in spirit. She could
think of nothing withering enough, so ended with a click of her teeth
that attempted to express her loathing and disgust.

‘Well, if you should happen to think of any way, let me know, Ellen
B.,’ suggested Sewell mildly, as he shuffled down the road.

Not until he had disappeared from sight did Mrs. Bascom allow herself
the relief of gasping aloud.--So he suspected her, had warned her--Of
what? She was only doing her best to solve the mystery--by concealing
vital facts from the law, her conscience added!

She trembled, but her thin shoulders straightened with determination
even as she leaned weakly against a tree for support.

 CHAPTER IX

 GROPING


‘You get along home, Pete. An’ don’t forget to look in on Ruel’s pigs
an’ see that everythin’ is all right on his place. Scat, now. I’m too
busy to talk.’

Inspector Sewell pushed up his bone-rimmed glasses to their rightful
position on his stubby nose. They had been pulled down from his eyes
so that he might look over their rims as he addressed his assistant,
but the very act of pushing them back into place added a note of
finality to his words. Peter shambled away without even the formality
of a ‘Good-night.’

Ezra Sewell sat in an odd-looking room, known officially as the
office to the jail. To study its details was, in a measure, to
understand the man himself, for the room held many hints both
tangible and intangible as to the character of its master.

Coming upon the jail from the outside, it appeared to be a
single-storied, unpretentious brick house, unless it were viewed
from the back where curtained windows gave place to barred ones.
A single door in the exact middle of the façade opened into the
office, large, square, and sunny. This was neither cluttered nor
formal in appearance, though it contained a variety of objects, each
scrupulously clean and neat, obviously upholding the maxim of ‘a
place for everything, and everything in its place.’

Hooked rugs in gay colors attracted the eye to the polished floor; a
row of pots in the windows added a touch of scarlet geraniums against
the white curtains, while a red checkered cloth and rose-painted lamp
adorned the table in the center of the room. A Governor Winthrop
desk filled one corner, attesting to a shrewd piece of ‘trading’ on
Sewell’s part; a safe in the opposite one was decked with a tidy and
a shining silver loving-cup bearing the inscription ‘To Ezra Sewell
in token of ten years’ service to the community--From his fellow
townsmen and women.’ Finally, an armchair, in which no one but its
owner ever dared to sit, a homemade bookcase, and four cushion-seated
chairs completed the main furnishings of the room.

Facing the outer door was another, now open, which gave a glimpse
of a narrow corridor with its three barred cells, while similar
doors on the right and left walls opened into a small bedroom and
bath and a kitchen, respectively. The remaining space on the walls
was incongruously decorated with a lithograph of ‘Rock of Ages,’ a
shotgun, a framed certificate, a pair of handcuffs of antiquated
design, a glass case of worsted flowers, and an ormolu clock.

At the desk, Ezra now sat in his shirt-sleeves, biting his pencil and
staring at a blank sheet of paper before him. His toes were hooked
back around the front legs of his chair, and his blue shirt bulged
out from under gaudy pink suspenders as he leaned forward intently.
For some time he sat motionless, while the light of the early
evening waned. At last, with a long-drawn sigh, he started to write,
then threw down his pencil and went to the table, lighted the lamp
ceremoniously, and, pushing aside the electric drop light on the
desk, set it down at his elbow and resumed his task.

He wrote a heading, followed it with a few brief lines, then read
them over aloud.

 List of Things to be Investigated

  1. Where the weapon is hidden.

  2. The motive for the murder. (Note: check up on King’s early
  life somehow.)

  3. The meaning of the figures 24578.

  4. Who the thief was and what he got.

‘But he might have been any one,’ a voice from outside the room
interrupted the reading. ‘How can you expect to find a man who just
walked in the window and out again?’

‘I think it was some one not so far off, Ruel,’ answered Sewell, as
if the interruption was not unexpected. ‘It wouldn’t surprise me none
if it had somethin’ to do with the murder too, in a way.’

‘Why do you think that?’

‘Little things. Not a sign of a clue, yet he was scared away sudden;
no attempt at real robbery except in that one drawer of the desk
where only papers were; the fact it happened just when it did, sort
of.’

To a visitor, this exchange of questions and information between a
prisoner and his jailer would have seemed strange, to say the least.
As a matter of fact, this was not the first time such a conversation
had been held. Sewell’s wits seemed to be sharpened by talking things
over with some one like Ruel and meeting his objections, for he was
loquacious both by nature and environment. To Ruel it had been a
God-send, for by this means he was able, in spite of his forced
confinement, to learn details of progress in the investigation.

‘Finally,’ continued Sewell. ‘Number five is--“What’s Ellen B.
concealin’?”’

‘But surely you don’t think she’s guilty?’

‘Guilty of what? Murder?--No. Hidin’ some sort of information or
somethin’ like that--yes.’

‘Why don’t you ask her point-blank then?’

‘Who, Ellen B.? I guess you don’t know her--or wimmen, for that
matter, son,’ answered Sewell lugubriously. ‘Give ’em nothin’ to talk
about, an’ you can’t stop ’em; let ’em decide to keep a secret, an’
the Lord help you!’

‘I take it you don’t think much of----’

‘Hold on. I ain’t said nothin’ against her an’ the whole tribe of
’em,’ bridled Sewell. ‘An’ I ain’t given up findin’ what she knows.
There’s ways of gettin’ around saints an’ devils--an’ she ain’t
exactly either.’

Ruel wisely made no comment on this bit of philosophy, so Sewell got
to his feet, walked over to the safe and squatted before it. Slowly
he twisted the combination, squinting at it and working his jaws. The
first time it failed to work, but the second attempt opened the door.
He took out a cardboard candy box, carried it to the desk and spread
out its contents--a bit of cement, the slip of penciled figures from
King’s dressing-gown pocket, the typewritten sheet from Sylvia’s
machine, and the sheaf of papers containing the guests’ summaries of
what they had seen.

These he eyed thoughtfully, touching one after another with the tip
of his finger, then drew a fresh sheet of paper toward him and made
a row of nine, carefully spaced circles, tracing them around a small
button. In these he put numbers from 2 to 0, then laboriously spread
his fingers over certain of these, studying his hand speculatively.

‘It’s possible--with hers,’ he muttered. ‘I just been tryin’, Ruel,
to see if it would come natural like to punch those typewriter
figures without thinkin’ about it much. Seems like it would, with
Sylvia’s hands.’

‘Of course it was only a coincidence if she says so,’ agreed Ruel
eagerly. ‘I tried that scheme myself on the wall here.’

‘Defacin’ township property, was you?’ chuckled Sewell. ‘You’ll have
to settle up for that.’

‘But, Ez, you know she had nothing to do with it. You said yourself
she was innocent.’

‘So I did, before she was found.’

The bars of the cell rattled furiously and Ruel’s voice raised to an
angry pitch.

‘Ezra Sewell, you listen to me. Sylvia could no more have----’

‘I’ve heard all that before, son. No more could she be the one to
grab for his money on that will business--but she’s doin’ it. Explain
me that first, before you tell me what she couldn’t do.’

‘But you haven’t given her a chance to explain. There must be some
reason. She says King told her he was going to make that will.’

‘That’s just the trouble. He didn’t!’

‘How do you know? You mean to call her a--a liar?’

‘Seems so. Seein’ as how King told me that very day he intended
leavin’ quite a sum of his money to the village an’ such, it looks
queer. Besides, a blind man could see she was makin’ that up. She
ain’t a good liar.’

The bars rattled again impotently, but Ruel could think of no
adequate retort to this statement except a loud groan. It was Sewell
who spoke again.

‘Now as you’ll let me finish what I had a mind to say, I will. I
ain’t said I don’t think she’s innocent, an’ I ain’t said but what
I think she’s hidin’ somethin’. What that is an’ why, I aim to find
out, that’s all, an’ I hope it ain’t nothin’ against her.--Seems to
me if only these here women weren’t so blame full of hidin’ things,
we’d get along faster.’

He abruptly swept the papers and clues into the box, thrust it into
the safe, and strode into the corridor to stand just outside Ruel’s
electrically lighted cell, rocking back and forth on his heels as he
spoke.

‘Son, I may be gettin’ old an’ all that, but I ain’t a fool. Of
all them as looks queerest--the most suspected ones--you an’ her
are the ones I’m most certain are innocent. First off, I know you
two. Second, it’s just ’cause you can’t explain things only by
coincidences an’ such, that I know what you say is true. I mean to
say, it’s easier to make up a real good lyin’ excuse for somethin’
than just a coincidence that may sound queer. If you two had denied
things right out, it would be different, but you ain’t tried to, see?
Leastwise, you ain’t on the most important things. As to you, son, it
seems like you’re the only one I can talk to open an’ trust. Don’t
spoil it, Ruel.

‘I know it’s hard on you, keepin’ you locked up here. You don’t have
to say nothin’ about how you feel toward Sylvia either. It’s writ all
over you. But you’re doin’ more good right here, not confusin’ things
up by messin’ in with help, than if you were free. That plain an’
hittin’ the nail on the head?

‘Now, then. You’re blamin’ me for not gettin’ anywhere. Oh, yes, you
are, an’ maybe you ain’t the only one neither. But that ain’t the
fact.--It won’t do no good steppin’ on everybody’s toes, accusin’
before you have proof. An’ it does do a whole lot just lettin’ every
one go unsuspected like. Look at that robbery business. It wouldn’t
have happened, I’ll bet, if whoever done it thought he was suspected,
see? But it proved there’s still some one about who--well, who ain’t
to be trusted, puttin’ it mild.

‘Take each one by turn. The doctor an’ the judge. Old friends of
King’s an’ not to be suspected, except they were handy at the
murder an’ might have been the burglar, of course. Ellen B. an’
Sylvia--both actin’ queer, hidin’ things an’ playin’ some sort of
fancy game. Basil Stacey--well, he’s got some things to explain yet.
An’ you--tied up with circumstantial evidence. You’re the goat now,
all right, an’ it’s handy havin’ one, even if you know he just butted
into things innocent.

‘Why, it’s just so simple, it’s all complicated. I got it all figured
out how King was killed, certain sure. One of his guests knew all
about the play, an’ planned accordin’. In the dark, he slipped out
the window back of the guests an’ came in the one near the desk. They
were all oiled an’ the curtains made it easy not to let in any light.

‘He hid in the dark till after you was supposed to have killed King,
then did it himself, slippin’ back as he had come. He probably locked
one window after him, an’ the other while the folks were messin’
around lookin’ for clues.

‘His motive? That’s what we’ve got to find out, not how he did it
in every detail. An’ that means findin’ who King really was, where
he----’

‘Let me out of here, Ez, and I’ll trace back his life,’ pleaded Ruel.

‘How? Visit every city in the country an’ Europe lookin’ for a man
who probably deliberately hid his history? Don’t be foolish, son. You
just sit tight, an’ one of these days we’ll have you out of here in a
jiffy. Meanwhile, I ain’t accusin’ any one, but I’m watchin’ ’em all,
an’ if they think I’m a fool--so much the better.’

Ruel nodded, convinced for the time by Sewell’s arguments. The
latter, having delivered himself of this oration, turned on his heel
to walk away, but changed his mind.

‘I wonder, Ruel, can I trust you to help me?’

‘Just try me, Ez. It’s what’s driving me crazy, doing nothing.’

‘Well, ’tain’t exactly doin’ much.--If I let you out of that cell,
hide you somewheres for a while, you won’t try to get away?’

‘I swear I won’t, Ez.’

‘All right.’ He unlocked the door and motioned Ruel to follow him
into the bedroom. ‘Now you set there in the dark right still an’ just
listen--listen hard, see? I’m about due for some callers an’ two
pairs of ears is better than one, just in case--There they come now.’

Sewell hurried from the room, leaving the door ajar, and dodged
into the corridor where he waited until the outer door opened, then
slammed the cell gate shut, switched off the light, and reëntered the
office.

‘Howdy, Doctor, howdy, Judge. Just closin’ up for the night. Glad to
welcome you here, unofficial. Set down an’ make yourselves to home.’

Ezra closed and locked the door to the cells before taking a chair
himself.

‘How is the prisoner? Told you anything yet?’ asked Judge Brant as he
eased himself into Sewell’s armchair and crossed his plump hands over
the hilly folds of his vest.

Before he replied, Sewell carefully filled and lighted his pipe,
seeming intent upon his task, but covertly studying the two men.
Both physically and mentally they were direct opposites in type.
Judge Brant was the more prepossessing in bulk and appearance, Dr.
New the more insignificant in physique, but keener of expression and
quicker to make a decision or express an opinion concisely. They were
both well dressed, obviously men of means and authority in their
respective professions.

The doctor sat erect and alert, smoking a cigar leisurely, and
scarcely seeming to move as he did so. The judge puffed on his with
avidity, and had an annoyingly nervous habit of opening his eyes wide
as if in amazement every few minutes, when there was nothing to cause
such an expression.

‘No, he’s gettin’ restless, but he ain’t confessed yet,’ Sewell
answered the latter’s question.

‘A little psychology, eh? Cage the out-of-doors man for a while and
he’ll soon weaken. That your theory?’ continued the judge approvingly.

‘I’m not so sure he’s guilty,’ interposed the doctor. ‘I’m
remorseful, in a way, since it was I who first accused him. He seemed
so genuinely confused and bewildered that after it was all over his
face rather haunted me.’

‘Sure, I’d have said he was innocent myself,’ answered Sewell, ‘but
look at the evidence against him.’

‘He hasn’t refuted that?’

‘Not satisfactory, Judge.’

For a time they discussed this chain of circumstantial evidence,
Sewell giving Ruel’s reasons for the facts but maintaining a
skeptical tone, the judge frankly doubting, and Dr. New weighing
their remarks mostly in silence. From this they drifted into an
argument over various phases of the mystery, the inspector allowing
the two men to choose their topics at will, listening, for the
most part, and putting in a remark or question only when directly
addressed or when the talk began to lag. He gained nothing in new
theories or knowledge, the two men being frankly puzzled and without
definite suspicions except for Ruel.

At length Sewell took a more active part in the conversation,
introducing the matter of the burglary so skillfully that it did not
seem to be his doing at all.

‘It’s puzzling,’ Judge Brant responded to his lead. ‘It seems to
me, after due thought, that the burglar must have been after some
definite thing and knew exactly where to look for it. Miss Hawley
mentioned papers were kept in that desk, as I recall it. That makes
it all the more certain it was some one who knew King and his ways
intimately.’

‘I don’t agree with you there,’ said Dr. New, leaning forward
intently. ‘Your logic seems to me at fault. A desk is a natural place
in which to keep papers, while a locked drawer invites investigation.
It is one of the first places in which a thief would look. Besides,
he stumbled into a chair and knocked it over, thus awakening us.
Certainly that would argue for an outsider who was not familiar with
the room.’

‘Both seem pretty possible to me, gentlemen. But seems like the
biggest part is, there’s no marks where he could have come in an’
gone out, none in the flower-bed outside the window, none in the
room. As to the chair, it’s easy to hit one in the dark, an’ after
the confusion in the house any one may have moved it out of its
rightful place.’

‘I suppose you’re right, Inspector, yet, if he was a clever man,
could he not have foreseen all this?’

‘Maybe, Doctor. But presumin’ it was an insider, who would you say it
was?’

‘I’ve thought of that, but I don’t like the obvious conclusion.’

‘What’s that?’

‘Well, it was neither the Judge nor myself. We each heard the sound,
jumped out of bed, and opened our doors almost simultaneously as we
struggled into bathrobes. As to Basil, his room was down the hall,
and he joined us before we reached the bottom of the stairs. That
lets him out. Miss Hawley couldn’t have done it and gotten back up
the stairs again with her bad ankle. That leaves only----’

‘Ellen Bascom!’ cried Sewell incredulously. ‘Why, I’d never thought
of her--for that.’ True the idea had never occurred to him, but now
that it had been suggested, it opened up several possibilities quite
against his will. ‘You say she was in her room an’ questioned you
when you came up again?’

‘Yes, she was standing in her doorway. She didn’t seem to be a bit
frightened, either,’ remarked Judge Brant thoughtfully.

‘She wouldn’t be,’ muttered Sewell. ‘How was she dressed?’

‘Scantily. Looked like a bed quilt around her shoulders, eh, Judge?’

‘Something like that, something bulky clutched around her shoulders.’

Ezra laughed aloud at this picture, but there was relief as well as
humor in his tone.

‘That settles it. I sort of hated to believe Ellen B. would do such
a thing. Now I know it. She wouldn’t be kitin’ around the house in
a nighty, not Ellen B.!’ He chuckled again, then stopped abruptly.
‘That kind of spoils the rest, too, though.’

‘What do you mean--what rest?’

‘Judge, you’re sure about somebody bein’ in your room?’

‘Of course. My wallet was on the floor, open, some of the papers half
out, others scattered to one side.’

‘That all?’

‘It was enough.’

‘But look here. Maybe it fell to the floor an’ they just spilled out.’

‘Not with my coat carefully spread over the back of the chair. It’s
a habit I always have--hanging it that way.’

‘Sure you did it as usual that night?’

‘Of course. Ask Dr. New. He came in and saw it for himself.’

Thus appealed to, the latter looked thoughtful for a moment before
replying.

‘Sorry, Judge, I’m afraid I can’t agree with you. Now that you
mention it, I’ve quite a vivid recollection that it was hanging by
one arm. I wouldn’t take an oath on it, but I’m reasonably sure.’

The judge’s fat cheeks flushed. ‘I--I think you’re mistaken, Doctor.
I’m very particular about my personal habits and I always hang my
coat up at night. I’d gone leisurely to bed and there’s no reason why
I should not have done as usual.’

‘It’s no killing matter, after all,’ said the doctor, raising his
eyebrows slightly at his companion’s confusion. ‘At that, habits----’

‘But it is kind of important,’ broke in Sewell hastily. ‘I can see
you’re a neat man, Judge, an’ I noticed that particular. But if you
forget this once, it means we’re suspectin’ some one innocent, see?’

‘Oh, I see, of course,’ muttered the judge somewhat apologetically.
‘I only objected to criticism about my personal habits, because I’m
sure that----’

‘Look here, was this chair near the door, between it an’ the bed?’

‘Yes, it was.’

‘Then maybe you knocked off the coat when you hurried past it, eh?’

‘That’s exactly what happened!’ agreed the doctor, nodding to Sewell
in appreciation of his adroit explanation.

‘Maybe,’ said Judge Brant, still rather doubtfully obstinate.

‘Then you see we’ve been tryin’ to make out one of the women did it
when neither one had a hand in any such thing.’

‘One of them? You mean to include Mrs. Bascom?’

‘That’s what I was thinkin’, Doctor, back there a ways when I said I
hadn’t figured her for thievin’ downstairs, but for somethin’ else. I
knew she didn’t, though, when you spoke of her nighty!’

He grinned again and the other two joined in with him.

He had accomplished his purpose; whether rightly or wrongly, for he
was not so certain as he strove to appear, he had diverted suspicion
from both the women. Incidentally, he had gained an interesting,
though irrelevant, view of the two men’s characters. Dr. New was
evidently a keen observer and a diplomat, whereas Judge Brant was
rather methodical and resentful of any personal criticism no matter
how trivial.

Soon after this, the clock struck ten, and the men arose to depart.

‘Don’t hurry, gentlemen, it’s only nine-thirty. That clock strikes
kind of contrary on the half-hours instead of right. It always has.’

‘Why don’t you have it fixed, then?’

‘Always aim to, but never seem to get there. Besides, I’m used to
it,’ apologized Sewell, noting their exchange of amused glances.

They delayed a few moments, but soon left. Once they were gone, the
inspector called to Ruel and led him back to his cell in silence.
When it was locked once more, he faced the young man through the
bars, waiting for him to make some comment on what he had overheard.

‘I don’t see why you wanted me to listen to _that_,’ said Ruel
disgustedly.

‘No? Then I take it you wasn’t so interested?’

‘Only that it was all another proof of the utter foolishness of
suspecting Sylvia for anything.’

‘Maybe. Personally, I calculate talkin’ is always interestin’ an’
informin’. Me, now, I learned a lot. Ellen B. ain’t involved like
I kind of thought she might be, an’ that’s somethin’. Then the
doctor is kind of softenin’ toward you while the judge ain’t. That’s
somethin’ more. As to the burglar, I kind of got a hunch who it was,
if not what he was after. Seems as if all that’s worth pretty much to
me.’

Ruel stared at him queerly.

‘You mean--Basil?’

‘Ain’t many others I could mean, sir. Think it over, same as I will,’
answered Ezra, shuffling away down the corridor.

‘But he was King’s friend, Ez. Why should he----’

There was no use addressing the vacant air; Ezra had gone without
answering.

Slowly Ruel prepared for bed, twisting and turning the inspector’s
remarks in his mind, trying to interpret Basil’s actions in the light
of this new suspicion. He had liked Basil in a general way, had
envied him for an assurance and easy manner so contrasting to his
own diffidence. They had been casual friends, meeting often at King’s
house, yet they had never been drawn together closely by any mutual
interests, and Ruel had recently begun to resent Basil’s attentions
to Sylvia, although never with any real foundation.

Like a flame, fed by a spark of suspicion, this resentment grew, even
though it had no fuel to feed upon--a fact that only increased its
intensity. As he climbed into the narrow cot bed, one thing became
more and more sure in his mind. Ezra Sewell’s methods were certainly
individual, his lazy geniality just a useful blind. Back of it all
was a keen brain, and Ruel felt suddenly reassured. He trusted
himself and Sylvia implicitly in this man’s hands.

 CHAPTER X

 ESCAPE


Mrs. Bascom was very busy the following morning, yet her activities
were somewhat peculiar. She had gone to sleep the previous evening
reading another of King’s books, awakened quite early, finished the
story, then tiptoed down to the library for another volume. This she
skimmed through, reading a page here and there only, and completing
it before breakfast.

From that time on, she had a book under her arm wherever she went,
and only when necessary did she raise her eyes from its printed
pages. For the most part she had remained in her room with the door
to Sylvia’s partly open. In this way she could be of service at a
moment’s notice if needed, but, more important still, she would be on
hand if Basil attempted to make his threatened call. This he had not
done, however, and nothing out of the ordinary had occurred.

Sylvia, pleading weariness, had decided to remain in bed until noon
at least. She had stayed awake, but whenever Mrs. Bascom looked
into the room, her expression was thoughtful, as if turning all the
energy saved by her languor into mental activity. Mrs. Bascom had not
interrupted this mood; in fact, she was sure Sylvia had not even seen
her stand in the doorway at all.

The house had been very quiet. The doctor and the judge had gone
for a walk, while Basil was content to read and write in the rooms
below. This did not surprise Mrs. Bascom, for she knew that he was on
his vacation, also that he had expressed a wish to be left alone to
loaf about and not feel he must go anywhere or do anything requiring
energy; yet she looked in on him more than once during the morning
and kept a watchful eye on what he was doing. Thus she knew, when he
later on went for the mail, that he was taking along two letters of
his own, but they were the ordinary size and bulk.

It was about noon when she finally laid aside her books and nodded
silently to some weighty decision she had made. She was replacing the
last volume in the bookshelf in the library when Basil returned.

‘Caught in the act!’ he laughed. ‘I’ve always heard it was the meek
and mild pillars of virtue who really support blood and thunder
literature, but I’d never suspected it of you. It only goes to show
how little we can judge our fellow beings by their faces--in a manner
of speakin’, as Sewell would say.’

A withering response rose to her lips, but she checked it back and
forced herself to smile.

‘You would think there was enough mystery about here, even to satisfy
me! But I read one of King’s stories--and that was my downfall.--Did
you stop for the mail?’

‘Worse and worse!--I have a letter for you, and I do believe it is in
a masculine hand.--Ah, your blush confirms it. Here, let me not delay
the happy moment longer.’ He turned about with elaborate courtesy
after he handed her the note.

She glared at his back for a moment, her lips curling with
unconcealed disdain to relieve the resentment she could no longer
control, then read the letter quickly. A gleam of satisfaction
lighted her eyes as she stuffed it into her dress, forcing herself to
speak casually and naturally.

‘A business matter--quite successful, too.’

‘Sounds serious.’ Basil turned about again with a fatuous grin. ‘I
only hope you haven’t been playing the market, Mrs. Bascom? There are
widows and----’

‘Widows!’ finished Mrs. Bascom emphatically, and the one word neatly
closed the subject. ‘I think dinner--or lunch, as King always called
it--will be ready as soon as you are.’

‘Good, I take the hint. I’ll be with you in a tick.’

Throughout the meal they talked quite steadily on various
subjects, but the one uppermost in their minds had no share in the
conversation. Mrs. Bascom asked a good many questions about city
life, a subject for which she expressed considerable interest and
little knowledge. Basil responded readily enough to her lead, but
she rather suspected his answers were not always strictly truthful.
Though his manner was deferential and serious, she sensed the lighter
mood behind it, so accepted his statements in equal gravity without
believing a word of them. That he could be quite charming and
entertaining, he proved conclusively, yet she was on her guard to
discount all this, repressing a growing dislike for him and his easy
duplicity.

Ezra Sewell arrived soon after the meal was concluded. He requested
Mrs. Bascom to find out if Miss Hawley felt able to go through the
papers in King’s desk and check up on a possible loss. This Sylvia
consented to do, insisting she was perfectly rested and anxious to be
of assistance. As soon as she had dressed, Sewell himself came up to
assist her down the stairs, but she declined his offer, saying it was
time she learned to get about alone, become less dependent on others.

Basil joined them in the den where the robbery had taken place, and
Mrs. Bascom watched keenly for some interchange of greeting between
him and Sylvia, but the latter did not look up or speak as she began
to take out the papers from the drawer that had been forced open.

Coming upon a sealed envelope that was not labeled, she reached out
for a paper-cutter with which to open it, but there was none on the
desk. Sewell interpreted her gesture rightly and put her question
into words.

‘A paper-knife? It ain’t where it usually is, eh?’

Sylvia nodded.

‘What kind of thing was it?’

‘Why, it was one of Mr. King’s favorite keepsakes. It was shaped like
a sword--silver blade and cleverly imitated hilt.’

Something had warned Mrs. Bascom at Sewell’s first question, so she
was able to conceal her emotions at the moment. Undoubtedly it was
the knife she had found in the chute--had thought was familiar--knew
was the one used to murder King!

‘It was always kept on this desk?’

‘Always.’

‘Hm.--Perhaps the burglar opened the drawer with it, an’ took it off
with him,’ suggested Sewell with apparent indifference. ‘But let’s
see if he took anythin’ else, Miss Hawley.’

She continued with her work, a moment later looking up at the
inspector.

‘There is one bundle of papers missing. They were folded sheets, a
package about two inches thick, I should say.’

‘You know what was in ’em, what they were about?’

‘No, not exactly. I only saw the bundle, folded up in a blank sheet
of paper. There were several sheets inside this, and it was something
to do with business, I gathered.’

‘Then you wouldn’t know ’em if you saw ’em again?’

‘No.--All I do know is that Mr. King locked them in there late in the
afternoon of the--the day he died.’

‘But he might have taken ’em out again himself.’

‘I doubt it. He was busy with his guests from that time on. I’m sure
he did not remove them.’

‘An’ the thief did, eh?’

She nodded silently, and for the first time gave one fleeting glance
at Basil. Mrs. Bascom saw this, interpreted it as half pleading, half
defiant, but Basil’s expression did not change in response to it.

‘Then it seems like another of these here affairs that might have
been done this way or that way, for all we know. I’d give fifty cents
if I could get hold of somethin’ definite an’ clear just for once,’
grumbled Sewell disconsolately. ‘You ain’t got anythin’ to add, I
s’pose, Basil? You knew somethin’ about his business affairs. Do you
happen to know about this?’

‘Not a thing more than Sylvia does,’ responded Basil readily. ‘He
spoke of some business matter he wished to run over with me sometime,
but gave no hint as to its purport. He has often talked things over
with me--the business end of his writing and all--so I have no idea
just what he had on his mind this time.’

‘This safe, Miss Hawley, I wonder if the thief broke into that too?’

At this implied question, Sylvia pointed to a panel in the wall and
told the inspector how to open it. This he did, revealing the locked
door of a wall safe.

‘Maybe you’d better open it an’ make sure.’

‘I can’t, Inspector. It was Mr. King’s private safe and only he knew
the combination.’

‘Then I guess we don’t need to bother with that now.--Miss Hawley,
if you don’t mind, I’d kind of like to have a little confab with you
alone, just to run over certain things a bit.’

This was a request Mrs. Bascom had both anticipated and tried to
avoid. She had fully intended to talk with Sylvia herself, to impress
upon her the very real danger of her position. That she had not done
so was partly her own fault, partly due to Sylvia’s unresponsiveness.
Yet Mrs. Bascom had come to fear Sewell’s innocent conversations, his
apparent simplicity, so she tried to forestall him.

‘Ezra Sewell, you know she isn’t fit to go through another grilling.’

‘Grillin’, ma’am? I promise it won’t be that--just a friendly chat.’

‘Call it what you will, she’s upset and----’

‘No, Mrs. Bascom,’ interrupted Sylvia. ‘I may have been foolish and
nervous before, but I’m perfectly fit again now. Besides, I want to
clear up things, to talk them over with Inspector Sewell.’

Mrs. Bascom attempted to dissuade her, but Sylvia remained firm,
ignoring all hints obdurately, quite as determined on the interview
as the inspector himself. Almost literally shoved out of the room,
Mrs. Bascom’s temper very nearly got the better of her. She even
contemplated eavesdropping to further her own knowledge of events
and to be able to rush in and interrupt matters if Sylvia became too
involved. But before she had decided on a definite plan, Dr. New
entered the hall and insisted upon leading her away to another room
for a private discussion of Sylvia’s physical condition.

‘Frankly, Mrs. Bascom, I’m rather troubled about her,’ he began,
placing the finger-tips of one hand against those of the other and
staring at them as he moved his palms backward and forward. ‘I do not
mean so much her physical as her mental condition.’

‘She seemed perfectly rational--if that’s the proper word--to me.
She’s just been going over King’s papers with Inspector Sewell and is
now talking with him alone.’

‘Was anything found to be missing?’

Mrs. Bascom briefly recounted the discovery of the loss of the papers.

‘I see. Still I’m not satisfied. Her mind is clear now and her
physical reactions seem normal for the moment, yet, when I stepped
into her room earlier this morning, she was quite the reverse. Her
pulse was racing, her nerves too taut, although she seemed relaxed
to the point of enervation. When I talked with her, asked a few
simple questions to test her reactions, her answers were disjointed,
almost irrelevant at times and contradictory. Finally she became
moody, silent, and hostile, to judge from her eyes.’

‘But is that exactly abnormal?’ countered Mrs. Bascom. She was
privately wondering whether this was acting on Sylvia’s part, or
whether her plan to make the doctor suspicious of the latter’s mental
balance was not working a little too well. ‘You know she’s been moody
and unlike herself ever since I found her in the chute.’

‘You mean, in other words, that because she is a woman she has a
right to be changeable?’ twitted the doctor. ‘Seriously, it is this
exaggeration of mood that troubles me, you admit she is different,
vacillating from mood to mood. A normal mind has periods of extremes,
depressions on the one hand and gayety on the other, but between them
come stretches of repose--a sort of middle ground, so to speak. She
goes too rapidly from one extreme to the other and does not seem to
gain that equilibrium which is natural.’

‘True--in a way. I’ve noticed that myself. But what do you infer from
that?’

‘Merely, we must watch her more carefully, Mrs. Bascom. Understand
me, I am not actually alarmed or predicting any relapse, but this
mental state, if allowed to continue, would result in a super-nervous
condition that might prove decidedly serious. You have influence with
her, you can do much that a man or her physician cannot. Find out
what is really troubling her and let me know. Then we can take steps
to counteract it.’

It was upon the tip of Mrs. Bascom’s tongue to reply that she had
already tried and failed, but instead she promised to do all she
could. Then the doctor gravely left her.

For a time she sat there, silent and thoughtful, her mind working
rapidly. Something must be done, some action must be taken. But
what? Ezra Sewell seemed content to let matters drift; that was not
her idea of tracing a murderer. She had little as yet to go on, yet
she had something which no one else knew about and her ruse for
protecting Sylvia had obviously borne fruit.

Her speculations were interrupted when she heard a door open and saw
Sewell assisting Sylvia toward her room. The latter was silent and
thoughtful, but the inspector talked in a cheerful monologue. Mrs.
Bascom followed after them to Sylvia’s room, and there stopped Sewell
as he was leaving.

‘I want to talk with you. Where are you going now?’

‘Well, I was goin’ down town, Ellen B. ‘Course if you----’

‘I’ll go with you,’ she interrupted quickly, then turned to Sylvia
who was sitting silently by the window.

‘Did he ask you a lot of questions?’

‘A few. We just talked--in general.’

Mrs. Bascom realized she was in an unresponsive mood, so made her
comfortable with some cushions and prepared to leave.

‘Sylvia, will you promise me to stay here in this room and rest until
I return. Will you?’

Sylvia nodded assent rather wearily.

‘Ring this bell if you want anything and I’ll tell Martha to listen
for it.--Now remember, you’ve promised to stay here.’

‘I will, Mrs. B.’

‘By the way, I intend to stop in at the jail for a moment or two,’
suggested Mrs. Bascom offhandedly. She waited, but Sylvia turned her
head away and did not speak. ‘I thought you might like to send a
message to Ruel or perhaps a note?’

‘Tell him I’m sorry, of course,’ murmured Sylvia in such a low tone
that Mrs. Bascom scarcely made out her words. They were far from what
the latter had expected for an answer, but when nothing more was
added, she left the room, her face puckered into a frown as soon as
she entered the hall.

So Sylvia would not even send a message of cheer to Ruel, much less
the sort of intimate message she had expected to carry!

Ezra Sewell sat in his automobile patiently waiting for her. After
one glance at her face as she climbed in beside him, he drove rapidly
down the road and maintained what for him was a reckless pace until
they drew up before her home.

‘Well, here we are, Ellen B. Now what you got to say? From the look
on your face back there, I don’t know whether to expect a revelation
or a revolution!’

‘My information may prove both, Ezra Sewell,’ she answered briefly.

‘Information, did you say?’

‘I did, even though you may think you’re the only one who knows
anything about this affair.’

‘Wish I was an’ did, ma’am. Seems like I ain’t an’ don’t, though.’

‘I’m glad you admit it, at least.--However, I came to tell you two
things, and here they are. The other day I recalled one little fact
I’d almost forgotten. While we were searching for clues after the
play, one of the guests, Mrs. Tenney, found a stone or pebble on the
floor, but did not mention it in her written summary as it seemed so
unimportant. I wrote her when I recalled it. Here is her answer.’

She handed Sewell the letter Basil had delivered to her. He read it
slowly aloud:

  MY DEAR MRS. BASCOM--

  Yes, I do remember finding that pebble or piece of cement or
  whatever it was. As it seemed useless, I threw it out of the
  window later. Roughly (no pun intended, I assure you), the spot
  where I found it was on the edge of the rug a few feet from the
  desk over toward that closed window.

  That answers all your questions, I think, though for the life of
  me I can’t see anything to them. I suppose I am not intended to!

  Do give my love to that wonderful specimen of a ‘constabule’
  who----

Ezra read the remainder of the note in silence, chuckling at the
innuendos quite frankly.

‘Thanks for lettin’ me see this--all of it,’ he nodded. ‘It kind of
backs up our suspicions, don’t it?’

‘The other information,’ continued Mrs. Bascom, paying no attention
to his remark, ‘concerns Mr. King.--Have you ever read any of his
books?’

‘Sure. Mighty lively an’ interestin’ fun, too.’

‘I have just finished skimming through them all--especially some of
the older ones I did not know he had written. I think they are more
than merely interesting stories. They tell where he lived before he
went to New York.’

‘My land!’ exclaimed Ezra fervently. ‘You don’t mean it! Ellen B., if
you ain’t the cleverest, smartest woman----But get on, tell me the
rest.’

She was flushing faintly for no accountable reason at his compliment,
but her words were tart enough. ‘It’s plain as can be to any person
of sense. It was Boston.’

‘What was the book you found that in? How are you sure?’

‘It was in most all of them.’

‘But look here, near as I can remember they were written about
different places.’

‘Some of them were, of course, but more of them were laid in Boston
than any one other city. Besides, I found several, written some years
ago, I guess, and they were all about Boston. His characters are
invariably New England people too. I guess I’ve visited my sister in
Boston enough to know his descriptions are accurate, and that’s why
his stories are clear and interesting, because he wrote about places
he knew. Another thing. He must have known detectives and criminals
and their ways. He shows it in his books.

‘I believe, Ezra Sewell, that he himself was a detective working in
Boston at one time, and his death was an act of revenge by some
crook he had captured. That explains everything.’

She concluded with an emphatic nod. Ezra took off his hat slowly
and scratched his head. He had been prepared to accept some shrewd
deduction from Mrs. Bascom, but this was a little too intangible, too
far-fetched for him.

‘Well, now, seems kind of possible, but I dunno as it means as much
after all. Sounds kind of like one of his own stories. ’Course I can
write down there an’ inquire a bit, but----’

‘You ought to go there yourself--do something at once,’ snorted Mrs.
Bascom, observing the doubt in his face. ‘It is certainly worth that
effort to find out.’

‘Nope, I can’t get away just now, Ellen B. Got too much to do here.’

‘In other words, you don’t believe me.’ She made a clicking sound
with her teeth that expressed her feelings of scorn for his judgment.
‘Very well, then, Ezra, I’ll tend to that myself.’

‘Goin’ down personal, you mean?’

Mrs. Bascom ignored his questions pointedly.

‘I want to talk to Ruel, if it’s possible. Will you take me there or
shall I walk?’

‘’Course I’ll take you to the jail.’

Wisely he let the matter of King’s identity drop for the time being.
After all, there was a chance that her deductions might prove right,
and certainly he must let no such chance escape. He could write
officially to make inquiries. But she was piqued, and he felt sure
she would do as she had threatened. Ellen B. was tenacious, not
easily discouraged, and if she determined to go on such a wild-goose
chase personally--well, that was her affair, and between them, they
would certainly find out all there was to know.

When they reached the jail, a new thought struck him.

‘Did you go for the mail to-day?’

‘No, Basil Stacey brought it.’

‘Hm. Then you can’t say as to who got letters, eh?--Happen to know
whether he did any writin’ himself, mail anythin’ to-day?’

‘Yes, two letters.’

‘Observin’, ain’t you?--Well, guess I’ll do a sort of errand or two,
Ellen B. Take me half an hour, maybe, then I’ll come back here for
you. That long enough for a call?--Help yourself, ma’am. You’ll find
the key to his cell hangin’ in the office near the door. Go right in
an’ make yourself to home. Guess he ain’t a desperate criminal, so
you needn’t be scared.’

Chuckling softly, Ezra left his car and watched her quizzically as
she disappeared into the jail, before he set off down the street,
whistling tunelessly as he scuffed away.

His first errand led to the post-office and general store, where he
lounged until the little room emptied of its few leisurely customers,
then he went to the mail window and gave the postmistress an order to
list all incoming or outgoing mail for any of the people at King’s
house. Questioning her as to the morning’s mail, he received a bit of
information that was both interesting and illuminating. Basil Stacey
had mailed his two letters, both addressed to his office, and with
them two packages of approximate letter size, though much more bulky.
These had gone under registered mail to his office also.

Turning this information over in his mind, Sewell next sought out
several of his feminine neighbors in the village and shamelessly
gossiped with them, in each case leading the subject about until
it touched upon Sylvia Hawley, and resulted in a brief character
sketch through these women’s eyes. Invariably, Sylvia came out with
flying colors as a thoroughly likable, sincere, and unselfish woman,
irrevocably devoted to Mr. King and his affairs.

It was almost an hour later he returned to the jail, expecting to
find Mrs. Bascom either already gone or impatiently waiting with
some quite personal remarks in store for him. She was not in the
office, and a glance at the hook where the key to Ruel’s cell hung
proved it was still in place. Grinning to himself, Ezra stepped into
the corridor to ask Ruel when she had gone and if she had left any
message. But the words froze on his lips.

There in the cell sat Mrs. Bascom, gagged, and tied hand and foot,
perched bolt upright on Ruel’s cot!

Ruel was gone!

 CHAPTER XI

 EZRA TURNS THIEF


Ezra Sewell stood stock still, too helpless with surprise to move.
Ellen Bascom, a prisoner--Ruel Moore, his rightful prisoner, gone!
The obvious conclusion was that Ruel had made this exchange and
escaped, yet Ezra was reluctant to accept the obvious. It was not
like Ruel to have done such a thing, nor was it consistent with Mrs.
Bascom’s character to have submitted to the exchange. She might have
been overpowered, but again that was scarcely plausible from either’s
standpoint.

As he stood there holding to the bars in his astonishment and trying
to muddle out some explanation, the comedy of it suddenly struck
Ezra. A chuckle escaped his lips, became a prelude to a convulsing
sputter of laughter. Ellen Bascom in jail! Ellen Bascom, helpless,
gagged so that she was even robbed of the power of speech, sitting
there bolt upright in a cell, powerless to move, her eyes snapping at
his! And even in her captivity, she was so calm, so haughty in her
pose, so ridiculously out of place.

His mirth passed as quickly as it had come. His duty and a desire to
learn from her own lips what had happened were the chief causes for
this, but back of them may have been a guilty feeling at the baneful
stare from her eyes, just visible above the gag. Obtaining the key
from the office, after a hasty glance both inside and outside the
jail to make certain Ruel was nowhere in sight, Ezra unlocked the
cell door and hurried to Mrs. Bascom’s side, his face now grave and
serious.

First he untied the gag, which proved to be Ruel’s handkerchief, or
rather, one that Ezra had lent his prisoner. But Sewell noted it
was quite loosely tied, might easily have been slipped off by the
manipulation of her jaws, if she had really tried to free herself.
The moment it came undone, Mrs. Bascom spoke, her words and tone
sharp with sarcasm.

‘Have you quite finished your laughter at a poor, helpless woman?
Please don’t consider relieving my discomfort for an instant until
you have entirely exhausted the humor of your side-splitting joke.’

‘Now, now, Ellen B., I apologize right off to you. It wasn’t a
gentleman’s way to be actin’, I know, but if you’d only been me an’
could see how you----’

‘Thank you, I don’t need to _see_. I can _feel_ how helpless and
uncomfortable I am.--A woman in distress must seem very comical to
you, of course.’ By this time, Ezra had unbound the rope from her
wrists and was stooping to loosen those which shackled her ankles,
but she quickly drew her feet back beneath the edge of the bed. ‘I
prefer to do the rest myself, Ezra Sewell.’

Ezra straightened and turned his face away to conceal the involuntary
grin that broke over it. Of course he should never have presumed to
touch her ankles!

She sprang to her feet and took a step toward the door, but Ezra
stepped in front of her to block the way, and she faltered back as if
from contact with him.

‘Hold on a bit there, Ellen B. Seems like as if there might be a
little explainin’ before you go.’

‘Oh!’ The exclamation, though brief, expressed withering scorn. ‘I’d
thought that to a keen mind such as yours, the situation would be
quite obvious. I beg your pardon for presumin’ on your intelligence!’

‘No presumin’ I’m sure, Ellen B.--I suppose it was Ruel as did this?’

‘Not at all! Of course I did it myself--just to amuse you. It was
such a good joke, so easy to tie myself all up, lock myself in, and
hang up the key, outside!’

‘But why did he do it?’ asked Sewell, quite oblivious to her ridicule.

‘Because I asked him to, naturally. He was so nice about it, too.’

‘Come, Ellen B., this ain’t no time for jokin’.’

‘No? I guess I must be dense. I’d thought you--er----’ She paused as
their eyes met for an instant. There was an unspoken command in his
that suddenly made her flush and stammer. This only increased her
ire, yet somehow it checked the remainder of the retort that came to
her lips.

‘A prisoner has escaped,’ continued Sewell pointedly, ‘an’ it’s my
business to find out how an’ why, ma’am.’

The very mildness of his tone warned her that he was in earnest, and
she began to speak rapidly to cover up her confusion.

‘I came here as soon as you left me, found the key, and entered this
cell. Ruel appeared glad to see some one really interested in his
welfare and innocence. I sat down on the cot, he stood before me,
and we were talking when----’

‘About what, ma’am?’

‘Nothing special--Sylvia, and the mystery in general.’

‘You was deliverin’ a message from her to him, maybe?’

‘No. She sent none, though I asked her----’ Mrs. Bascom stopped in
confusion, and glanced sharply at Sewell as she interrupted her
thought. ‘Suddenly he made a break for the door that I had left ajar,
and closed me in. It seemed to be a spring lock or something. Then he
returned, let himself in again, and bound and gagged me as you saw.
He’d gotten the rope from outside somewhere. After he’d locked the
door again, he hung up the key and went away.’

‘Without explainin’?’

She shook her head in the negative.

‘How long ago was that?’

‘Thirty or forty minutes, I should say. We talked only a very short
time before this happened.’

‘Hm. There wasn’t nothin’ you said as might have given him reason to
do this?’

She bridled instantly, recovering some of her former asperity. ‘Are
you accusing me of urging him to do this, Ezra Sewell, of asking him
to tie me up?’

‘I’m askin’, not accusin’, Ellen B.’

‘Then I’ll answer you that I’m not a medium with powers of seeing
into other people’s minds, even though I can read yours. I’m sure
I never stopped to analyze my words or what he thought in his mind
about them.’

‘I can believe you there, Ellen B.,’ nodded Sewell enigmatically,
but if her words were intended as an evasion, he either overlooked
or deliberately let them pass unchallenged. A slight relaxing of
her tenseness did not escape his keen eyes, however, as he abruptly
changed the subject. ‘Hope he didn’t tie you up too tight? Them ropes
didn’t hurt, did they?’

His solicitude was disarming and, in spite of herself, Mrs. Bascom
thawed perceptibly. Ezra asked her a few other inconsequential
questions, then pushed open the door and followed her out into the
office, concealing his own emotions at this latest development.

‘Can I drive you somewheres, Ellen B.?’ he asked as they came to the
outer door.

‘No, thank you. I’m going to my house for a little, then back to
Mr. King’s. I don’t know just how long it will take me, and I need
the exercise, anyway, so I’ll walk.--I--I hope you won’t hold this
against Ruel. I think he was sort of distracted and impatient over
his confinement. I’m sure he meant no wrong--to any one.’

‘Oh, that end of it’s all right. But when a prisoner escapes, it’s
kind of serious business, you see.’

‘Not if it’s a secret--if no one knows about it, Ezra.’ Her eyes met
his squarely, and this time he perceived their message at once.

‘Perhaps not,’ he nodded slowly. ‘Anyways, as long as no one knows
except us two, we won’t do anythin’ serious about it--yet. Of course,
his escapin’ is bad, a felony in itself. But if he was proved
innocent of murder, I guess the judge would pardon that, Ellen B.’

Mrs. Bascom smiled quite genuinely in her relief, then turned and
hurried down the street. Inspector Sewell stood in the doorway
watching her until she had disappeared around a curve in the road.

‘Smart woman--slick as a whistle,’ he muttered to himself as he
reëntered the office and, walking over to the desk, sat down to think.

Now that he was alone, he arrived at a definite conclusion almost
instantly. It was not because Ezra Sewell was mentally slow that he
had not reasoned this out before. There were two very distinct causes
for his delinquency; one, that he had been too interested in Mrs.
Bascom and her retorts to consider seriously the reason for Ruel’s
act, the second, that he could not help but admire her spirit and the
ease with which she evaded questions she did not care to answer, yet
never told a positive falsehood. Nor had he tried to force answers
from her by reiterating such questions. He knew by experience the
futility of such a course, appreciated the value of a more indirect
method of securing the truth.

With her departure, however, everything seemed perfectly clear and
obvious with scarcely a thought.

‘Pretty clever, but just a little too much so to fool me,’ he said
aloud, unconsciously addressing his words toward the cell where Ruel
had formerly shared his oral musings. ‘Guess it happened about this
way. Ellen B. didn’t think I paid enough attention to her bright
idea about goin’ to Boston to look up King’s past, so darned if she
didn’t persuade Ruel to go for her--just like that! Not that it’ll do
any harm, as far as I can see. Guess it’ll be better for him than
stayin’ cooped up here as long as folks still think he’s a prisoner.

‘Well, now, let’s see. She has him lock her in an’ tie her up just
to make it look natural an’ fool me. But there’s where she laid it
on a bit thick, seems to me. It wasn’t necessary at all, at all. An’
naturally he tied her up gentle, too, so all that talk was--well,
actin’--all except her bein’ mad at me for laughin’ an’ her bein’
willin’ to keep it all a secret.’

Ezra slapped his knee and chuckled again at the picture his memory
recalled. Then his eyes fell upon a folded slip of paper, tucked
