beneath the ink bottle. It proved to be a note, without address or
signature. But neither was necessary as he read the hurried scrawl in
Ruel’s handwriting:

  I promised not to break away from _you_, but not any one else.
  Don’t worry, I won’t disgrace you, and will keep out of sight.
  Will call on you the moment I return.

‘Yep, when he returns--from Boston. So that’s that, all neat an’
tidy.--But that Ellen critter. ’Tain’t good for her to think she’s
foolin’ me completely. ’Tain’t good for any woman, least of all her.
Guess I’d better just drop by her house an’ kind of let her know by a
hint like.’

He carefully locked the outer door of the jail so that no one could
enter and discover Ruel’s absence, and the very resistance of the
lock attested to the fact that this precaution had not been taken for
months or years. Then he set off down the road at a comfortable amble.

It was not far to Mrs. Bascom’s house, spick and span in its recent
coat of white paint, set off by trellised crimson rambler vines
and shaded by a tall elm that dwarfed as well as protected it. The
pathway to the front door cut through velvety grass and lanes of
flowers, here and there speckled with early blossoms, but mostly
green with promise of later blooms.

The shades were all pulled down to the window sills to keep out the
hot sun, but the door stood wide open. Ezra stepped inside, pulling
off his somewhat battered felt hat and twirling it on his fingers,
as he glanced at the closed doors on the lower floor, then up the
stairs into their cool darkness. Not a sound came from anywhere in
the house. He put a foot on the stairs to ascend, then drew back.

‘Nope. She might be dressin’ or somethin’,’ he muttered, then raised
his voice in a lusty shout. ‘Ellen B., oh, Ellen B.’

Immediately there was a crash like the hasty closing of a box or
trunk, followed by a scurry of footsteps. Ezra listened as they
descended from the attic and brought Mrs. Bascom to the head of the
stairs. She was flushed, excited, and startled.

‘My land, you scared me!’ she explained needlessly. ‘I--I was just
seein’ to something up there and----’ She stopped abruptly to recover
her breath. ‘Well, what do you want now, Ezra Sewell?’

‘Me? Nothin’,’ he drawled, but he wondered why she seemed to look
almost guilty. Could it be that Ruel was hiding here, waiting for a
chance to slip away? It would be easy to find out. ‘I just happened
along to ask you if you was ready to go back. Thought I’d drive you
up.’

The flush slowly seeped from her face, though she still eyed him with
suspicion as she descended the stairs.

‘Yes, I’m ready now, and I’d be glad for a lift after all.’

‘Then I’ll go get the car, shall I?’

‘Don’t bother. I’ll go with you.’

Was it only imagination or was she now quite anxious to get him away
from her house? He waited while she locked the door, then slipped the
key under the mat quite casually. That rather settled his suspicions
that Ruel was in the house. Such being the case, why was she so
perturbed?

Before he could answer his own query, Mrs. Bascom briskly led the way
to the street and turned in the direction of the jail.

The ride back to King’s house was made in silence as far as
conversation was concerned--a silence enforced by the clatter of the
ancient automobile. But when they arrived in a little swirl of dust,
Ezra shut off the engine and faced his passenger quizzically before
she could get out.

‘I’ve been thinkin’, Ellen B. About that idea of yours of trailin’
Mr. King. Guess there might be somethin’ to it after all.’

‘I’m sure there is, but you needn’t bother about it. I’ll tend to it
in my own way as I said I would.’

‘Then it won’t be necessary for me to make the trip, eh? ’Course
you’ll tell me what you find out?’

‘Certainly.’

‘An’ you’ll keep it a secret, about what happened this
afternoon--Ruel an’ all?’

‘I’ve already said I would,’ she answered as she descended to the
road. ‘I’m not likely to boast about it. I should think that would be
obvious--even to you!’

‘That’s fine, an’ I hope he learns somethin’ too.’

Mrs. Bascom turned so suddenly she had to steady herself against the
automobile.

‘He? What do you mean?’ she questioned with an unsuccessful attempt
to sound innocently bewildered.

The self-starter chugged loudly, caught, and the engine roared with
staccato explosions. Just before he threw in the clutch, however,
Ezra smiled down at her and called above the noise, ‘Tell him I’ll
always have a room an’ bed waitin’ for him--when he gets back!’ Then,
with a leap, the car sprang forward with Ezra grinning to himself at
the consternation on her face.

‘Maybe that’ll give her somethin’ to think about, eh, my friend?’ he
asked a jay that eyed him saucily from a tree by the side of the road.

As he drove toward the village, the automobile went slower and
slower, accommodating its pace to his thoughts. Some of the events of
the day had cleared of themselves, but others were still puzzling. He
could not forget that look of startled guilt on Mrs. Bascom’s face as
she confronted him from the top of the stairs. For that matter she
had been acting queerly ever since King’s death. What could be the
reason? Did she know more than she had told? Obviously she did. But
was that knowledge guilty or----

There seemed no logical alternative, yet he refused to accept the
inevitable conclusion. It was utterly impossible to suspect Ellen
Bascom of guilt. On the other hand, it was equally impossible
to believe Sylvia or Ruel was guilty, yet facts, circumstantial
evidence, everything seemed against them.

His musing was interrupted by his arrival at the jail, but was
automatically resumed as he prepared to cook himself a hasty supper.
Again the mood was shaken off while he ate whole-heartedly, only to
return, once this ceremony had been performed and the dishes neatly
washed and put in their respective places.

‘A trunk. Now what would she be doin’ with a trunk?’

He jammed tobacco into his pipe so emphatically that it would not
draw. This necessitated cleaning it out again, refilling it, then
neatly brushing up the tobacco he had spilled in the process and
hoarding it back into the can. Darkness had fallen, but he made no
move to light the lamp on the table. He tilted back his chair and
rocked on it as he smoked and muttered aloud.

‘Say it was puttin’ somethin’ away, now.--Don’t seem logical. She
didn’t have anythin’ with her an’ she’d already been away at King’s
a couple of days. Trust Ellen B. to have done her tidyin’ before
that.--Well, then, say she was takin’ somethin’ out.--But she didn’t
bring anythin’ down as I could see.

‘Then she was lookin’ at somethin’ that was already put away there.
That’s the truth. Perhaps it was somethin’ she’d put there just
recent----’

His chair came to the floor with a thump and he remained immovable
for a full minute.

‘She was concealin’ somethin’ in her bosom that day, an’ she was
kind of guilty about it. Now to-day she was guilty-lookin’ again at
bein’ found in her own attic.--Ezra Sewell, if you ain’t on the right
track, you ain’t no weather prophet!’

With this rather mixed remark, he again relapsed into silence. It was
broken only once by another disjointed bit of philosophy. ‘Women are
peculiar, but usually they have reasons for things an’ the doin’ of
’em.’

It was quite dark when Ezra got to his feet, fumbled about in the
familiar room until he found a flashlight, tested it, then went out,
locking the door again behind him. Apparently bent on an aimless
walk, he followed the street down to the edge of the village, then
cut off into a field that ended in a pine grove. Into this he
disappeared.

Fifteen minutes later he had circled around and was approaching Mrs.
Bascom’s house from the rear, taking care to keep in the shadows
as much as possible. Gaining the doorway, the elm tree effectually
darkened the house so that no one would have seen him there--had any
one been about so late at night! His groping fingers found the key
under the mat, and with it he unlocked the door. Once inside, Ezra
turned the key in the lock and breathed easier.

‘Nice way for an officer of the law to be sneakin’ around, but all’s
fair in love an’ detectivin’,’ he mused, directing his flashlight
on the stairs up which he mounted on tiptoe with unconsciously
exaggerated caution.

He did not pause at the second floor, but continued up the short,
boxed-in flight to the attic. Here he flashed the rays of the light
about the room before advancing. It was a single large room,
covering the whole area of the house, its ceiling formed by the
gabled roof, high in the middle and sloping sharply down to meet the
floor at the sides. Pushed into these narrow spaces were two neat
rows of trunks, boxes, and bundles, the latter carefully labeled.

‘It was a trunk lid she slammed, that’s certain.’

He advanced to the first trunk and lifted its lid. A whiff of moth
balls assailed his nostrils, and the light revealed the smooth
surface of some blankets. The next one seemed full of newspaper
bundles, carefully tied with string. Their odor was of lavender. But
when he opened a third, the hand that held the light trembled for a
moment. There on top of a paper wrapped bundle lay a wisp of white
cloth, partially concealing a knife or dagger, its point stained an
ominous brown!

Ezra Sewell goggled at it for a moment in astonished silence, holding
his breath. Then, with a low whistle, he bent his light down, moving
it closer until it almost touched the knife. The cloth had a sort of
bow in one end. It was obviously part of a woman’s dress--but not
Ellen Bascom’s. The thought, as it flitted through his head, seemed
to bring a peculiar relief.--No, it was more like a younger woman’s
fancy, such as Sylvia----

His fingers slipped off the battery button and he was plunged
into darkness, but he scarcely noticed that as his mind whirled
incredulously. Then he switched on the light again, wrapped the cloth
carefully about the knife and placed it gently in his breast-pocket.
This done, he closed the lid and stole down the stairs. Unlocking
the door, he opened it a crack, listened, then slipped out. A moment
later he had replaced the key under the mat and was sauntering down
the street as if nothing more startling than an evening’s stroll had
been occupying his mind.

Once inside the jail, however, his lassitude changed to briskness.
The door shut and locked, the shades drawn tight, he lighted the lamp
and set it upon the desk. Then he withdrew the knife from his pocket,
unwrapped the cloth and studied them both carefully.

The knife seemed almost familiar.--Yes, it was the paper-cutter from
Mr. King’s desk--the one Sylvia had reported missing. The stains
were undoubtedly clots of blood--King’s blood. But there were no
finger-marks anywhere on it.--Then the murderer had worn gloves,
presumably. It all corroborated his theory of a carefully planned,
premeditated murder.

Disconnected ideas and conjectures chased through his brain.

How and where had Mrs. Bascom found it? It must have been the morning
she discovered Sylvia in the grain chute. Of course it was what she
had concealed in her dress. Then obviously she had found it just
before or after Sylvia’s rescue.--The bit of cloth, stained and cut,
was it a clue or had it been used merely as a convenient wrapper for
the weapon?--Both. It was dirty from the dust of the chute and must
have been torn from Sylvia’s dress after she was taken out.--That was
it, the knife had been in the chute with Sylvia! And that explained
why Mrs. Bascom had concealed it--because she feared it would
implicate Sylvia.

Sylvia’s peculiar actions, her insistence upon probating the will,
her strangely cold, withdrawn manner. Was she implicated, then? Did
she know about this knife, or----No! She need not have pointed out
its loss. Besides, her face, her tense, frightened look, her subtly
pleading eyes as her lips calmly insisted upon the terms of the
will that seemed the one touch of avarice and greed--surely these
were not the actions of a murderer. She was doing it all for some
purpose--acting a part. But what purpose?

Basil, who had mailed two packages suspiciously like those stolen
from the dead man’s desk--or were they stolen? Sylvia had said so,
yet she might be in league with him, might be shielding him--Again,
no. It was not consistent with her character. Her character?
Certainly that too was inconsistent----

Abruptly Ezra Sewell stood on his feet, bundled the knife into the
cloth, and put it away in the safe. Then he began to pace the floor,
even going into the corridor to stand before the empty cell, staring
at it as if to hear some answer to his silent queries from Ruel.

It was midnight when he again reseated himself at the desk and calmly
began to speak aloud, addressing his absent prisoner.

‘It don’t change things a mite after all, Ruel. It helps, in a way,
but still we don’t seem to get hold of much of anythin’ certain. Now
here’s what we know.

‘Man--or woman--he was one of the guests that night an’ knew all
about the plans beforehand. While it was dark, he sneaked out one
window an’ into the other, killed King, then hid the knife an’
covered his tracks in the confusion. My guess would be reasonable he
dropped it down that chute an’ didn’t get a chance to get it again.

‘That seems clear logical, but it don’t decide who the man was or
why he did it, eh, son?--Well, that’s what I’m after, only darned if
I see how I’m goin’ to get there. It ain’t no use accusin’ any one,
but I’ve got to do somethin’ mighty quick. There’s too much hidin’
an’ queer business to suit me. Guess, Ez, you’ll have to do some
mighty direct questionin’ one of these days.--Anyway, I hope you find
somethin’, boy, even if it does look like a wild-goose chase.’

This speech relieved Ezra’s mind somewhat, for within fifteen minutes
he was comfortably in bed and snoring wheezily.

 CHAPTER XII

 ‘IT’S GONE!’


When Ezra Sewell drove off in his car, Mrs. Bascom was so astounded
by his remarks that she could not think of a retort until he was out
of sight, and by that time more serious matters were clamoring for
consideration in her mind.

Of course he had guessed her scheme in sending Ruel off to
Boston--how, did not matter much--and Ezra’s words implied he would
not attempt to interfere with this escape. Yet this was not enough
to satisfy her. Woman-like she tried to probe further, to reason
out why he had not objected to Ruel’s release, what his attitude
toward her would be, and whether, after all, her somewhat impulsive
scheme, had been the best possible one to accomplish her ends. Ruel
Moore was methodical and dependable rather than quick-witted or
imaginative; yet he had been keen to perceive her meaning in the
hurried instructions she had given him. Did that prophesy he would be
equally clever at unraveling the mystery upon which he had been sent?
She fervently hoped so, not only for the purpose of clarifying King’s
past life and catching the murderer, but also to vindicate her own
judgment--though this latter reason she did not fully accredit at the
time.

Behind this was another startling fact. Ezra Sewell was not easily
hoodwinked, it seemed. This was the second time he had made veiled
remarks to her, such as his observations on the bulge in her waist
when she was carrying the knife concealed there. Did that signify he
knew a lot more than he had revealed, that perhaps he had not been so
inactive as he appeared? Knowing him, she was inclined to believe so,
yet he could not possibly understand even as much of the tangle as
she did.

Pondering and speculating, Mrs. Bascom shook the dust out of her
skirt with her habitual care, straightened a coil of hair that had
been jolted loose during the ride, and resolutely closed her mind to
these tantalizing speculations, as she turned toward the house. At
least she now had a means of finding out whether Sylvia was deeply
interested in Ruel or not. She, Mrs. Bascom, had presumed so until
Sylvia refused to send any message of encouragement to him, but she
was not altogether sure this should be taken as conclusive one way or
another. She had no reason for this feeling, but intuition warned her
not to make a too hasty decision.

Before mounting the stairs to Sylvia’s room, Mrs. Bascom proceeded to
the kitchen to ask a question of Martha Dobson, whom she found just
withdrawing two delicately browned loaves of bread from the oven.

‘Back again, Martha,’ she said with an appreciative sniff at the
baking. ‘Everything serene? Miss Hawley comfortable?’

‘Comfortable? I presume so,’ answered Martha, rather shortly with a
toss of her head that invited further questioning.

‘Then she hasn’t called for you, I take it?’

‘That she hasn’t--not for _me_, Ellen Bascom!’

‘What do you mean?’ inquired the latter, surprised at her emphasis.

‘Exactly what I said. She’s had no need of me, though that hasn’t
prevented her calling for a certain other person as I might mention.’

‘The doctor? She’s been sick again?’

‘Sick? Hump. Hardly! Not when she’s been a carryin’ on fit to kill
with that Stacey man--as I never could abide.’

‘Basil Stacey?’ cried Mrs. Bascom incredulously. ‘Carrying on? Where?
How?’

‘It was scandalous, I’d say.--An’ she, supposed to be sick an’ upset
over Mr. King’s death, settin’ there laughin’ an’ talkin’ just as
if nothin’ had happened. An’ with him at it too, visitin’ her right
there in her bedroom!’

Martha paused dramatically, her flour-powdered arms indignantly
akimbo, a picture of outraged dignity.

‘In her room? Talking and--and laughing, you said? But you must be
mistaken. Sylvia promised----’ She faltered, recalling that Sylvia had
only promised not to leave her room. ‘Why didn’t you stop him, forbid
him to----’

‘Forbid him, is it? As if I didn’t try to, an’ he pushin’ me aside
with a laugh, while she tells me to go away an’ leave them alone! It
only goes to show what them city folks is really like. An’ I’d always
thought she was different.’ Martha sniffed and walked away into the
pantry.

Mrs. Bascom literally ran after her.

‘Martha Dobson, you tell me just what happened. I’ve got to know.’

‘You will, quick enough.’ Martha wiped away a tear which might have
been of anger, but which was more likely of personal shame and
humility at Sylvia’s treatment of her. ‘It wasn’t ten minutes after
you left, I was goin’ up to see if she wanted anythin’. This man was
walkin’ up just ahead of me an’ she calls out to ask if it was him.
He answers it was, an’ started straight for her door, so I sidled
ahead of him to keep him out. But there he stands, lookin’ hard at
her, while she, in one of them fetchin’ wrapper things that makes
her look like a girl almost, sat smilin’ an’ sayin’, “Come right in,
Basil, I want to have a talk with you.”

‘At that I spoke up, tried to get in ahead of him to close the door
an’ bring her to her senses. An’ what does he do but push me away
with some silly joke she laughs at! I was that taken aback, before I
could move or speak again, he’d actually closed it in my face.

‘With that I pushed it open again an’ started givin’ him a piece
of my mind good an’ plain, but she frowned, stood up an’ ordered
me--ordered me out of the room! Flabbergasted, I was. I didn’t know
what I was doin’ until I found myself outside in the hall again, an’
heard him lockin’ the door on me!’

‘Locked the door--with Sylvia----’ Mrs. Bascom’s eyes widened
with horror, and she turned as if to hurry away from some utterly
unbelievable gossip.

‘Oh, he’s gone again now, never fear,’ said Martha quickly, mistaking
her movement as a determination to go upstairs and interfere herself.
‘But if you could have heard them, laughin’ an’ talkin’ as I did,
Ellen Bascom, you’d----’

‘Then you--you listened?’

‘Yes, I did, an’ I’d do it again without no pricks of conscience,’
Martha justified herself stoutly.

‘Of course. It was your duty. But what were they saying?’

‘I couldn’t hear very well, especially at first, they talked so quiet
an’ serious like, he a-holdin’ her hand an’ bendin’ close to her.’

‘But the other room--why didn’t you go in there by that door?’
questioned Mrs. Bascom, unaware that she might be revealing more than
she intended by this knowledge of a better listening post.

‘I did, but it was shut tight an’ I was afraid if I opened it, he’d
come an’ lock it too. Well, after a bit, this Stacey let go her
hand an’ drew up a chair. I was a-lookin’ through the keyhole, you
see. An’ then they just began laughin’ an’ smilin’ away at each
other as if--as if he was her best friend,’ Martha finished lamely,
suppressing a stronger phrase that obviously rose to her lips.

‘How long did you stay there?’

‘Till he went--more than half an hour later, an’ me so stiff with
kneelin’ there my legs almost cracked. He walked straight out of the
house an’ hasn’t been back since, so I came down here without a word
to her. An’ that’s all I know, though it’s more than enough, I’d say
to any one as asked me.’

The two women stared silently at each other, each picturing that
scene which Martha had just described. At last Mrs. Bascom spoke in a
low, hurried voice.

‘You did exactly right, Martha, both in listening and telling me.
It--it must be as the doctor feared--her mind is affected.’

‘Did he say that? You mean she might not know--might not realize
what she was doin’?’ whispered Martha, eager to accept any plausible
explanation of Sylvia’s rebuffs.

Mrs. Bascom nodded slowly, though she was far from certain this was
the whole truth.

‘I think we’d better keep this to ourselves, not tell any one, don’t
you?’ she suggested.

‘I won’t tell a soul.--An’ I hope it’s so, that her mind is----I
mean, it would be terrible, only it seems as if it was the best thing
to happen----Oh, I’m that upset an’ confused I don’t know what I’m
sayin’.’

Tears trickled down her kindly, wrinkled face and for a moment Mrs.
Bascom soothed her like a child, then hurried away down the hall. But
her steps retarded as she mounted the stairs, paused on the landing
part-way up to adjust her mind to this new complication.

Considered of itself, this meeting between Sylvia and Basil would
have appeared as unaccountable as it had to Martha, yet for all
its strangeness, Mrs. Bascom sensed certain things in view of the
previous conversation she had overheard between them. That their
serious talk had been concerned with the matter of the stolen papers
and Basil’s subsequent threat, Mrs. Bascom had not a doubt, and that
they had arrived at some amicable agreement was quite clear from the
laughter and talk that had followed. Yet how could they have agreed,
arranged a plan between them? Basil held the upper hand, was not
likely to have receded from it. Had he merely extended the time limit
of his threat? Improbable, for in that instance, Sylvia would still
have been perturbed, scarcely free to laugh.

Then there was only one other assumption possible--that Sylvia had
given in to him. Incredible--impossible! Surely she was in love with
Ruel. It seemed obvious--their actions previous to King’s death,
their being together so much. And yet Sylvia was so strange, had
seemed to ignore Ruel’s imprisonment.

There was a way of finding out the answers to some of these
speculations, and Mrs. Bascom hesitated no longer to take it. But
her determination suffered a temporary setback when she arrived at
Sylvia’s room. Peering through the door that stood ajar, she saw
Sylvia was fast asleep in her chair, so Mrs. Bascom postponed her
questioning, entered on tiptoe and stood at her side, studying her
face in an attempt at cool impartiality which broke down almost
immediately.

There is something disarmingly frank and innocent about the face of
a sleeper. The closing of the eyes at once takes away the animation
and subtlety which give depth to the expression, while the relaxing
of the muscles in sleep robs a face of any artificiality or pose,
moulds it into those characteristic lines which may be controlled
or repressed during consciousness. So with Sylvia’s quiet face,
there was only a pathetic droop to her bowed lips and an appealing
youthfulness that demanded sympathy, not condemnation. It could never
be the face of a murderer or a cold-blooded plotter, Mrs. Bascom
decided again, her sympathies swallowing up her resentment of a few
moments ago.

Retiring to her own room, she freshened up for dinner, then sat by
the window calmly reviewing the facts as she knew them, until her
watch pointed to six-thirty. A glance in at Sylvia’s room disclosed
she was just awakening, so Mrs. Bascom entered and cheerfully asked
what she would like to eat. Sylvia returned her smile quite naturally
for an instant, then abruptly sighed and seemed to withdraw within
herself once more.

‘Don’t bother, Mrs. B. I shall hobble down as soon as I slip on a
dress,’ she said with forced briskness.

‘You’d much better stay up here, Sylvia dear. It’s no bother at all
to bring you a tray.’

‘Thank you, no. I’m not an invalid any longer. Besides, I--I want to
be with people and listen to conversation again instead of being all
alone.’

Mrs. Bascom realized it was useless to argue, so returned to her room
and puttered about for a few minutes until Sylvia should be ready,
intending to assist her down the stairs. Her offer, however, was
stoutly declined.

‘I can manage quite well, Mrs. B., if I just go slowly. You’ve all
been babying me too much lately, and it’s time to call a halt.’

The others had already assembled when the two women arrived in the
lower hall and Basil came forward immediately, linking his arm
through Sylvia’s and supporting her weight quite easily. Mrs. Bascom
noticed that Sylvia did not refuse his help nor draw away from him.

The meal proved to be rather a labored one as far as conversation
was concerned. Judge Brant and Dr. New were somewhat weary and
uncommunicative, replying only when directly addressed. The doctor,
moreover, seemed to be watching Sylvia closely, obviously surprised
and interested by her attitude toward Basil. These two were talking
and laughing together, rather ignoring the others. Their conversation
was light and aimless, Basil exerting himself to the utmost to
entertain her, Sylvia encouraging him, though at times her animation
grew forced, her interest merely a veneer.

Following Mr. King’s custom, the two women left the men to their
smoking after the meal, but Sylvia decided not to go to her room yet,
insisting she wanted to check over all the papers in the desk before
retiring. Mrs. Bascom accompanied her to the den and sat down to read
the local news while Sylvia worked. An hour passed in which Mrs.
Bascom did little reading, but Sylvia worked in absorbed silence,
sorting the papers, taking notes, and making calculations.

At last Mrs. Bascom put aside the newspaper and, making an excuse of
going to her room for something, ascended the stairs, her mind made
up to write down and arrange a résumé of the details and discoveries
she had made in an effort to clarify matters.

She had moved quite silently and as her hand was stretched out to
touch the doorknob of her room, she abruptly became rigid, listening
to a faint sound from within. Somebody was in there, had just opened
one of the bureau drawers!

Cautiously she pushed on the door, which was unlatched, until she
could peer in, regardless of the fact that she might be discovered.

Against the dim outline of the window was silhouetted a man’s
figure, stooping over the bureau. As she watched, he switched on a
flashlight, holding it inside the drawer so as to concentrate its
rays, and began to poke an inquiring finger among the neatly folded
garments which it contained. The reflected light was not bright
enough for her to make out the man’s features, but he seemed to be
of large frame, and his fingers were short and thick--like those of
Judge Brant!

Unsuccessful in his search, he treated the other drawers in the same
manner, entered the closet, even rifled her suit-case. When he turned
off his light after this, Mrs. Bascom realized he must come out into
the hall in another moment, so stepped aside from the door, waiting
to challenge him when he opened it.

Footsteps crossed the room, stopped, then continued, but toward the
communicating door to Sylvia’s room! Mrs. Bascom waited until they
had passed over the threshold, then, without hesitation, dodged into
her room and continued her spying.

The same procedure was followed in Sylvia’s room. Each drawer was
searched, but apparently without result. The closet yielded nothing.
Then the figure turned to the writing-table and the light flashed on
and off as it was scrutinized. Suddenly the man sighed or whistled
in a low tone and, before she realized what he was doing, swiftly
crossed the room to the hall door and disappeared.

Mrs. Bascom scurried to her own door, opening it just in time to see
him enter a room farther down the hall. It was Judge Brant’s room!

She thought rapidly. She must make certain who this man was. On the
other hand, if it should prove to be the judge himself, she must have
some ready excuse for knocking on his door as she did not intend to
accuse him or reveal her knowledge of his prowling yet--not until she
could think out some reason for it.

She was saved all this, however, for at that moment the doctor’s
voice from below called the judge lustily. Drawing back, Mrs.
Bascom saw the door open and the judge himself hurry out and down
the stairs. As soon as he had disappeared, she went to his room and
knocked. There was no answer. She opened the door and boldly turned
on the light, but as she had expected, the room was empty. On the
bureau lay a flashlight!

Then it was the judge, beyond a doubt. But why had he searched the
two rooms? What was he after?

Mrs. Bascom returned to her room and sat down to ponder these two
questions. Fear gripped her, a weakness that robbed her of all her
self-assurance at one sweep. She had felt quite capable of playing
the detective until now, had rather gloated over the knowledge she
was concealing from Ezra Sewell. Yet nothing seemed to clarify under
her handling, everything was becoming more confused and complex.
Every one seemed to be acting queerly, unnaturally, concealing some
act guiltily--Sylvia, Basil, and now Judge Brant. Would it be Dr. New
next?--The thought sent an unaccountable chill down her spine.

Footsteps began to ascend the stairs slowly. She jumped to her feet,
her heart racing. Some one was approaching stealthily, testing each
step before placing his weight upon it. She shrank back with eyes
fixed upon the door, expecting to see it slowly open. Step by step,
softly, the person mounted, reached the top, then----

Mrs. Bascom shivered and tried to laugh as her body relaxed so
suddenly that she would have fallen if she had not caught the
footboard of the bed. It was only Sylvia, limping and slow!--Strength
raced back into her body and with it a new determination to finish
what she had set about to do; namely, confront Sylvia with some, if
not all, of the facts she knew.

Mrs. Bascom smiled grimly to herself, thinking of her foolish fears
of a moment ago.--Confess defeat so easily, run to Ezra Sewell with
her tale--and probably have him scoff at it as all imagination----No,
not she! Without waiting to knock, she walked into Sylvia’s room,
resolved not to leave it again until certain pertinent questions had
been asked and answered.

‘You’ve finished your work over the papers? Nothing new discovered, I
suppose?’

‘No. There were some things to be acted upon at once. I sorted them
out and will attend to them to-morrow,’ murmured Sylvia abstractedly.
‘There’s so much to be done. I’m glad--it will keep me busy, help
me not to think.’ She spoke in such a low tone that her words were
scarcely audible, but Mrs. Bascom did not miss them.

‘Exactly. And now that you’ve recovered so nicely, Sylvia, I think
it’s time we had a good talk over matters. While you were still ill
from the shock, you said such unaccountable things that----’

‘But I have nothing to retract,’ interrupted Sylvia, a note of
weariness in her voice. ‘Please let’s not discuss that now, Mrs. B.
I’m so tired of it all.’

‘Not too tired to work--or chatter like a magpie with Basil!’

Mrs. Bascom’s shot went home. Sylvia winced and dropped her eyes, a
slow flush gathering in her cheeks. She clenched and unclenched her
fingers nervously; her lips opened to respond, then closed again with
a snap and her mouth set into a firm, straight line. Mrs. Bascom
waited, but when she understood Sylvia did not intend to speak, she
did so herself.

‘Sylvia Hawley, if you don’t try the patience of a saint--and I’m not
one. What’s come over you? You haven’t been acting like yourself at
all. Even Dr. New, who hasn’t known you well before, has an excuse
for thinking your mind is--well, upset.’

She hesitated, hoping this hint might elicit some reply. Sylvia
glanced up quickly, and there was evidence of a struggle going on in
her mind, but she did not speak.

‘Don’t you understand I only want to help you? Don’t you see your
position is rather a precarious one?’

‘You mean--I’m suspected of--of some part in this murder?’ Sylvia
whispered.

‘Yes, you are--but not by me. Oh, I can help you, if you’ll only let
me. I know----’

‘No--no, you don’t--you can’t!’ exploded Sylvia; then added with
pathetic pleading, ‘You said you’d trust me. I told you before I must
go on--alone. Please--please don’t ask me anything more. It’s hard
enough----’

‘Hard, is it?’ Mrs. Bascom’s tone shifted from sympathy to scorn as
she deliberately taunted Sylvia to make her reply. ‘I suppose I’m to
believe you are suffering while you laugh and talk--yes, flirt with
Basil Stacey! And Ruel is shut up in jail, accused of murder!’

Sylvia shrank back and hid her face with one hand, making a gesture
with the other as if to push Mrs. Bascom’s words away.

‘I thought you had some feelings, the ordinary decency of a woman at
least,’ continued Mrs. Bascom. ‘So did we all of us--up till that
awful evening. But now----What do you suppose people can believe,
except that you are somehow guilty--don’t honestly care what happens
as long as you get your employer’s money! It’s not hard to see why
some would rather see you behind the bars than Ruel!’

Sylvia jerked her face from her hands. Her reply was unexpected.

‘Then why don’t they make the exchange?’

Mrs. Bascom jumped to her feet in astonishment. The prospect of being
put into prison, far from frightening Sylvia, seemed to be what she
most desired! It was unaccountable, unless----She clicked her teeth
abruptly. What a blind fool she had been not to see it all before!

But her tone showed no change of emotion as she continued her probing
mercilessly.

‘I think perhaps he’d prefer to stay where he is than to see the way
you are carrying on. When he hears how you and Basil----’

‘No--no. He must not hear that!’ Sylvia was on her feet, her face
ashen and distorted with emotion. She clenched her fists and leaned
toward Mrs. Bascom, her words fairly tumbling over one another in
a burst of passion. ‘Swear to me you’ll never tell Ruel!--It isn’t
true. He’d never believe you. I--I won’t have him told.--If you love
me--if you have one speck of respect for me or ever had, you’ll never
tell him.’

‘And why shouldn’t I tell him what I saw with my own eyes?’

‘It’s not the truth, I tell you,’ almost hysterically Sylvia shouted
the words. ‘I hate Basil. I----’

‘You think I’ll believe that when I--when we all saw you act that way
toward him?’

‘You must. There was a reason.’

‘Well?’

‘I can’t tell you.--But promise me you won’t tell Ruel?’

‘Not just yet, Sylvia Hawley, because he’s beyond telling. He has
escaped from the jail and disappeared!’

Sylvia swayed backward as if from a blow. Her lips formed Mrs.
Bascom’s words, but she could not speak them aloud. Her eyes fixed on
the other woman’s face, agonized, as she tottered back and fell into
her chair gasping for breath.

‘Then you do love Ruel?’ questioned Mrs. Bascom seizing her chance.

Sylvia nodded passionately, her defense overthrown.

‘You’re trying to shield him, is that it? You think that by casting
suspicion upon yourself, you----Sylvia, do you mean you suspect
Basil? You’re trying to lead him on, flirt with him and find out what
he knows?’

Dumbly Sylvia nodded again.

‘But that’s so foolish. You’ll only get yourself into trouble without
helping Ruel at all. You must tell me--everything.’

‘I--I knew you’d say that. It’s why--I couldn’t--tell you.’

‘But, my dear, don’t you see now that I can help you? You’re only
tangling things up for us all. I know more than you think, and I can
see the damage you are doing. I know that Basil was the thief the
other night, I know he threatened you, saw you enter the judge’s
room. I know you lied about the will. I found the dagger in the grain
chute with you and have been hiding it----’

Mrs. Bascom was stunned into silence by the appalling effect her
last words had produced. Sylvia seemed about to faint; her eyes were
glassy, her breath coming in faint gasps. But she gathered herself
together, raised herself to her feet by sheer will power.

‘I’ll tell you--everything--show you----’

She took a step, stretching out her hand toward Mrs. Bascom for
help and support. The latter sprang to her side and assisted her to
the desk where she fumbled under the blotter, at first slowly, then
frantically.

‘It’s gone!’ she wailed suddenly, and leaned back against Mrs. Bascom
heavily.

 CHAPTER XIII

 CONFUSION


Mrs. Bascom gently assisted Sylvia to her chair without asking what
it was that had disappeared from her desk and so distressed her.

‘There, now, you’re all upset again, my dear. I’m going to put you to
bed at once and----’

With a perfunctory rap, Dr. New opened the door abruptly and came
toward them.

‘I was passing down the hall when I heard Miss Hawley cry out,’ he
explained, looking sharply at Sylvia. ‘You’re not well at all, are
you?’

He took her wrist automatically to feel her pulse.

‘She’s tired and upset about something,’ said Mrs. Bascom. ‘I was
just suggesting bed for her.’

‘See that she goes there,’ confirmed the doctor with a meaning glance
at Mrs. Bascom. ‘I have a little medicine with me--some soothing
drops. I’ll get them.’

He hurried away, shaking his head to himself, and Mrs. Bascom bent
down to unfasten the straps of Sylvia’s pumps. She had just completed
this task when the doctor returned with a small bottle and drew
her aside, ostensibly to give her directions for its use. To these
he added a whispered caution: ‘Don’t let her talk. It might prove
dangerous for her to become more excited than she already is. This
drug will soothe her nerves and make her sleep.’ Mrs. Bascom nodded
silently, and he again withdrew.

Listlessly Sylvia allowed herself to be helped into bed, but once
there she refused to lie back or take the medicine, sitting erect and
tense.

‘I must tell you everything before I go to sleep, Mrs. B.’

‘To-morrow, my dear, not to-night. The doctor said----’

‘I don’t care what he said. I couldn’t sleep anyway unless I get
this off my mind. I’ve worried and muddled along trying to do what
seemed----’

‘Sylvia, you simply mustn’t talk now. These drops will help you to
forget it and sleep, and to-morrow I’ll listen.’

‘I won’t take them until after I’ve told you, Mrs. B.!’ decided
Sylvia stubbornly. ‘I don’t like Dr. New anyway. He stares at me
peculiarly, is forever asking me questions and telling me to rest.
Of course I’m tired--who wouldn’t be after going through what has
happened to me? But you must listen now and help me as you said you
would, Mrs. B. dear.’

Sylvia clutched her hand, but not hysterically. Her voice was
steadying itself with each word she spoke, and her eyes seemed clear
and normal.

Mrs. Bascom made a quick decision. The doctor was mistaken in his
diagnosis of Sylvia’s trouble, from what the latter had just said,
seemed to think she was in need of close observation. This was
partly due to his ignorance of Sylvia’s exaggerated mental strain,
partly, thought Mrs. Bascom, to her own deliberately misleading
hints. Moreover, if confession was good for the soul, it was also
relieving to the mind, and Sylvia would rest far better with a clear
conscience. Hence Mrs. Bascom did not try to dissuade her further.

‘There’s so much to tell,’ began Sylvia, instinctively dropping
her voice to a low murmur as she felt Mrs. Bascom’s willingness to
listen. ‘Every word of what I told you after you found me there in
the grain chute, was true--every word. And you said you found a
dagger--a knife in it, too?--But I thought you told me----’

‘That was after I went to look at your request,’ interrupted Mrs.
Bascom to justify her apparent falsehood. ‘I truthfully said there
was nothing in the chute then. There was not, because I had already
removed the knife and hidden it in my dress just after finding you.
It was the paper-cutter you missed from Mr. King’s study.’

‘His own paper-cutter!’ echoed Sylvia. Her eyes grew moist with
sincere grief, but she winked back the tears resolutely. ‘It was
after I learned the details of his death that I formed my mad plan.
I _tried_ to be suspected, tried to act so queerly they would blame
me and let Ruel free. Don’t you see, it means everything to him, his
freedom, whereas for me----His farm, his success, depends upon his
own hands, in a way, and now was just such a critical time for him.
Besides, I--we liked each other and I wanted to help, to spare him if
I could.’

Mrs. Bascom murmured comfortingly, ‘I understand your reasons, my
dear. But it was a mad scheme.’

‘I know--now. I was afraid to confide in you, sure you’d try to
dissuade or actually prevent me from carrying it through. It was
hard, but I made myself do it--for his sake. Some things conspired
in my favor, others complicated my plans.--I lied about the will. It
seemed a sure way of drawing suspicion to me. I knew Mr. King had
meant to leave me something--he’d told me so just the day before,
said he was drawing up a new will. But I’ll tell you about that in a
minute.

‘The night after you found me, I lay in bed thinking and determined
to get that wrong will from the judge and destroy it. If I could do
this without being seen, I thought I could confess to the theft or
something, but refuse to give up the paper, thus involving myself
even deeper. Just as I was trying to scheme a way to accomplish this,
I heard the commotion downstairs. It seemed providential. After the
men had gone down, I hobbled into the judge’s room and was searching
his pockets when the pain in my ankle made me feel faint. I was
afraid of losing consciousness there in his room, so left without
obtaining the will. On the way back to my room, Basil saw me. Later
on he told me so and threatened----’

‘I know all about that. I listened, heard what you both said from my
room. Perhaps it was wrong, but I thought the end justified----’

‘I’m glad you did.’ Sylvia squeezed Mrs. Bascom’s hand in lieu of
further words. ‘Then you know all that without my telling you. Well,
the next day I discovered what this supposed thief had taken, as I
suspected. It was a package of stocks or securities that Basil had
delivered to Mr. King the day before his death. I guessed that he’d
taken them again--Basil, I mean--and that made me suspicious of him.
I began to recall other little things. Mr. King had trusted Basil
very freely until recently, but something--some business deal--had
gone wrong and I think he had begun to suspect Basil of poor judgment
if not--well, deliberate manipulation of funds. These stocks had
something to do with it all, I know.

‘When I left Basil’s office to accept Mr. King’s position, there had
been other things I tried to forget, irregularities I half suspected,
but never proved, things he said to me----Oh, I might as well confess
it all. He had proposed to me and proved beastly, intolerable when I
rejected him. That was why I was only too glad to get away. I tried
to hint to Mr. King, but he did not understand, seemed so sure of his
own business acumen that I weakly did not press it. Besides, I did
not care to drag in my personal affairs. So I treated Basil amicably
for Mr. King’s sake.

‘But when Basil threatened me, I saw something more behind his move.
Of course he was trying to force me to marry him, that I realized at
once, but I staved him off with half excuses, determined to play the
part of relenting slowly so as to find out if there was anything else
he knew--if he had been involved in more than theft. That was why I
invited him to my room here, tried to flirt with him and played up to
him as I did at dinner. You do understand now?’

‘Of course I do. I never did suspect you of anything wrong. I thought
you were foolish and did not realize what you were doing. I was hurt
at your attitude toward me, too, but now I understand your motive.’

They were silent for a moment, each thinking her own thoughts.

‘It has all been useless too. Either he does not know anything about
the--the murder, or else he is too sly. I’ve found out nothing from
him.’

‘This thing you missed from the desk just now, what was it and why
did it upset you?’ prompted Mrs. Bascom.

‘It was Mr. King’s latest will, the one he had drawn up to include a
legacy for me. I found it with some papers in his desk and slipped it
under that blotter yesterday.’

‘Was it signed?’

‘By his name only, not witnessed. But I think I can explain that.
Somehow it must have been a mistake, don’t you see? It was the one
he had meant to execute and have witnessed by those people, but it
must have gotten mixed with the fake will he had planned to use in
the play. I’m sure it must have happened that way. They signed the
wrong one, don’t you see? I meant to write those three people and ask
exactly what happened--see if there was any chance of an error.’

‘I’ll do it for you--to-night,’ said Mrs. Bascom eagerly. ‘Better
yet, perhaps, I can telephone in the morning. I’m sure you must be
right.--Now, Sylvia dear, lie down and sleep. What you’ve just told
me----’

‘Wait. I’ve told you everything, but I too want to know----Where is
Ruel?’ Her voice broke pleadingly.

‘Don’t worry about him, Sylvia,’ smiled Mrs. Bascom, and proceeded
to explain how she had prevailed upon him to ‘escape,’ as well as
her reason for being so curt in her manner of revealing this before.
‘He’s safely in Boston by now.’

‘Did he know--did he think that I----’

‘No. I gave him a message from you--made up one such as I thought
you’d really want to send him,’ murmured Mrs. Bascom with a faint
flush in her cheeks as she hastened over this intimate revelation. ‘I
firmly believe Mr. King used to live in Boston, and Ruel is going to
do his best to trace him there. It might help to establish a motive,
turn up something in his past, you see.’

‘Boston--Boston,’ whispered Sylvia thoughtfully. ‘Mr. King spoke of
it, seemed familiar with it as with many other cities. Yet he never
referred to his past to me any more than to his other friends. It may
be----Oh, I hope he finds--something. But what makes you think it was
Boston?’

Mrs. Bascom explained her theory as derived from the reading of
King’s books, and this time she found a willing convert to her
deductions. Again she counseled sleep and arose to get the medicine
when Sylvia stopped her for a final question.

‘Tell me about the paper-knife.’

Mrs. Bascom did so, ending with the details of how it had cut and
stained Sylvia’s dress.

‘Then that was the touch on my shoulder,’ said the latter with a
shudder. ‘And that was what slipped down my back, cutting my dress
and falling to the bottom of the chute. It was the--the murderer,
hiding it.’

‘Probably, but you mustn’t think of that now.--Here, drink this and
go to sleep. To-morrow we’ll talk it over again and plan out a
better campaign.’

‘You mean to tell Ezra Sewell? I’d trust him. I wanted to tell him
before, only that would have spoiled my scheme.’ She swallowed the
medicine and sank back on her pillow gratefully. ‘You’ve no idea how
tired yet rested I feel, now that I’ve told you and you understand.
You’ve been so kind and dear to me, Mrs. B.’

‘I’ve been a blind fool,’ snorted the latter, running her fingers
soothingly over Sylvia’s smooth forehead.

Sylvia shook her head in denial, smiled, then relaxed like a tired
child and was soon peacefully sleeping.

When Mrs. Bascom had turned out the light and opened the window, she
retired to her own room, leaving the door between wide open. Slowly
she undressed in the dark and got into bed. But as she lay there,
wide awake, questions battled in her brain for answers. Why had Judge
Brant stolen that unsigned will? Was Basil merely a thief, or did he
know something sinister about the murder? Had Dr. New been passing
the door as he claimed, or was this more than coincidence? If he had
been deliberately listening----

Idly her own thought of earlier in the evening returned. Basil, Judge
Brant, and now Dr. New--all acting strangely! Then another question
superseded all the others. Should she take Ezra into her confidence
now? It was her plain duty to do so, yet----Why was it she hesitated?
Why did she treat him as she did, unreasonably, belligerently? She
liked him, appreciated his common sense, his slow but sure methods,
and yet----

Sleep put an end to her cogitations abruptly.

When she awoke the next morning, her first thoughts were for Sylvia,
but one glance sufficed to show she was still asleep, so Mrs. Bascom
dressed and went down to breakfast. The men were waiting for her.

‘How is Miss Hawley now?’ asked Dr. New as they took their places at
the table.

‘She was still asleep when I came down.’

‘What, has she been ill again?’ inquired Basil rather offhandedly.

‘She suffered a nervous attack last evening. Possibly the strain of
being up to dinner.’ Dr. New frowned slightly at Mrs. Bascom as if
blaming her for this. ‘I trust it was nothing worse.--You gave her
the medicine--immediately--as I directed?’

‘Oh, she insisted upon talking for a few minutes first, and seemed so
determined I let her relieve her mind without trying to cross her,’
answered Mrs. Bascom quite truthfully, if not wholly accurately.

‘Eh? What’s that? How long did you let her talk?’ The idea disturbed
the doctor markedly.

‘Only a few minutes. I’m sure it did no harm,’ answered Mrs. Bascom
with calm self-assurance.

‘I’m not so sure, but I hope so.’

Mrs. Bascom skillfully changed the subject at this time. She had no
doubts upon the subject, rather thought less of the doctor for his
manifest error of diagnosis and consequent qualms.

Halfway through the meal, Basil Stacey was summoned to the telephone,
and when he returned he was smiling broadly.

‘I’m all flattered! That was a request from none other than Saint
Peter, Sewell’s right-hand sleuth, for a _tête-à-tête_ with his
master at the jail this morning.’

‘Better think over all your sins,’ laughed the doctor.

‘Luckily, my conscience is clear,’ retorted Basil.

Mrs. Bascom bit her lip in an effort to choke back a retort to
this remark, but an interruption by the judge saved her from any
embarrassing revelation.

‘I’m not so sure any one has so much to fear from Sewell. You know,
I’ve been wondering. That man Sewell seems to get nowhere on this
case. He impresses me curiously, and I’m considered quite a judge of
character.’

‘But a rotten punster!’ added Basil.

‘It wasn’t intended, I assure you. I’m serious about this, Mr.
Stacey.--As I was saying, two or three times he’s seemed quite quick
and keen under that drawling manner of his; yet at others, he’s dull
and stupid. He’s not a man of action, and what we need is some one
to _do_ something. Now I’m dropping down to Boston this morning on a
little personal matter, and I’ve almost decided to hire a detective,
one really trained to the profession. What do you think of the idea,
Doctor?’

‘Not a bad one, as I’ve already thought of it myself! But I’d hardly
call it a good one either. Personally, I believe Sewell is doing all
that’s possible--which, after all, isn’t much, I’ll admit. But what
could any one else do? There’s not a chance of finding any so-called
clues now. He’s questioned all of us again and again, found out all
there is to be learned. In other words, tell me what use a detective
could be and I’ll be only too glad to help you get one here.’

‘Well, detectives are trained men, specialists in the science of
reading and interpreting people and motives.’

‘People--such as ourselves? Then you suspect one of us?’

‘No, no, I don’t mean that.’

‘And motives, when we know so little about King after all,’ continued
Dr. New.

‘A detective might find out something about him,’ blustered Judge
Brant.

‘How? Where would he start?’

‘That would be up to him.’

‘Yes, but if we, his closest friends, know nothing, who would? If he
has deliberately concealed his past--as it rather seems he has--then
don’t you suppose he’s done a thorough job of it?’

‘One thing, though,’ interrupted Basil. ‘I believe King was once a
detective, and that ought to help.’

‘No, Basil, I don’t see it. Even granted that--which I doubt--it
would be rather difficult and expensive, not to mention foolish, to
search the whole United States for some record, say twenty years old
or so which might mention King. Besides, was that his name--then?--Of
course, Judge, do as you see fit, and I’ll back you, but frankly this
idea doesn’t appeal to me--not without something to work on.--What do
you say, Mrs. Bascom?’

‘I say, leave it to Ezra Sewell,’ she answered succinctly.

‘The “ayes” have it. Leave it to Ez!’ chuckled Basil.

The judge capitulated. As soon as breakfast was over, he left in his
automobile which was already at the door, intending to take the first
train to Boston. Basil went with him to the village to answer his
summons to the jail, the doctor settled down on the piazza to read,
and Mrs. Bascom peeked in on Sylvia, then left her asleep to help
Martha about the house.

Ten o’clock came and there seemed nothing left to do, so Mrs. Bascom
decided the time had come to act. As Sylvia showed no signs of
stirring, she left her in Martha’s charge and started out to the
village to make her delayed confession to Ezra Sewell.

‘Going in town?’ asked Dr. New with a yawn as she came out of the
house.

‘Yes, for a little while. There are a couple of things I want to see
about. Miss Hawley seems to be resting comfortably and Martha will be
on hand when she wakes. Will you be here too?’

‘Probably, or not far away. I may take a short walk, but I’ll look in
on her before I go.--By the way, if you should see Sewell, tell him
I shall probably drop down later on in the day to see him. I rather
enjoy talking with him, you know, Mrs. Bascom. His conversations are
always illuminating.’

‘So I’ve found,’ she smiled cryptically as she walked away toward the
road.

It was an almost cloudless, warm day yet not too hot to be
uncomfortable so she started off briskly. But as she drew nearer the
village, her pace slackened.

The prospect of her interview with Ezra was not altogether a
pleasant one. Her mind was made up--she would not funk it--yet
somehow she felt loath to do her obvious duty. It was not through
any doubts or fears that he would suspect Sylvia after he heard the
whole truth; it was not for any qualms about involving Basil or Judge
Brant. Rather it was a purely personal dislike to admit defeat for
herself, to ask for his help in carrying out what she had planned to
do herself. Yet now this task had grown too involved and complicated
for one person.

Mentally she reviewed what she was about to say, arranged the details
chronologically and neatly so that they might be presented in the
briefest, clearest form. She wished that Ruel’s mission were already
completed--and successfully. That at least would be a point in her
favor!

Drawing near her house, she decided to obtain the knife and give it
to Ezra, thus making a clean sweep of the whole affair. She walked
up the path, stooping to pull up an encroaching weed here and there.
Weeds are such handy things to have--when they may be used as an
excuse to delay an unpleasant duty a little longer!

‘Ellen Bascom, you quit tryin’ to fool yourself,’ she suddenly
remarked out loud, after wasting several minutes at this task. ‘It
don’t do you any good stalling and putting this off. March, now! Do
your duty like a woman!’

With this injunction, she went straight to the door, unlocked it,
then sniffed audibly and paused. A faint odor like that of stale
tobacco seemed to reach her nostrils for an instant, only to be
drowned out by the fresh breeze that swept in with her.

Tossing her head, she mounted the stairs to the attic and threw back
the lid of the trunk where she had put the paper-cutter. She stared.
It was not there! In a panic she stepped back a pace and ran her eye
along the other closed lids.

‘Am I losing my memory? I could have taken my affidavit it was in
that one.’

Quickly she opened one after another, removed the top layer of each,
felt down the sides, but in vain. The knife, her one bit of tangible
evidence, was gone!--That whiff of tobacco in the hall--it had been
real, not an hallucination after all. Some one had been in her house!

Mechanically she closed the trunks again and hurried down the stairs
into her bedroom. In the doorway she stopped short, involuntarily
passing her hand across her eyes to clear her vision of some
impossible sight. But it was real! The room was all in confusion.
Drawers were pulled open, their contents scattered about ruthlessly;
the closet was a jumbled mass of garments, even the bedclothes were
mussed and pulled aside.

From room to room she wandered with increasing fear and
consternation. Each had been searched with no effort to replace what
had been moved about. The whole house had been thoroughly ransacked.

Mrs. Bascom did not touch anything--she was too dazed. Without
realizing what she was doing, she walked out the door, locked it,
and, clutching the key in her hand, started rapidly down the road,
unmindful which way her steps led her as she groped mentally for some
explanation of this theft.

That it placed her in a decidedly compromising position was
obvious. Whether or not Ezra would have forgiven her for temporarily
concealing vital evidence no longer mattered. Now she had lost it,
was technically an accessory or something, she thought--might even be
subject to imprisonment. Perhaps the murderer had taken the knife. In
that case, she had only destroyed another chance of finding him and
bringing him to justice.

Wait! There was only one person who had any reason to suspect her
of concealing anything, and that person was Ezra himself! But even
if he had guessed the truth, why should he have taken this means of
securing the knife? Surely he would have first challenged her with
his knowledge. Again she could not imagine him leaving everything in
such a mess. No, it was some one else, some one who----

Dr. New! He had been at Sylvia’s door listening! It was just before
Sylvia had called out that Mrs. Bascom had mentioned finding and
concealing the knife, and he must have heard this--searched her house
as the logical place for concealment. He had found it then, but why?
Did he believe her guilty, or was he merely going to turn it over to
Ezra? He had spoken of his intention to call upon Sewell that day,
also he was yawning and sleepy. That was it, he had searched her
house last night! But her position was still more dangerous now. She
must see Ezra at once--explain everything before the doctor told his
story.

Her decision made, she looked up and found herself at the entrance to
King’s drive. In her abstraction, she had walked directly away from
the jail where she had meant to go.

She hesitated, decided to look in on Sylvia, then return to the
village once more, even though this would mean missing her lunch. The
house was silent and apparently deserted, so she ran up the stairs.
Just as she reached Sylvia’s door, however, it moved open and Dr. New
confronted her gravely.

‘I’m glad you’re back, Mrs. Bascom. You may be needed.’

‘What do you mean? Sylvia’s not--worse?’

He nodded silently, drawing aside so that she might look into
the room. There on the bed lay Sylvia, her eyes closed, her face
strangely waxen and lifeless.

‘Dr. New--tell me!’ gasped Mrs. Bascom, thoroughly alarmed.

‘She is in a coma, has not roused since you went away and I
cannot wake her. Her pulse and respiration are subnormal, and her
reaction----’

‘Tell me in plain words,’ interrupted Mrs. Bascom, unconsciously
grasping his arm for support.

‘There is little I can say. I was worried after what you said of
her having talked against my orders, and when she did not awaken,
my fears seemed justified. A shock--some sort of nervous reaction,
perhaps, I cannot tell as yet. But those words of hers may have been
her last for hours--perhaps days. There is nothing we can do now but
wait.’

Mrs. Bascom paled, trembled, and clung to him.

‘You don’t think----’

‘I’m not unnecessarily alarmed yet, nor, in a way, surprised. It is
merely a question of time and complete rest, I think.--But do not
take it to heart. It was not your fault. She must be kept quiet,
that’s all.--Did you give Sewell my message?’

‘I didn’t see him,’ gasped Mrs. Bascom weakly. ‘What--what can I do?’

‘Nothing. Rest, in case you are needed later.’

 CHAPTER XIV

 THE PRODIGAL RETURNS


Inspector Sewell was late in keeping his appointment with Basil that
morning; in fact, he almost forgot it entirely. He had stepped out
for a brief walk and had become so absorbed in speculations over
the murder that he had gone much farther than he intended. When he
arrived at the jail, however, he found Basil cheerfully talking with
Pete, his assistant.

‘Sorry to be so late, Stacey,’ apologized Sewell with a friendly nod.
‘I kind of lost track of time for a bit, I guess.’

‘Hard on the trail, eh?--Well, I’ve been well entertained by Pete
here,’ grinned Basil.

‘Good. Then you ain’t really missed me much. Pete’s some talker, I
know, an’ some folks say he’s my only rival in that line.--But that
ain’t neither hare nor hound. Seein’ as I kept you waitin’ so long,
I’ll just ask you to stay to dinner with me to make up, if you’d like
to.’

‘Decidedly. It’ll be the first time I ever ate in or accepted an
invitation to a jail, Sewell.--My suit is fortunately striped, so I
guess I’m already dressed for the part!’

Sewell laughed heartily at the joke, dismissing Pete with a nod
and settling down to a comfortable smoke. Under guise of a general
conversation he probed Basil about his business, displaying an
unexpected knowledge of financial matters. Basil expanded readily,
in fact, almost succeeded in making a sale of some speculative stocks
to the inspector. It was not definitely closed, however, Sewell
deciding with Yankee caution to sleep on the matter. Then the latter
began to prepare the meal, taking Basil into his small kitchen while
he cooked with appetizing skill and lack of effort.

Over the food the conversation veered around to the affair of the
murder, when Basil abruptly asked Sewell if he hadn’t forgotten to
feed his prisoner, Ruel.

‘Oh, I’ll tend to him later. It don’t do him no harm to wait a bit.
About all a man in a cell has to think over is his next meal, an’
they say anticipatin’ is better than gettin’, you know.’

‘I see. And a little hunger sometimes loosens a man’s tongue, eh?’

Sewell nodded and winked slyly.

‘Don’t do no harm, anyways. But I thought as you was a friend of his.
Sounds like you kind of expected him to confess to somethin’.’

‘I do--and I don’t, Sewell. I’ve been thinking over matters a lot
recently, and it seems inevitable--his having something to do with
it at least. I know you must feel the same way or you wouldn’t keep
him a prisoner.--As to his being a friend of mine--well, it’s hard
to express myself clearly. He is, in a casual sort of way, mostly
because he was a friend of King’s.’

‘An’ Miss Hawley’s?’ added Sewell suggestively.

A faint but unmistakable frown momentarily crossed Basil’s face.

‘Of course.--You understand I like him myself too. He always seemed
a solid kind of man, if you know what I mean, not exactly quick and
keen, but quiet, almost to the point of dullness in comparison with
the people I know. I suppose that’s the New-Englandism of him.’

‘Maybe, though he ain’t always lived in these parts an’ he’s been to
college. Guess he would make a kind of hard man to beat in anythin’
he really wanted.’

‘Some things, but not others,’ smiled Basil assuredly. ‘Perhaps
it’s just that he doesn’t seem to fit in with King’s other friends,
in a way. But what I’m getting at is this. As far as I can see,
all the evidence points to him and it’s only his denials and half
explanations that oppose it. I’d be the last man to suspect him of
guilt myself, yet he must be guilty until proved innocent, to twist
the phrase, and that doesn’t look very promising of accomplishment.’

‘But suppose he was shieldin’ some one else?’ put in Sewell.

‘Some one else?--You don’t mean--Sylvia?’

‘I certain sure do.’

‘That’s ridiculous. It only goes to show his method of insinuation if
he’s been trying to throw suspicion that way on an innocent person.
Confidentially, that’s one of the reasons I don’t like him, Sewell.
He doesn’t seem to know his place at times, assumes liberties, if you
know what I mean. I’m afraid it’s rank jealousy.’

‘You mean you an’ her?’

‘Between ourselves, yes. We have an--er--understanding.’

‘I see. That sort of clears up some things a bit,’ mused Sewell
aloud. ‘It makes some of his remarks kind of more forceful an’ I’m
glad you warned me.’

‘It wasn’t a pleasant duty, but after all I’m glad you know.’ Basil’s
manner was quite convincingly relieved, though his smile was a trifle
smug.

‘Now there’s one or two questions I’d like to ask you as might be
important in that connection. First off, when he came out of the room
after the play, you was standin’ there by the window.--Which side was
it?’

‘On the right as you face the window from the outside. I remember
that distinctly.’

‘An’ you’d just been standin’ there all the time?’

‘Exactly--waiting for him.’

‘That’s that. Now the other thing’s about the burglary business when
those papers was taken. You don’t remember anythin’ about them?
Seems queer, because Ruel was here in jail an’ if he was the guilty
party----’

‘But the two don’t necessarily have any relation, surely.’

‘Some one took advantage of King’s death to get them papers, seems
like to me.’

‘Coincidence! Besides, the thief was an outsider, so might have done
it without even knowing King was dead.’

‘Maybe, but I doubt it.--You don’t know any one as wanted any of his
papers, then?’

Basil shook his head silently, but did not meet Sewell’s gaze.

‘Well, now, I tell you what we’ll do. I’ve kind of got a scheme. You
sneak out into the next cell to Ruel’s when I open the door into the
corridor, an’ listen while I ask some questions, see? Better step
right inside the cell in case he comes close to the bars, because you
can hear just as good an’ won’t be seen. You listen close, an’ later
we’ll compare notes, see?’

Basil fell in with the plan eagerly. When Sewell opened the door and
motioned to him, Basil dodged into the empty cell, drawing its door
almost closed behind him and listening as Sewell stepped to Ruel’s
cell, calling gruffly, ‘Now, prisoner, I’ve decided we’ll have a
little show-down an’ get back of some of this lyin’. Not one bite
more food do you get, my man, until you tell the truth an’ the whole
of it, see? There’s ways of makin’ a man tell the truth, an’ I’d
advise you not to make me use too many of ’em.’

Basil waited for a response, pressing against the bars to listen. He
scarcely noticed the slight click as the lock snapped into place,
but Sewell was listening for it. Suddenly the inspector stepped down
closer to the bars of Basil’s cell.

‘You heard what I said?’ he called.

Basil nodded silently, thinking it was a double question both for
his and Ruel’s benefit, but he was surprised when Sewell continued,
addressing him directly:

‘Good land, Stacey, you’ve locked yourself in there an’ I can’t get
you out. The key’s mislaid!’

Basil stared at him helplessly, scarcely comprehending what this
situation might mean.

‘But you must!’ he cried, his voice raised sharply as he tugged at
the bars. ‘I refuse to stay cooped up in this----’

‘Oh, you ain’t exactly a prisoner, young man, if that’s what you
mean. Of course I haven’t got any right nor reason to keep you
against your will, only you see I can’t let you out without a key,
can I?’ There was a hint of mockery in his tone that did not escape
Basil. ‘You should have gone into the other cell, anyway. Guess you
must have made a mistake again about your directions.’

‘Directions? You told me to come here and if you don’t----’

‘Hold on. It don’t seem as if I’d instruct you to lock yourself in a
cell without a key, now, does it? Besides, if you got into the wrong
one, I’m not so surprised, though I can’t see why a man should get
all confused over which is his right an’ which his left hand. But
there have been cases. Take a man I know, for instance, who stands on
the left of a window, yet insists it was the right. You’d think he’d
know better, wouldn’t you?’

‘What do you mean? What are you babbling about?’ yelled Basil, but
the color came and went in his cheeks with great rapidity.

‘An’ since it looks as if you’d have to stay here a bit till I get
somethin’ to let you out with--them bars are mighty stout--here’s
another little puzzle you might kind of think over for somethin’ to
occupy your mind. When I come back, we’ll talk it over some more,
perhaps. You see I’m sort of wonderin’ about a certain man I know. He
committed a theft of some papers like, an’ sent ’em away by mail----’

‘You mean to insinuate I----You’re keeping me here illegally for----’

‘Hold on, Stacey. No need to get all roiled up that way. I ain’t
said no names at all, have I? I was just explainin’ some interestin’
ideas of mine an’ you seem to think they fit you so well you’re
denyin’ ’em all! As to keepin’ you here, that ain’t my fault. I
didn’t lock you up or anythin’, did I? It was just an accident like.
Of course I can’t hold you even if I’d wanted to. But knowin’ you’re
so anxious to help out in this mystery, maybe you’ll overlook any
inconvenience of bein’ here--until I can find that key to let you
out, see? Meanwhile, I guessed you’d like somethin’ to think about
an’ pass the time.’

Sewell’s words were apologetic, but his tone was not sincere, so that
their double meaning was obvious. Basil was not a prisoner, yet he
might be held there until he satisfied Sewell’s purpose and would
have no cause for objection later. He began to storm and bluster, but
the inspector turned away, muttering that he had better set about
looking for that key. Then Basil called aloud to Ruel to witness his
plight, but when he received no answer, Sewell poked his head in the
corridor door for a final gibe.

‘Guess you won’t get no response out of Ruel just now. Maybe you’d
like to figure out why!’ Then he closed the door with a bang and
sat down to think. That his ruse would quickly scare a confession
from Basil he felt certain, but that this confession would prove
of little real value he also felt decided. From his talk with this
man and a study of his character, Sewell had evolved a theory that
quite plausibly explained everything. Basil was greedy--a sharper
in business. He had stolen back some stocks which, if found, would
somehow react to his loss or discredit. That accounted for the
robbery.

As to the rest, he did not seem either cunning or brainy enough to
have planned and executed this murder. His presence on the wrong
side of the window when Ruel came out, no doubt had some simple
explanation. Also his attitude toward Miss Hawley was quite clear.
He was trying to force her into marriage to obtain King’s legacy.
He had spoken of an ‘understanding,’ had acted confidently, but not
until to-day. Obviously he had been talking to her, probably held
some threat over her. This would account for her strangely upset and
nervous condition.

Sewell’s contemplations had reached this point when they were
suddenly interrupted by the entrance of Judge Brant, who had just
returned on the afternoon train from Boston. He was quite red in the
face and excited.

‘Just dropped in for a little call, Sewell,’ he began, glancing about
the room quickly.

‘Glad to see you, Judge. Sit down. I was wantin’ to have a talk with
you,’ answered Sewell, noting his eagerness questioningly. ‘Been to
the city? Have a good trip?’

‘Yes, quite an interesting one,’ said the Judge, sticking a cigar in
the corner of his mouth and preferring to chew it rather than light
it. ‘How is the case progressing, Inspector?’

‘Slow, slow. But things are headin’ up some.’

‘And how is your prisoner?’

‘Prisoner? Well, he’s kind of restless, but I gave him somethin’ to
occupy his mind for a bit.’

‘He’s still in his cell, of course?’

‘Locked up, snug as you please.’

‘Then I’d like to see him for a moment.’

‘Now, Judge, don’t know as I can allow that,’ answered Sewell without
change of expression, but becoming alert. ‘Don’t see as that would be
policy just now.’

‘Why not?’

‘Well, you see he’s a guilty man, one that’s under suspicion, at
least, an’ not one to receive callers. Besides, you’re sort of an
interested party an’ that makes it bad.’

‘Interested in finding the murderer, yes. But I want to see your
prisoner for a minute.’

‘Then I’ve got to know why, Judge. Don’t seem to me you’d have any
real important reason for seein’ him as I can figure out.’

‘Oh, I won’t speak to him if you forbid that,’ answered the judge
rather testily.

‘Hm. Then why disturb him at all?’

‘Why? I’ll tell you why. Because I don’t believe he’s there at all!
You’re lying to me, Sewell, and I’m dissatisfied with the way you’re
handling this affair. I almost hired a real detective to-day, and now
I’m sorry I didn’t!’

‘You mean to say you don’t believe I have a prisoner?’ remarked
Sewell, apparently not at all disturbed by this accusation. ‘But I
swear he’s there safe an’ sound.’

‘Then why this hesitation about letting me prove it for myself?’

‘An’ if I might say the same to you, why this sudden notion of yours
that he ain’t?’

‘Because I’ve seen him with my own eyes--in Boston, that’s why!’

Sewell was not entirely unprepared for this answer, so he was able
to conceal his thoughts. It complicated matters quite badly. The
judge was suspicious and antagonistic. It would be hard to explain
matters to him without either drawing in Mrs. Bascom, which would be
disastrous in every way, or without further exposing himself to the
charge of incompetence in his official capacity. His eyes flitted
about the room for an instant, paused at the open window behind the
judge’s back, then returned to his accuser’s face placidly.

‘Sure you weren’t mistaken in thinkin’ you saw Ruel in Boston?’ His
voice was unnecessarily loud and sharp.

‘Positive. I met him almost face to face, but was so astonished he
slipped away before I could think to call the police.’

‘Queer. Seems like it must have been his double, then.’

‘Why not prove it without further hedging?’ snapped the judge puffing
to his feet with determination.

‘All right, I will, seein’ as you insist,’ said Sewell, standing up
in turn. ‘We’ll go in there,’ he motioned with his thumb toward the
closed door into the cell corridor, ‘an’ I’ll prove you’re wrong.
But first I’ve got a question or two to ask you myself, Judge. If
you don’t mind comin’ into my bedroom so there’s no chance of bein’
overheard, I’d like to tell you somethin’ confidential, an’ then I’ll
prove Ruel is there in his cell all right.’

His assurance nettled the judge, but he nodded assent briefly. Sewell
led the way into the inner room and closed the door behind him.

‘Now, then, Judge, you’re accusin’ me of bein’ slow. Well, maybe
I have been, but I’ve heard some things, right enough--more’n I’d
have heard by threatenin’ an’ accusin’ people right and left. For
instance, I’ve got the knife as was used to kill King; I know who did
the thievin’ after his death; I know how the murder was done exactly,
where the man stood----’

‘The man or the woman,’ interrupted the judge. Though obviously
impressed by Sewell’s manner and words, he appeared equally innocent
of any foreknowledge of these facts.

‘No, I said “man.” You’re basin’ too much on Miss Hawley’s attitude
about things.’

‘Wait. Since you know so much, let me add something you haven’t
learned. There is more than mere suspicion about that will which
makes me mistrust her.--The night of the theft, now. You rather
convinced me that Mrs. Bascom did not go to my room, but some
one else did, of that I was sure. Again, King had told me of his
intention to redraw his will, leaving a more generous share of his
estate to her, that is, Miss Hawley, but certainly not all of it.
That kept recurring to my mind unpleasantly. Of course she had access
to his papers, so--to cut directly to the point--I searched her room
on the chance of finding something further. I also searched Mrs.
Bascom’s. In Miss Hawley’s, I did find something--quite interesting.’

He paused for a too obvious dramatic effect, rather overdoing it.

‘I found a draft of that will King had spoken about--the one
containing her legacy. It was signed by him, but not witnessed,
hence not legal. And that’s not all. It was hand-written in the
same ink as the one used in the play, and was almost exactly the
same in provisions as his former will except for this new bequest.
That proves he really meant it to stand and that this play one was
somehow a mistake, see?--Now, why was Miss Hawley concealing this and
insisting on the probating of the false one? Answer me that!’

The inspector shook his head gravely; he could not answer it.

‘One thing more. Whatever Miss Hawley’s reasons are for acting this
way, I’m firmly convinced Mrs. Bascom not only is aware of them, but
is abetting her. I’ve noticed many little things and I’m positive
they understand each other.’

‘Perhaps I agree with you there, Judge. Anyway, I thank you for
tellin’ me this, an’ I think it’ll help quite a lot.’

‘How? I don’t see.’

‘Nor I either--definitely. But I’ve got a plan to try out an’, unless
I miss my guess, I’ll know quite a lot more before night.’

‘You mean you have a concrete suspicion? If so, why don’t you _do_
something.’

‘I’m goin’ to, only knowin’ some things an’ people as I do, it don’t
seem wise to be too hasty about it. I think----Hold on, there’s the
telephone.’

Sewell opened the door into the office, inwardly thankful for the
interruption. He took down the receiver and before he could speak a
woman’s voice challenged him.

‘That you, Ezra Sewell?’

‘Certain sure, Ellen B.’

‘I want to talk with you. It’s something important I have to tell.’

‘About time,’ answered Sewell cheerfully, ‘only better not say it
over the ’phone if it’s important.’

‘When will you be up here?’ she cut in, ignoring his provocative
taunt on her common sense.

‘Soon as I can, Ellen.’

The receiver clicked in his ear. It must be truly important, thought
Sewell, for she had sounded quite humble and disturbed, had not
flashed back at him at all. Probably she had discovered the loss of
the knife, eh?

‘Now, then, Judge, if you want to see the prisoner with your own
eyes, come along,’ said Sewell aloud. He opened the door into the
corridor and pointed to Ruel’s cell.

Judge Brant stepped forward and looked in at the barred door. There
lay a man on the cot, apparently asleep. At that moment, however, he
stirred, twisting his head slightly around so that the judge could
