see his face. It was Ruel! The skepticism faded from the judge’s face
as he turned to Sewell with a nod.

‘I’m satisfied, though I could have sworn that was Ruel in Boston.’

At the sound of his voice, Basil suddenly appeared in his cell,
then quickly drew back again. His first impulse had been to call
out, but he checked it. After all, he did not wish to announce
his predicament, make it worse by explaining himself to the judge
unnecessarily. But as soon as he had heard the judge leave the jail,
Basil called to Sewell, who came to his cell at once.

‘Has he gone?’

‘Who, Judge Brant? Yep, he’s gone.’

‘Then listen, Sewell. I swear to you this is gospel truth. I did
take some papers from the drawer of King’s desk. They were an--an
unfortunate investment I’d made for him--some stocks, you see. I
mailed them to my office, as you evidently found out somehow, in
order to rectify the error. I swear he--or rather his heir--won’t
lose by it. They were technically my stocks, in a way, and I----’

‘Never mind tryin’ to excuse yourself, Stacey. You admit you took
’em.--An’ you don’t know nothin’ else about his death?’

‘On my word of honor--no.’

‘What about that matter of bein’ on the wrong side of the window
after the play?’

‘I’d been trying to get those stocks then. While Ruel was carrying on
the play, I ran to the house, knowing no one would be there. But the
drawer was locked and I hadn’t time to get the stocks. I hurried back
just as Ruel came out.’

‘Didn’t notice anythin’ else? How did you come back, which way, I
mean?’

‘Around the front of the barn and up the hill. It was the quicker
way.’

‘Didn’t happen to notice anythin’ unusual--toward the back, say?’

‘Not a thing. What do you mean?’

‘Never mind.--Guess I believe you this time, Stacey, only take
warnin’. It don’t pay to tell fibs. You always get found out an’
maybe get into bad trouble.’

Basil’s mind was taken up at that moment with something more
important to him.

‘Have you found the key yet?’ he asked anxiously.

‘The key? Well, now, seems as if I’d just thought of where it might
be!’

Sewell shuffled away to return a moment later and unlock the door.
Immediately Basil stepped out with a sigh of relief.

‘There’s no need for any one to know about--about this business and
my being shut up here, is there, Sewell?’

‘Maybe not. Can’t tell yet. But I’d kind of advise you to stay pretty
close to King’s house for the next day or two, Stacey.’

‘Don’t worry. I will. I’m afraid not to--now.’ He glanced curiously
in at the sleeping Ruel. ‘I’ve got a lot more sympathy for him after
this experience.’

Sewell chuckled mildly, but made no reply as Basil hurried out of
the jail, anxious to get as far away from it as possible. As soon as
he had left the corridor, Ruel opened one eye experimentally, then
jumped to his feet.

‘Gosh a’mighty, Ruel, I’m glad to see you again,’ exclaimed Sewell
fervently. ‘Give me the key so I can come in.--It was kind of nip an’
tuck for a while back there. When I saw you peekin’ in at the window,
while I was stallin’ the judge, I almost let out a yell an’ spoiled
it all. An’ I’m glad you took the hint an’ snuck in here.’

‘I was on the same train with him,’ explained Ruel. ‘I knew he’d seen
me in Boston and I’d completed my job, anyway.’

‘Didn’t find what she’d sent you for, eh?’

‘She?--Then you know?--But I did, Ez. I found out King had lived in
Boston, only--well, I hated to dig up his past, Ez, once I found its
trail. It may establish a motive, but, for God’s sake, let’s leave
his past buried otherwise.’

‘As bad as that, Ruel?’

‘Yes.’ Slowly he withdrew a photograph, and some papers covered with
penciled writing from his pocket, placed them before the inspector,
then began his story in a low, vibrant tone.

 CHAPTER XV

 MRS. BASCOM CAPITULATES


‘Since you know about Mrs. Bascom’s hand in my going away, I need not
explain why I left the jail here, Ez.’

The inspector had seated himself on the cot bed in Ruel’s cell as the
latter paced the floor, recounting his experiences on that hasty trip
to Boston.

‘It wasn’t very hard to persuade me, especially as it was a chance to
_do_ something instead of sitting cooped up here. I hoped you would
understand I wasn’t running away.

‘Well, I took a short cut through the woods to the main road, where
I was lucky enough to get picked up by a man who was driving in his
automobile to Boston. We made it in good time, and I started looking
for any record of “Detective King.” I can’t begin to tell you how
many places I----’

‘Don’t try, son. Just skip to the important part,’ interrupted Ezra.

‘That was this morning. Mrs. B. had thought the murder was the work
of some ex-convict, you know, who held a grudge against King as a
detective. I happened to think of those figures in that connection,
wondered if it was possible for them to have been his prison number.
So I went to the Charlestown Jail to check up on them. For some
reason the police in Boston had been very willing to help me in every
way, so got me the proper credentials.’

‘They would. I telephoned ’em that you, as my agent, was to have
clear hoein’.’

‘So that was it!--Well, I found the number in the Rogues’ Gallery all
right, but not what I expected. The convict’s photographs were all
too plainly King!’

‘Hold on, Ruel. It’s easy to be mistaken in photos sometimes.’

‘Don’t think I didn’t _want_ to be, Ez, but the likeness was too
strong and obvious. It was King, younger and under the name of
Jameson, but King beyond a doubt. Then I looked up his record, later
copied parts of it and the newspaper accounts of his trial. You’ll
find it all as I wrote it down.’ He pointed to the papers he had
taken from his pocket, but Sewell scarcely glanced at them.

‘Tell me about it yourself, Ruel, kind of brief like,’ he commanded.

‘It’s not very complicated. Jameson, or King as we knew him, was
in partnership with an older man named Fox. Under cover of a
merchandising brokerage business, it appeared they had been carrying
on some sort of shady work, smuggling or something like that. Finally
the Government got on their trail through some deal in jewels, and
laid a plot to capture the whole gang red-handed. They did get many
of the lesser agents, and when they entered the partners’ office
suddenly, they found King bending over Fox, who was dead--stabbed.
The knife was in King’s hand.

‘King--I can’t think of him as Jameson--was tried and all the
evidence established his guilt, though he maintained he was innocent,
even denied all knowledge of the illegal deals and so on. When his
lawyer saw how the verdict was going, he evidently persuaded King to
change his plea to self-defense. The sentence was finally given for
manslaughter.

‘After twelve years in jail, King was pardoned and seemed to have
disappeared. That was eight years before he came to New York.’

‘An’ he was wealthy then, eh?’ muttered Sewell, half to himself.

‘No, that was one of the ameliorating circumstances to his release.
His partner seemed to have kept most of the money, and it was never
found.--Oh, I don’t believe King was guilty in any way. The money he
made was his own, because he was a good business man and writer of
fiction. He was too kind, honest, and sincere a man for such things.’

‘Seems so,’ echoed Sewell, ‘but it looks now as if no one would ever
know for certain.--But see here. His partner, anyway, was dealin’
shady. That means he must have had a lot of others helpin’ him.
See the point? It might have been one of ’em, one of his gang that
followed King an’ tried to blackmail him--finally killed him. That’s
logical, even if the rest ain’t.’

‘But such a man could not have been one of his guests, Ez.’

‘Yes, he could. If King was innocent, he wouldn’t have known any
of ’em, the gang, see? This man might have made friends with him
on purpose.--Well, anyway, I guess that proves the motive, certain
sure--it was blackmail or sort of revenge. But the question still is,
who was the man?’

‘You haven’t discovered anything since I left?’

‘Yep, quite some, an’ I guess there’s more comin’ right quick.’

‘How? What?’

‘Never mind now. Guess I’ll wait a bit an’ maybe tell it all in a
bunch.’

‘At least, Ez, tell me about Basil. What was he doing in that cell? I
was dumfounded when he first spoke, though I realized it was a sort
of confession he was making.’

‘Believe what he said?’

‘Yes, I do. It explains everything he did. He’s weak, capable of
desperate means to protect his interests, but I’m certain not of
murder. I don’t admire him exactly----’

‘Nor he you!’ chuckled Sewell, then recounted the facts of Basil’s
temporary confinement. ‘But here I am gaddin’ as if I had nothin’
else to do when Ellen B.’s waitin’ for me.--You just rest up a bit,
Ruel. You’ve earned it, I guess. Only don’t go listenin’ to any more
Eves’ or Ellens’ temptin’s, see? Jailbreakin’ ain’t no joke, Ruel,
an’ I’m kind of pergerin’ myself, coverin’ your trail. Guess I ain’t
makin’ no mistake, though.’

With this injunction, Sewell went out, locking the door behind him,
climbed into his automobile and drove rapidly to King’s house. Mrs.
Bascom, having heard his noisy approach, met him on the steps. Her
kindly face was rather strained and pale, but her eyes did not falter
as they met his.

‘Afternoon to you, Ellen B. I was kind of held up a bit gettin’ here,
for which I apologize, seein’ as it ain’t right to keep a woman
waitin’--especially you.’

To his surprise, she did not make any sarcastic retort such as he had
expected.

‘Ezra Sewell, I’ve got a lot to tell you,’ she answered seriously.
‘Let’s go out on the lawn a bit.’

Her glance about was eloquent. Plainly, she feared being overheard,
so chose the lawn where no one could approach without being seen.

‘How’s Miss Hawley to-day?’ remarked Ezra as they walked toward the
elm tree in the center of the grass plot.

‘Worse. She’s in a coma, and Dr. New is afraid it may be serious.’

‘You mean she’s really sick?’

‘Yes. And it’s all my fault.’

‘Shucks, Ellen B. Don’t believe it for a minute. You’d never hurt a
fly--even if you got a chance to swat it.’

She did not respond to this gibe, but faced him, tense yet hesitant.
Twice she tried to speak and failed. Her eyes dropped nervously from
his face and her hands fluttered aimlessly about the pleats of her
dress.

‘Ezra, I’ve been a--a foolish woman, and it’s not easy to start
tellin’ on myself.’ Unconsciously she slurred her words and brought
them out in awkward breaths.

‘Gosh, Ellen, if that’s all that’s troublin’ you, let me help out
a bit.--You kind of think I don’t know much about this affair, eh?
Well, you ain’t the only one of that opinion, an’ I’m glad of it.
That an’ lettin’ others do a lot of talkin’ has learned me things.
Land knows there’s lots I wish I knew--some as I hope you’ll tell me
now--but still I do know perhaps more than you calculate.

‘For instance, I know Basil stole those papers an’ why, also that
he probably is holdin’ somethin’ over Sylvia’s head. Then again, I
know about the judge, pokin’ into ladies’ rooms.--Oh, so that ain’t
news to you, eh?--Well, here’s another. Ruel’s back an’ what he found
about King helps some. Then there’s the paper-knife as was used to
kill King. I know----’

He stopped short at the audible gasp and the strangely awed
expression that came into Mrs. Bascom’s face.

‘Ezra Sewell, you--you mean to say you know--I----’

He smiled at her confusion, but not unkindly.

‘There’s ways of findin’ out things, Ellen B., an’ I’ve found ’em!
But I guess if you an’ me sort of get together on some of ’em,
instead of pullin’ crosswise, it might help a bit, eh?’

‘Ezra Sewell, if I’d only known this before----What a smart Alec
I’ve been!’ She was almost sniveling with the sudden relief of her
wrought-up emotions.

‘There--there, it’s all right, Ellen B. I couldn’t understand you
for--for keepin’ back from me, b-but I never did suspect you of no
wrong nor nothin’ like that.’ Sewell was so embarrassed that he
stuttered as he clumsily tried to soothe her.

‘I’ve been a fool--a fool! And to think you were trustin’ me all the
time too, while I was only trying to be a better detective than you.’

Ezra burst into a laugh that he could not control.

‘A detective, Ellen? Well, I never! If that don’t explain most
everythin’ in a word. So that was why you was so clever an’ smart
at findin’ things, cookin’ up ideas an’ concealin’ information. A
she-detective! An’ you succeeded too. Why, look at that inspiration
about King’s past life, an’ findin’ the murderer’s weapon----’

‘That was only because I happened to come on Sylvia first. Oh, I
should never have kept it from you, only I thought--I----’

‘You thought I was kind of passin’ out on the job. Still, I heard you
defendin’ me once, Ellen, an’ I don’t hold nothin’ against you--not
if you tell me everythin’ now.’

‘I will, Ezra. It’ll be easy and a relief.’ She was recovering her
poise rapidly and once more faced him steadily, alert and eager.
‘You’ve got the knife, then?’

He nodded silently.

‘I thought--I hoped so, only it didn’t seem like you to turn my whole
house upside down. I suppose, not knowing just where it was----’

‘What’s that? Guess you’d better begin at the beginnin’, Ellen. I
got the knife ’cause I guessed where it was, but if your house has
been searched, that wasn’t my doin’.--Let’s take one thing at a time,
startin’--well, wherever you began detectin’.’

Mrs. Bascom needed no further encouragement. With obvious relief
in her tone she began her story, relating everything in detail and
speaking rapidly. She recapitulated her share in the discovery of
the murder, told of finding Sylvia and the knife, repeated Sylvia’s
description of her accident, what she had seen at the time of the
robbery, and the overheard conversation between Sylvia and Basil.
At this point, Sewell interrupted for a moment to tell of his recent
conversation with Basil and the latter’s confession.

An account of Judge Brant’s search of the two women’s rooms, the
discovery that the knife had disappeared and Sylvia’s most recent
confession ended Mrs. Bascom’s revelations. Sewell capped them with
the judge’s explanation for his search of the rooms.

‘I should say, ma’am, your career as a detective has been a mighty
strenuous one,’ continued Sewell. ‘An’ it’s been mighty useful too.
Some ways it looks better, after all--not hearin’ all that till now,
I mean--because it puts everythin’ plain an’ unconfusin’.’

‘I don’t see anything very plain about it, Ezra. All it does is clear
Sylvia and Ruel, who never needed it.’

‘An’ some others, too, Ellen B. Yourself, for instance. Oh, you
needn’t jump like a scared pullet. I don’t mean I suspected you of
murder, but I did of knowin’ too many things an’ keepin’ mum about
’em. Guess you gave me reason enough, too.’

‘Then why didn’t you ask me outright about them? I’d have told you if
I’d known how much you’d found out already.’

‘Would you?’ responded Ezra quizzically. But he did not wait for
an answer as he ruminated aloud, ‘Now, then, let’s sort of arrange
things a little. Sylvia an’ you are out of it--entire. So is Ruel, in
my opinion. Basil Stacey--well, I kind of think he told about all he
knew, an’ I don’t somehow believe he had spunk enough to do anythin’
worse than thievin’ to protect his own hide. That leaves Judge Brant
an’ Dr. New on the scene--unless it was one of the other guests, an’
I doubt that.

‘The judge, now. He’s appeared keen on findin’ the murderer, an’
the things he’s been doin’, when he explained ’em, seem reasonable
enough. The doctor, of course, never was involved, except for his
bein’ on the spot like. Yet it’s got to be one of those men, Ellen
B. The oiled door, the pieces of cement on the window an’ floor, an’
all the careful arrangements prove it was one of the guests. Add the
motive we’ve just turned up--thanks to you, ma’am--an’--well, common
sense, an’ intuition, an’ everythin’, an’ it comes out Stacey or
Brant or New.’

‘Explain the motive. You said Ruel was back, but you haven’t told me
what he found.’

‘It was kind of a shock, Ellen.’

‘I know. But it seemed the only way. I could not go to Boston myself,
and you----’

‘Oh, I don’t mean your sendin’ Ruel there. That was clever of you,
Ellen, but kind of obvious--the way you was so carefully an’ loosely
tied up when you didn’t have to be at all, bein’ locked in the
cell.--No, I mean the facts he found were a shock.’

Briefly he recounted what he had just learned from Ruel, while Mrs.
Bascom breathed in quick gasps.

‘His life as we know it,’ finished Ezra, ‘don’t seem to gibe with
such a history, an’ yet----’

‘Of course it does,’ she interrupted with quick sympathy. ‘He never
was guilty to begin with--he couldn’t have been. It was all his
scheming partner and his gang. But King was just the sort of man
to take everything on his shoulders--give himself and his money to
others in a mistaken sense of restitution to society or whatever
you’re a mind to call it. Besides, it may have been his partner’s
hidden money he did it with.’

‘By gosh a’mighty! I bet you’re right at that,’ muttered Sewell,
impressed with the logic of the idea. ‘It ain’t no use denyin’ it,
Ellen B., we ought to team up together--when it comes to detectin’, I
mean,’ he added somewhat hastily and with a touch of embarrassment.
‘Anyway, that number bein’ written on the paper in his pocket shows
some one knew about his past trouble, an’ that some one must have
been among the guests. It seems logical King might never have known
him, an’ that all goes to prove it was one of this partner’s gang,
seems to me.’

‘It would explain a lot, but how are we going to tell which one it
was? I don’t see that all this brings us any nearer finding the man
who killed King.’

‘Wait a minute. What about your house bein’ all upset? I didn’t do
it, so who did an’ why? That’s the final proof, see? One of these
here men did it, ’cause he’s handy an’ knows what’s goin’ on all the
time.’

‘You mean it was some one after the knife?’

‘That or somethin’ connected with the murder as he thought you had.
Basil, now, he seems quite a breaker into things. Do you suppose he’d
have done it for any reason?’

Mrs. Bascom stared thoughtfully at Sewell for a moment.

‘It’s possible. He was away from the house a good deal yesterday, but
if he didn’t commit the murder, as it seems likely, why should he go
after the knife?’

‘You’re sure you never mentioned to no one where you’d kept it?’

‘Never, not even to Sylvia.’

‘Well, that seems to end it. There’s still one chance left, though.
I kind of--well, saw that you was concealin’ somethin’ in your dress
that day you had the knife. It’s possible he or some one might have
guessed the same thing as I did, an’ looked for it just to give it
over to me.’

Again they seemed to have argued in a circle, but it did not worry
Ezra. It was not the first time he had struggled on after apparently
baffling defeat. He started once more to build a sound case against
Basil, only to find it could be knocked down too easily. In passing,
he mentioned Sylvia’s name and this recalled her condition to Mrs.
Bascom.

‘I’m so glad she told me everything before this relapse.--You don’t
think her condition is serious, do you?’

‘Not bein’ a doctor, I can’t say, Ellen.’

‘I mean, judging from what you saw of her, from your talk with her,
you’d not think her mind affected seriously or anything, would you?’

‘Far from it, Ellen. She was upset in her mind--an’ who wouldn’t have
been with what we now know was in it--but I couldn’t get nothin’
out of her except what she had a mind to tell. I could see she was
thinkin’ rapid, an’ she dodged certain questions quite neat--like you
did--but all that, I should say, showed she was far from sick.’

‘But she is now.--Ezra, I’m worried. If I helped to cause her relapse
as Dr. New said----’

‘He’s got to blame some one, ain’t he, an’ you was handy. A patient
under his care gets worse; whose fault is it? Certainly not
his!--Shucks, Ellen, if her mind’s affected like he says, that wasn’t
your fault. Why, you couldn’t get into her mind like a germ--nor
influence it much up to lately, as far as I can make out.’

This comforted Mrs. Bascom considerably, so that when they turned
toward the house, she felt quite buoyant and ready to throw herself
into the work once more. First she went to look at Sylvia, but found
her condition unchanged. The doctor had left some medicine to be
taken immediately if she stirred, and this stood ready by the side
of her bed. Inexpertly Mrs. Bascom tried to feel Sylvia’s pulse,
but gave up after several attempts when each count proved so widely
divergent from the last. She took a book and sat by the window
without opening it, mulling over the news which Ezra had imparted to
her, but her sense of relief at having shifted the responsibility to
Ezra’s shoulders was so great that she could not think clearly.

In the meantime, the inspector had driven straight to Mrs. Bascom’s
house. She had mentioned the fact that the key was no longer under
the mat, so he had borrowed it from her. Now he entered boldly, ran
up a shade to let in the light and began a search of his own.

Everything was still in confusion, as Mrs. Bascom had not
tried to straighten up, but his search was fruitless. Taking a
magnifying-glass from the parlor table, he studied the knobs and
edges of the drawers that had been opened, scrutinized parts of the
woodwork, table-tops, chairs, but after doing this in three rooms,
he gave up the task. There were no finger-prints, only faint blurs
here and there. Obviously the intruder had worn gloves, just as the
murderer had!

Leaving the lower rooms, Sewell ascended the stairs and opened a door
at the end of the hall. The room was stuffy with the odor of musk,
which, he found, proceeded from a broken vial in the bureau drawer.
It seemed to warn him that he was trespassing on no man’s ground, and
he flushed to himself as he rather hastily resumed his search. It was
in vain. Nowhere was there a clue as to the identity of the intruder.

Disappointed, he carefully drew down the shade on the lower floor
room again, locked the door, and left the house, muttering, ‘Now,
what in time was he after, I’d like to know? There’s one thing
certain, he wasn’t a burglar, since nothin’ seemed to have been
taken, an’ he did know what he was about, wearin’ gloves.’

At the jail, Sewell went straight to the safe and took out his
meager store of clues one by one. These he carried into Ruel’s cell
and spread out on the cot. Ruel started forward at the sight of the
knife, but fell back again at a sign from the inspector.

‘Don’t you touch a thing, son. You just listen to me with both ears
forward an’ I’m goin’ to tell you everythin’, just in the order it
happened, whether it was me or Ellen B. or any one else as really
found it out. They say two heads are better than one. Well, let’s
see if three is better yet. An’, mind you, not an interruption till
I finish. This is only part for your benefit an’ most to hear myself
tell everythin’ I know, see?’

Ruel nodded mutely, his eyes wavering back and forth between the
clues and Sewell’s serious face. Then the latter began, speaking in
a slow, emotionless drawl, picturing everything that had occurred as
if some third person had shared in or watched it, detail by detail.
Several times Ruel choked back an exclamation or question with
difficulty, but he did not speak until Sewell had ceased talking.
Even then he kept silent, for there seemed nothing he could add, no
solution for the mystery he could offer.

‘Well?’ interrogated Sewell at length. ‘Am I to take it a third head
is as befoozled as two of ’em?’

‘At least it all goes to show one thing,’ began Ruel hopefully. ‘It
proves Sylvia is innocent, was only----’

‘My soul an’ body!’ cried Sewell thunderously. ‘I might have known
it. When I take pains to lay the whole thing open before you, ask you
to help me an’ treat you confidential with every bloomin’ detail, all
you’ve got to say is that Sylvia’s innocent!’

‘But I did listen carefully, Ez,’ said Ruel, abashed. ‘I followed
every word, only I don’t see as it clears up anything except her and
Mrs. Bascom’s attitudes. I don’t mean----’

‘Oh, well, I should have expected it,’ said Sewell philosophically, a
twinkle creeping into the corners of his eyes, though his frown did
not relax. ‘It was the same way with her--sayin’ as she’d done it all
for your sake an’ talkin’ to Ellen B. an’ tellin’ her about lyin’
an’ falsifyin’ things just because she--because of you.’

‘Did she say that, Ez?’ pleaded Ruel, seizing him by the arm in his
eagerness.

‘Let go my arm! What you tryin’ to do, bust it clean off with your
sledgehammer hand?--There. That’s better.--Of course she said it, an’
probably a lot more things Ellen an’ I’d blush to repeat.--Hey, where
do you think you’re goin’ now?’

‘To see Sylvia!’

‘No, you ain’t, young man. You stay right here an’ act sensible.’

‘Sensible? That’s exactly what I’m doing.’

‘Hump.--Look here, son. She’s sleepin’ an’ needs the rest. She’s plum
worn out lyin’ for you,’ reasoned Sewell, concealing the seriousness
of her rest, for he knew such knowledge would drive Ruel straight to
her side. ‘You don’t want to make her any worse, do you?--Well, then,
wait till to-morrow, anyway. Guess one day more or less won’t make no
difference to you two.’

‘That’s all you know about it,’ muttered Ruel, but he subsided
restlessly on a corner of the cot. A moment later he was on his feet,
grasping Sewell’s hand and wringing it until it brought a yell of
pain from the inspector.

‘Let go of me, you young ox,’ he bellowed. ‘I’ll have to arrest you
for assault an’ battery too, if you don’t leave me be.’

‘But, Ez, I’ve got to tell you how much I----’

‘No, you ain’t. I won’t listen to you. I’ve got enough on my mind
now, without havin’ to listen to--well, to what you’ve got to say.’

Sewell swept the clues up with one hand, and almost ran from the
room, kicking the door shut behind him violently to hide his chuckles.

His smile faded rapidly, however, as he locked the articles in the
safe once more, then sat down at his desk, gazing speculatively at
his little list of suspects with the comments he had noted opposite
each name.

‘He ain’t fallen into a trap of his own makin’ yet. Now I wonder if
he will--or have I got to set one for him?--The trouble is, I don’t
know exactly how to bait it, seein’ as I ain’t certain sure which
one he is. But he’s gettin’ nervous an’ more darin’, an’ whichever
he is, that’s somethin’. Sooner or later truth’ll come out, only if
Ellen an’ me could only push it along a mite faster.--Detective Ellen
Bascom an’ Inspector Ezra Sewell----’

He chuckled, then became pensively silent.

 CHAPTER XVI

 CONFLICTING WILLS


When Inspector Sewell arrived at King’s house the next day, he seemed
tired and hollow-eyed. That was the result of long hours of thought
and vain planning. Being of that healthy type of men who are used to
regularity and a full nine hours of sleep each night, the lack of it
showed plainly in his face. Yet his shoulders were erect, his eyes as
keenly alert as ever in spite of the dark arcs below them.

Mrs. Bascom met him at the door. By contrast, she seemed to have
rested and relaxed from her overwrought emotions of the previous day,
for her face was calm and unworried, her poise assured.

‘Good-morning, Ezra.’ She eyed him sharply and her mood softened
further.

‘’Mornin’, Ellen B. You look perky as a lark an’ fresh as a daisy.’

‘Can’t say you do, Ezra.’ It might have been a taunt, but her tone
robbed the words of any such interpretation.

‘Guess daisies an’ larks ain’t in my line,’ grinned Ezra. ‘How’s
Sylvia?’

‘Just the same.--Before I forget it, I think I left a handkerchief in
your automobile yesterday. Will you help me find it?’

Mrs. Bascom moved down the drive toward the car and Sewell followed,
after her meaning glance at him. When they reached it, she went to
one side and began fumbling around the seats while he did likewise
on the other.

‘I thought it best to take precautions,’ explained Mrs. Bascom
quietly. ‘I wanted you to see this letter I received in answer to one
I wrote.’

She dropped a sheet of notepaper to the floor of the automobile,
and Ezra, with a nod of approval, bent closer to read it while he
pretended to putter about. When he had finished it, he stuck the note
into his pocket.

‘Thanks, Ellen. I guess we’d better talk it over with the judge.’

‘I didn’t know what your plans were--whether you wanted to confide
anything to him or the others,’ answered Mrs. Bascom, and the
suggestion of her complete dependence upon his decision revealed to
what extent she had now confided in him.

‘Some things, yes, but not all. I--it’s sort of nice of you, Ellen
B., to--well, to do like this,’ stammered Ezra, suddenly ill at ease
for no accountable reason.

‘I want you to handle things--to assume the responsibility. That’s
why I told you,’ she replied readily. ‘I’m out of this from now on.’

‘Not if I can help it. You’ve got to assist me, Detective Bascom,’
he grinned, continuing before she could interrupt with more than a
vague gesture of deprecation--‘this is what I aim to do now, subject
to change without notice, as they say. Not a hint about what Sylvia’s
told you, nor about the knife nor King’s past. Otherwise I’ll act
trustin’ as ever, an’ you do it, too. Only keep your eyes an’ ears
open an’ don’t be surprised if I don’t follow my own rules.’

She nodded and suddenly whisked a handkerchief out from her skirt,
then pretended to take it from behind the seat.

‘Here it is. I found it, Ezra,’ she called quite loudly.

Sewell straightened up to see the judge approaching behind him across
the lawn.

‘’Mornin’ to you, Judge. You’re just the man I want to see now that
I’ve done my one good deed for the day by helpin’ Ellen find her
handkerchief--if you’d call that dinky pen-wiper such a thing. Let’s
get along into the house out of the sun.’

Judge Brant nodded to Mrs. Bascom, and the three moved toward
the house with some inconsequential remarks. In the hall, they
encountered Basil talking earnestly to Dr. New, so Sewell suggested
they make it a full conference. He led the way to King’s den, where
they all sat down comfortably.

‘You seen Miss Hawley yet to-day?’ the inspector addressed the doctor.

‘Yes. She is still resting.’

‘You mean unconscious?--Well, I hope it ain’t as serious as Mrs.
Bascom told me it might be?’

‘Can’t say definitely as yet--certainly not in its present stage.’

‘Nothin’ we can do to hurry things along a bit, then?’

‘Not until she comes out of her coma.’

‘Well, that’s kind of unfortunate, as I think what I’m goin’ to tell
you has to do with her, an’ I’d like to hear what she’d say about
it. However, let me ask you, Judge, a question to start.--About that
will, how does one of ’em have to be signed to make it legal?’

‘It must be signed by the executor in the presence of three
disinterested parties, each signing after him in the presence of the
others. Is that what you mean?’

‘Precisely. An’ whatever’s signed that way is a legal will, eh?’

‘If the witnesses to the instrument know it was a will, yes, and
providing there is no fraud or such contingency.’

‘Supposin’ there was a mistake. Supposin’ there was two wills an’ the
wrong one got signed, all legal like.’

‘That’s not definite enough. Could you prove absolutely that it was
an error?’

‘Not positively, perhaps.’

‘Then, if one was legally executed, it would stand, supposing your
premise to be true.--Are you implying this is what happened in the
case of King’s will?’

‘I think so, only what you say complicates matters, some. Here’s
the facts. Mrs. Bascom here has received a letter from one of the
witnesses, one of the guests, you know, who signed his will. I’ll
read part of it to you.’

Sewell adjusted his glasses with care, took a letter from his pocket
and read aloud:

  We assembled in his library. There were several papers on his
  desk, rather scattered about, some signed, others not. One of
  these latter he took up, explaining it was his will and that we
  were to witness it.

  He glanced it over a minute, then wrote his signature. Of course
  I never looked at what was written except to notice that it was
  in his own hand. He gave me the pen first, and I was just about
  to sign it when there was a terrible clatter outside and King
  exclaimed, ‘Sounds like the local fire department in action.’ We
  rushed to the window and threw it open, all leaning out to watch.

  To us city people the apparatus was quaint and amusing, so we
  watched until it disappeared up the road. I remember King said,
  ‘Just a brush fire and not dangerous if they’re going in that
  direction, though it’s a bit breezy for any sort of a fire
  to-day.’

  When we went back to the desk, I picked up the paper that the
  wind had blown a little to one side, and signed it under his
  name. The others followed me.

  That is all I remember. I’ve told it in as much detail as I
  could....

‘The rest doesn’t matter,’ explained Sewell, folding up the paper
again. ‘One thing more, though. This witness showed her statement
to another one of ’em before sendin’ it, an’ he agreed to every
word--What do you think of that, Judge?’

‘I’d say it was perfectly legal,’ he answered slowly.

‘An’ that’s the one we found on King’s desk after he was killed,
the one makin’ Miss Hawley his heiress. But I want to explain what
I think really happened. There was another draft of a will. It was
found among some papers on his desk in the house here. This one
wasn’t signed except by King, an’ his writin’ was blurred a bit as
if somethin’ had smeared over it before the ink was dry.

‘Now that letter from the witness speaks of one or two interestin’
points. There were several papers on the desk, some already signed.
Again, there was a wind blowin’ when the window was opened,
interruptin’ the signin’. The paper was blown to one side, see?
Well, I guess you can figure out now what I’m drivin’ at. I believe
he’d signed one for the play, just a fake one meanin’ to fake some
witnesses for it. Then he signed the real one all right, but the wind
blew it aside and this witness picked up the fake by mistake.’

‘Ingenious--and plausible,’ chimed in Judge Brant, bringing his
hands together with a bang. ‘I’m positive something like that must
have occurred. Besides’--he turned to the others--‘remember King’s
expression when he first took up that will in the play? It was
not wholly acting, I believe now. He was genuinely surprised and
concerned for a moment, started to tear it up, then decided to go on
with the play. I’m sure it was when he discovered his error.’

‘Seems to bear out my theory, don’t it?’

The judge’s elation suddenly wilted. ‘But, Sewell, this is all
assumption. I don’t see how it can be definitely proved.’

‘You mean the signed one would stand in spite of this?’

‘It would be hard to discredit it.’

‘Why try to?’ interrupted Basil with assurance. ‘There’s no reason
for thinking he didn’t mean to make Miss Hawley----’

‘Yes, there is. He told me about his plans just the day before and
they included a bequest, but not the entire estate, to Miss Hawley,’
said the judge.

‘He may have changed his mind,’ argued Basil stubbornly. ‘It seems to
me she has a perfect right to insist on the terms of that will.’

‘Makes her quite an heiress--a right smart catch for some man, don’t
it?’ interposed Sewell calmly, his eyes meeting Basil’s squarely.

The latter’s dropped quickly before this taunt, as he turned away
with a gesture of bravado, but not before a flush stained his cheeks.
No one seemed to notice this except Mrs. Bascom who shot a glance of
approval at the inspector.

‘Now, folks, I’m pretty sure of one thing--to get back to the murder
part. Whoever did it must have known King pretty well to have had a
motive an’ he must have been right here on the spot.--I ain’t exactly
suspectin’ one of you here an’ I ain’t so sure about Ruel, so it
seems to me perhaps I’d better get along myself an’ call on every one
of the other guests present that night. It’s kind of a long process,
but it might lead to somethin’. What do you think?’

‘I’ve wondered why you didn’t do it before,’ remarked Judge Brant
dryly.

‘Not I,’ said Dr. New. ‘It seems to me the thing to do now, but only
as a sort of last gasp, when all else has failed. Of course, if you
had some definite clue, it would be different.’

‘That’s the funny part, ain’t it? There don’t seem to be any of ’em
to speak of. I ain’t said much about it to you before, folks, but
it’s struck me as queer all along that the murderer’s weapon hasn’t
turned up yet. That’s really the only argument against its bein’ one
of the guests, see?’

‘No, I don’t. What do you mean?’

‘Well, Doctor, it’s this way. Say one of ’em killed King with some
sort of knife. What could he do with it--where could he hide it? You
were all searched, an’ so was the barn. I even went over the ground
outside to see if it was buried. It wasn’t. Yet it must have been
hidden mighty quick after the murder unless it was some one from
outside who didn’t stay after his deed.’

‘What makes you think it wasn’t such a person?’ The question was
Basil’s.

‘Kind of a hunch, I guess; also the fact that whoever did it must
have known about King’s plans for the play pretty well or he’d not
have taken such a chance.’

‘A pretty strong argument against Ruel, then, isn’t it?’

‘Just as strong against you--or Miss Hawley,’ answered Sewell
briefly, and Basil abruptly subsided again. ‘Now, Doctor, let’s just
go over a point or two about the actual killin’, if you don’t mind.
We know it was skillful an’ sudden, struck right to the heart. I
just wondered whether that was an easy thing to do--hit the heart, I
mean--or did it require some special skill?’

‘Not at all. The blow penetrated the thorax between the sternal ribs
and----’

‘Hold on a bit. Thorax--I thought that was somethin’ in the throat?
Couldn’t you explain a bit simpler?’

‘Of course. The thorax is, in general, the part of the body between
the neck and abdomen. A blow directed at the heart, if it struck in
that region and hard enough, might pierce that organ or be deflected
into it by one of the ribs. Any one might have done it quite easily,
and death would be instantaneous with the stopping of the heart
action.’

‘I see.--Could a woman have done it, then?’

‘Yes.’

They were silent for a moment after this reply, the judge’s eyes
automatically glancing at Mrs. Bascom, then quickly away again.

‘Well, that’s only as I’d figured it out myself, an’ it don’t change
nothin’.--I guess that’s about all this conference is good for
to-day, unless some one’s got a suggestion to make.’

‘The only thing I can say is--let’s have some action,’ grumbled the
judge. ‘Things have been dragging on and on, but as far as I can see,
we’re no nearer finding the murderer. I don’t doubt your sincerity
in the least, Sewell, but I want to see King’s murderer brought to
justice.’

‘No more’n I do. If you can suggest some way of actin’, you’ve got me
beat.’

‘That’s just it. You’ve covered the ground thoroughly without
unearthing anything of value. Now, why not call in a little outside
help? It may be rather late in the day, but----’

Martha appeared in the doorway at this moment to interrupt with a
telegram for the inspector. This he read slowly without change of
expression, then got to his feet as he put the paper in his pocket.

‘I guess I will, Judge. I know a man for the job, an’ I’ll send a
wire to him right now by Pete who brought me this little business
matter.’

He left the room quickly, and was back again before any one spoke.

‘This man, name of Bartrum, ought to be here by to-morrow. Till then,
I guess there’s nothin’ to do but hold on.’

‘He’s a good man?’ asked Judge Brant earnestly. ‘I know a smart
detective myself, you see.’

‘I guess he’s smart as they make ’em,’ cut in Sewell with finality.

‘Good, I’ll wait till after he comes, then I really must get back to
my work. I’ve stayed longer than I had intended already.’

‘I also,’ added Dr. New. ‘If it were not for Miss Hawley’s condition
I’d have gone before. But I hope she’ll come around soon. If not, it
would be better to place the case in the hands of another doctor and
nurse, I’m afraid. It rather baffles me.’

‘Well, I guess that’ll be all right, gentlemen, an’ thanks for
stayin’ so long.--Ellen B., anythin’ I can do for you in the village,
or take you there, perhaps?’ drawled Sewell, his eyes meeting hers
for a brief glance.

‘No, thanks, Ezra.--But wait. I guess I will run down to the store a
minute, if you can spare the time.’

‘Certain sure. Got nothin’ to do but wait for help now.’

Mrs. Bascom bustled up the stairs for a peep at Sylvia, then hurried
out to Ezra, who was waiting on the piazza, chatting with the men.
The car was started and, Mrs. Bascom at his side, it chugged down
the road leaving a powdery cloud of dust behind it. Halfway to the
village, Ezra stopped by the side of the road quite suddenly.

‘Important errand, Ellen B.?’ he grinned.

‘Very. I need a spool of thread for the kitten to play with,’ she
replied with an answering smile. ‘What was it you wanted to tell me,
Ezra?’

‘Right smart at takin’ a hint, ain’t you?--Well, now, seein’ as we’re
partners in this business, I kind of thought you’d like to know about
that telegram. Women are supposed to be curious, you know.’

‘Not supposed to be--they are,’ corrected Mrs. Bascom in a
matter-of-fact tone.

‘Well, I’ll tell you, then. When Ruel had gone an’ I sort of guessed
where an’ why, I called up the police of Boston an’ told ’em to treat
him well, give him all the help possible. They did all right, an’ now
they’re wirin’ me some more information. Guess they must have done
some lookin’ around, too, an’ now here’s this man Bartrum they’re
sendin’ along to help. He knows all about that old case, see? They’re
sendin’ some more dope to me an’ it may be interestin’, but I guess
I can’t wait for it to come. Incidentally, I didn’t wire for him, as
he’s already comin’ as near as I could make out their message.’

‘So that’s your famous detective?’

‘Yep, though he may not know it!--But I declare I’d like to start
somethin’ right now--I’ve got to. I was thinkin’ most all of last
night how to lay a trap or somethin’, but I’m scared to--yet.’

‘You mean you really suspect one of those men here?’

Sewell nodded gravely, but declined to reveal his thoughts, and she
did not press him.

‘The knife--how about finding it suddenly? That might lure him into
exposing himself,’ suggested Mrs. Bascom eagerly.

‘An’ it only takes a woman that--’ he snapped his fingers loudly, ‘to
think of what took me most an hour! But it won’t do, Ellen. If some
one was after the knife in your house, he already knows you have it,
or thinks you have, an’ pretendin’ to find it would only make him
suspicious of a trap. No use doin’ that.’

They were silent for a moment, then Mrs. Bascom spoke.

‘Ezra, I’ve been wondering. Judge Brant seemed anxious to get hold of
a detective--but one that he knew. The moment you spoke of Bartrum,
he decided to leave. Do you suppose that has any significance?’

‘I can suppose lots of things, Ellen, an’ I’ll admit I’d thought of
that, but what’s the use? Dr. New’s leavin’ too, an’ both of ’em
have suggested gettin’ a detective, before. You can twist almost
anythin’ any one says or does into meanin’ somethin’ suspicious, when
you haven’t somethin’ definite to go on. But I’m gettin’ kind of
discouraged with myself, Ellen,’ he went on, his mouth drooping at
the corners--‘kind of losin’ faith in myself--about ready to give up.’

‘Give up? Don’t be a fool, Ezra Sewell. Don’t admit you’re stumped
when you’re not and you know it. Don’t fish for compliments from me!’

The tartness of her words revived him like a tonic. She had been
almost too docile since her confession, but this was more like her
old, vigorous self. Yet the flame was only temporary. When he egged
her on again, she bit her lip and answered mildly, turning the
subject.

‘We’ve had time enough to get to the village and back. Better turn
around so I can return and be with Sylvia. I’m worried about her,
Ezra.--By the way, does Ruel know?’

‘Not yet, though I kind of promised him a talk with her to-morrow.
He’s gettin’ restless an’ wants to see her. I guess you know why,
Ellen.’

‘Of course I do, the poor children.’

‘Children? Now don’t rub it in like that. Makes me feel like an
old Methuselah, an’ as to you--well, you ain’t exactly decrepit
yet--seems to _me_.’

With this remark, he started the car so abruptly that their heads
jerked backward painfully. When she had recovered herself, Mrs.
Bascom glanced at his whimsically stern face with its eyes held
straight to the front. It was a dubious compliment, perhaps, but
coming from him so unexpectedly it had taken her off guard. A reply
was impossible as he backed and headed for King’s house, so she
remained thoughtfully silent. When he stopped to let her out at
the door, however, she leaned close to make him hear as she gave a
parting word of advice.

‘Keep Ruel away for to-day, at least. Tell him some message from
Sylvia. Make up a strong one. You’d know what to say!’

Then she hurried into the house, leaving Ezra staring after her in
open-mouthed alarm.

He’d know what to say----Now what did she mean by that? The answer
worried him even more than the other questions on his mind as he
returned to the jail and spent an hour energetically sweeping up and
tidying the office. His message to Ruel seemed sufficiently personal
to pacify him for a time, however, especially when Ezra hinted it was
Sylvia’s own desire to rest and be at her best when they should next
meet.

Ezra’s housekeeping duties over, he was about to go outside again
when the telephone rang. It was Mrs. Bascom, who spoke in a low,
hurried voice.

‘I want you to come here after lunch. Take the back road and bring
Dr. Smith from the village. Be here by, but not before, two o’clock.
I’ll signal to you.’

With that the receiver clicked silent, and Ezra hung up. So Ellen was
at work again, eh?--She wanted the village doctor--why? Obviously to
see Sylvia. Again, why? And her abrupt manner----

‘Ruel,’ called Ezra, ‘do you know any funny stories? If you do, tell
me one quick so I can stop mullin’ over foolish ideas like I’ve got
now.’

But Ruel did not reply. He was asleep.

At the appointed hour, Sewell led the doctor toward King’s house
from across the orchard back of the barn. He consulted his watch to
be exact and scanned the house for some sign from Ellen. As they
approached cautiously, Mrs. Bascom appeared in the kitchen doorway
and beckoned for them to hurry, which they did.

‘Dr. Smith,’ she said as she led them toward the hall, ‘I want you to
look at Miss Hawley. I’ve been thinking back over her condition and
I’m not satisfied, somehow, with Dr. New’s diagnosis. He’s supposed
to be a fine nerve specialist, but--well, I’m not sure it’s all
nerves. The three men have gone for the mail.’

‘Tell me what you think,’ said the doctor, taking his cue from her
and not wasting time by idle questions to satisfy his curiosity at
this mysterious summons.

Briefly she recounted Sylvia’s symptoms of a nervous breakdown,
omitting the reasons for it, and Dr. New’s gloomy predictions,
then opened the door to Sylvia’s room. Sewell peeped in, but did
not enter, leaving that to Mrs. Bascom and the doctor. It was not
modesty that held him back, rather it was the realization that now
was a splendid chance to search the absent men’s rooms as he had been
planning to do.

Of necessity it was rather a hasty search, yet it was not
unproductive. He entered the judge’s room first, as it was the
nearest. Here everything was neat and precise as he had expected;
toilet articles arranged and orderly, clothes hung in the closet,
shoes in a row on the floor. A leather portfolio lay open on the
table, containing a sheaf of documents. One of them caught Ezra’s
eye and he slipped it out. It was a half-sheet with the typed
words--‘Prisoner No. 24578.’ This he tucked into his pocket.

The doctor’s room came next, a decided contrast in neatness. A suit
of clothes lay mussed in a chair, a medical book on the floor, while
the bureau top was a jumble of toilet articles. Two white pills lay
untidily on the carpet before the bureau, and Ezra neatly picked them
up. A handbag in the closet disclosed part of a doctor’s kit, a
stethoscope, some bottles of pills and liquids, and a roll or two of
bandages. As Ezra bent over this he sniffed once, smiled to himself,
then nodded and left the room.

Clothes for all occasions filled Basil’s room, each rather carefully
hung up or put away in drawers, but one thing in his almost empty
suit-case pursed Ezra’s lips into a soundless whistle. Tucked into
a side pocket next a flask, was a jimmy and a revolver! This latter
he unloaded, dropping the shells into his pocket, then, hearing
footsteps in the hall, hurried back toward Sylvia’s room.

The doctor and Mrs. Bascom stood there looking for him, the former
rather grim, the latter horrified, yet triumphant.

‘How is she?’ Sewell addressed the doctor, but his eyes were fixed on
Mrs. Bascom’s.

‘She will regain consciousness in a short time now.’

‘An’ she’ll be clear in her mind, do you think?’

‘Absolutely. She’ll only remember that she’s been asleep, probably. I
mean she will not know why she’s been unconscious for so long.’

‘But you do?’

‘I do!’

If he had expected to create a sensation by these words, the doctor
failed, for Sewell, still staring at Mrs. Bascom, merely nodded as if
the news was no surprise to him--was only a confirmation of some idea
of his own!

 CHAPTER XVII

 EZRA TAKES A NAP


Mrs. Bascom stared from Ezra to Dr. Smith incredulously. She had
anticipated the doctor’s statement as to Sylvia’s condition would
startle the inspector, but instead he took it calmly, did not even
ask for details as if he were already aware of them.

‘Ezra Sewell,’ she exploded at last, ‘do you mean to stand there, not
even interested enough to ask what was wrong with her? You act as
if----’

Sewell stopped her with a commanding gesture for silence, listened
intently, then spoke quickly and sharply.

‘That’s the judge’s car comin’ back. Go down an’ keep ’em from
lookin’ out back until we get away, Ellen. Mum’s the word till I can
call you up an’ explain.’

Seizing the doctor by the arm, he hurried him down the stairs and out
the back way, pausing until he heard Mrs. Bascom’s voice addressing
the men at the front of the house, then urging the astonished
physician out through the barn and thus away from it by the orchard.
Not until they had safely gained Ezra’s car, parked on the back road,
did the latter speak.

‘Doctor, I guess you must think I’m actin’ like a crazy loon, but
you’ll have to trust me a bit. I’ll just say this. Ellen’s a right
smart woman, an’ between her an’ me, before another day’s over,
things will be all straightened out clear. Just you be patient an’
keep your mouth shut tight on all this.--Take my car an’ drive back.
Leave it at the jail. I won’t need it for a while.’

‘But, Ez, don’t you even want to know about Miss Hawley? She’s
been----’

‘Never mind now. Think I could guess pretty accurate myself! We’ll
talk that over later on. Now, git!’

Sewell turned abruptly away, retracing his steps toward the barn.
As they had left it a moment ago, he had been careful to see that
the French window they used was not latched behind them. Now he
stepped through it again into King’s workshop. Withdrawing a clean
handkerchief from his pocket, he wiped off the hinges and lock of the
rear window, then performed the same operation on the one at the side
of the room near where the guests had sat. This done, he rolled the
cloth into a ball, wrapped it in a piece of paper, and retraced his
steps to the road, setting off toward the village at a brisk walk.

Fifteen minutes later he called Mrs. Bascom on the jail telephone and
seemed gratified by the apparent calmness of her tone.

‘Ellen, just answer yes or no to what I ask you, see?--Now, can any
one hear what I’m sayin’ except you?--Good. Then here’s what I’d
suggest doin’. Keep _every one_ away from Sylvia, even Martha. Lock
yourself in with her if necessary, but try not to arouse suspicion
an’, whatever you do, don’t let ’em know if she comes to. Otherwise,
try to act natural, an’ if you ever played a part, play that one for
all you’re worth.

‘Nothin’ will happen to-night--that I’ll guarantee. I’ll be up there
if I can, but don’t go out of your way to talk to me, an’ don’t leave
your room to-night--not for anythin’.--Understand all that?--I’m
bankin’ on you a lot, Ellen B.--That’s all.’

He hung up the receiver abruptly, next calling Dr. Smith and
listening gravely to his diagnosis of Sylvia’s condition.

‘Think she’ll be able to stand a little talkin’ an’ excitement by
to-morrow?--That’s good.--Well, I’m askin’ you to do somethin’
else for me now, Doctor. Pete here will be over shortly with a
handkerchief of mine. It’s kind of oily. Could you soak it out
somehow an’ find out what kind of oil it is?--Fine.--I’ll pick you
up to-morrow mornin’ about nine o’clock, an’ we’ll go up to King’s
house, where I guess everythin’ can be settled right prompt. See you
then.’

Pete had been listening to this conversation, so received the
handkerchief with a silent nod, as Sewell beckoned him to follow to
Ruel’s cell where he paused outside, speaking quite briskly.

‘Son, it’s all over but the shoutin’ now, I hope, though there may be
quite some of that.’

‘You mean--you know who killed King?’

‘Yep. But I ain’t tellin’ you or any one else--yet. Now here’s what
I came to say. I’ve still got to prove it an’ you’ve got to help me.
You an’ Pete here have an all-night job ahead of you. Now take this
calm, Ruel. The truth is, Sylvia’s comin’ around all right, but she’s
been pretty bad. Some one went an’ doped her, drugged her to keep her
mouth shut.’

‘Doped--Sylvia?’ Ruel shouted, shaking the bars as if to wrench them
from the masonry. ‘Who? Why? Let me out of here, Ez. I’ll----’

‘Shut up!’ commanded Sewell sharply. ‘I told you she was all right
now an’ I also said I needed your help. If you can’t control
yourself, I’ll say no more--leave you here an’ get some one as is
willin’ to act sensible.’

This threat sobered Ruel at once, but he gripped the bars until his
knuckles showed white.

‘Ellen B. helped to discover that, an’ she’s lookin’ after Sylvia
now.’

‘God bless Ellen B.,’ muttered Ruel fervently.

‘You said it, son. Now, then, there’s nothin’ to worry
about--absolutely nothin’. To-night Pete here will let you out of
that cell. You an’ him sneak up to King’s house, an’ don’t get seen
doin’ it either. When it’s dark, you hide in the bushes on this side
of it, Pete on the farther side. There’s a man in the house who might
try to sneak away, though I don’t think so. However, it’s up to you
two not to let him--to see that no one leaves durin’ the night--no
one.’

The men nodded, Pete with apparent languor, though his eyes sparkled
with interest, Ruel with set jaw and tense muscles. Sewell glanced at
the latter quizzically as he remarked, ‘Guess no one will get away on
your side, Ruel!--An’ incidentally, I guess these are your last hours
in jail, so make the most of ’em.--Get along on that errand now,
Pete.’

When the latter had gone, Sewell went into the office, took out his
little box of clues, tied them in a neat bundle, and stowed them back
in the safe again. Next he seated himself at the desk to write.
There were long pauses for thought and many lines were rewritten
several times. At last he sank back in his chair, read the result
of his labors twice, then tore the pages into bits. Thus Sewell
marshaled his facts and arguments in preparation for the following
day.

A telephone call to the judge made an appointment for the latter to
come to the jail at half-past seven that evening, then Sewell set
about preparing supper, whistling and humming quite light-heartedly.

Prompt to the minute, Judge Brant’s car drew up before the jail and
he bustled into the office, smoking a long fragrant cigar.

‘Here I am, Sewell. Have one?’ He offered a cigar to the inspector as
he settled down in the empty chair, drawn up into the light of the
lamp.

‘No, thanks, Judge. I ain’t used to highfalutin smokes. A corncob is
more suitin’ to me, an’ don’t take my mind off things by makin’ me
think how much I ought to be enjoyin’ the smoke.’

The judge chuckled. ‘Well, Inspector, is this another of your
conferences?’

‘Yep, sort of. More talkin’, Judge, but perhaps you’ll get some
actin’ for a change to-morrow.--How’s everythin’ up to the house?’

‘About the same. Mrs. Bascom had a headache and is staying in her
room; Miss Hawley is still asleep. It is certainly unfortunate--her
condition, just at this time.’

‘Ain’t it?--especially when she might be able to clear up
things--about the will business, for instance.’

Judge Brant nodded skeptically. He doubted this statement, but did
not bother to deny it. His face, lighted by the full glare of the
lamp, showed lines of worry, appeared weary and puzzled as if his
thoughts were troubling him, and his eyes rather avoided Sewell’s.

‘I suppose this case ain’t been run accordin’ to detective methods,
exactly,’ said the latter, clearing his throat noisily. ‘I’ll admit I
ain’t a go-gettin’ sleuth like in King’s stories, but to me, it ain’t
been the kind of thing such methods could apply to, if you know what
I mean. For instance, there’s been too few clues. Again, there’s been
too much of this keepin’ things secret, not offerin’ information like
people ought to, if they are anxious to clear up a mystery.’

‘To whom do you refer?’ asked the judge rather absently, as if only
half interested in Sewell’s words.

‘You!’

‘Me? I--I don’t understand!’ His interest no longer wavered. His
eyes, narrowed and searching, fixed upon Sewell’s, his cigar spilled
ashes unnoticed upon his immaculately brushed clothes.

‘About King. Why didn’t you tell me what you knew, Judge?’

‘What I knew?’ echoed the latter, but his face flushed with the
effort to keep his voice calm.

For reply, Ezra fumbled in his pocket and placed upon the table
between them the slip of paper that he had abstracted from the
judge’s portfolio. The latter glanced down at it, unable to conceal a
start of surprise.

‘I don’t see what you are driving at, Sewell. I suppose you found
that in my room. Well, it’s just a--a case in which I was interested.’

‘I’m kind of interested in it too--about King, alias Jameson, eh?’

The judge’s bluster, like a pricked bubble, vanished. ‘You--you know
that?’

‘An’ a good deal more. An’ I repeat, why didn’t you tell me?’

The judge stared incredulously at the inspector, but this time his
eyes did not waver under the other’s scrutiny. He became intent,
nervous, as he leaned forward, his fingers crushing the paper into a
ball.

‘You’re insinuating I had something else to conceal--some knowledge
of the murder, I suppose? I swear to you it’s not true. I had a
reason for keeping this knowledge from you, but not the one you’re
imputing.’

‘I guess I believe you, Judge, but tell me about it,’ replied Sewell,
knocking out the ashes from his pipe and preparing to refill it.

‘How much do you know?’ countered the judge.

‘What the police records an’ newspapers told.’

‘Then there is a little more I can add.’ There was a distinct relief
in his tone now. ‘I found it out only yesterday when I was in Boston.
That number in King’s dressing-gown pocket--when I first saw it I
felt sure I ought to recognize it, yet couldn’t. It’s been puzzling
me. Yesterday I remembered certain private documents King had given
me some weeks ago. They were kept separate from his other papers,
so I had almost forgotten them. When I looked them over, I found a
letter addressed to me which I had opened before and put aside. It
contained an instruction that in case any peculiar accident should
occur to him, I was to go to a certain safety-deposit box in a bank.
This was to be done only in case I should think it necessary.

‘I had not understood his instructions at the time, and he refused
to explain them. That’s why they made little impression on me. But
the safety-deposit box number was the same as that on the slip--his
prison number.

‘I obtained the specified paper, found it was his confession of
his imprisonment. But in it he denied all the charges against him,
disclaimed any knowledge of either the murder or the underhanded
business of his partner. Two other facts were important. First, he
admitted learning, by chance, where his partner had hidden some of
his illicit gains. There was no way of returning them or making
restitution, so he said nothing about it at the trial. He obtained
them when he was released, and made a fresh start. Part of the funds
he used for this purpose, but later paid them back scrupulously,
devoting the entire fund to charity--to helping others in distress,
and so on. His accounting of it is all there, and it was his moral
restitution, I suppose.

‘The second fact bears on his death, or so it seems to me. He stated
that for some months he had been receiving slips of paper with his
prison number on them--nothing more. He feared this was blackmail and
left this explanation, obviously in case--well, in case of his sudden
death through other than natural causes.’

‘That corroborates things pretty well, especially Ellen B.’s ideas.
An’ it was just like him to go an’ choose that special number for his
lock box.--Interestin’--kinks of human nature, ain’t they? But why
didn’t you tell me?’

‘The revelation of his past stunned me. It was like defaming the
dead to bring it all to light again. I knew I should tell you, but I
hesitated, tried to believe it would not be necessary. Oh, I might
as well tell the whole truth. I feared you would not be discreet,
Sewell. Frankly, I did not have much confidence in you, feared you
might let it all become known unnecessarily, so meant to get a
detective of whom I was more certain. I apologize, sincerely. I’ve
misjudged you and----’

‘Never mind that part. It’s done no real harm, lettin’ some others
think I’m a lazy sort of fool too.’

‘You mean you know who is guilty?’

‘I do. Bein’ a good listener comes in handy sometimes, Judge, an’
often words do speak louder than actions. But I’m wanderin’ from the
road. You haven’t told any one else?’

‘Not a soul. And you? Must the world be told?’

‘Not if I can help it. You seem to forget he was my friend, too, an’
the same to lots of others hereabouts.’ Sewell’s words were sharp and
indignant, but only for the moment. ‘Well, Judge, that clears up your
end quite satisfactory. Maybe I can count on you to help as well as
be--er--discreet, seein’ as we understand each other, eh?’

‘Absolutely. If there is anything I can do----’

‘Seems to me I’ve heard you say them words before,’ remarked Ezra
pointedly, ‘but this time I’ll take ’em at face value an’ trust
you’ve told me everythin’. Just forget about what we’ve been sayin’,
see? If any one asks you why I called you down here, say--well, tell
’em it was one of my usual blunderin’ conferences!’

‘I’ll play up, Sewell. But who is the person you suspect? Do I know
him?’

‘Certain sure, but I ain’t quite ready to tell you--yet. You might be
more help not knowin’, see? But I figure it won’t be long before you
do. This much I’ll say--it’s some one as is in King’s house now!’

‘Then why don’t you arrest him?’

‘Don’t worry. I will when the time’s ripe. Look here, Judge, I’ve got
two things you can do to help. The first is, sleep light to-night an’
keep one ear an’ eye open. I don’t want any one leavin’ that house
to-night. The other request is simpler--drive me up to the house now
for a spell.’

‘Of course, I will--and that answers for both,’ nodded the judge, his
whole attitude having been completely revolutionized. ‘You may be
sure I’ll watch carefully too. Ready to go right away?’

Ezra pocketed his pipe without knocking out the smoking ashes, took
up his battered felt hat, then blew out the light and followed the
judge to his waiting car. He nodded to Pete, who was loitering on the
curb, gossiping with one of the villagers, then they drove off in
silence. Yet, when Basil Stacey and Dr. New arose to greet them from
King’s piazza, Sewell became even more loquacious than usual. After
they had talked for some time, he suggested they play a game of cards.

‘You needn’t look so darned shocked,’ he added with a chuckle at
their startled expressions. ‘I’m human, an’ it’s good to relax with
a game once in a while. Sometimes I’ve wished we had more company
down at the jail for that reason, ’cause two can’t play much, an’,
besides, I know all the ways Pete cheats! To-night I kind of feel
like breakin’ out a bit, throwin’ over the traces for relief, you
might say, seein’ as the detective will be takin’ over my job
to-morrow, I guess.’

When they saw his suggestion was made in earnest, they agreed to it
readily, starting for the smoking-room to set up a table and cards.
As they stepped toward the door, Sewell touched Basil’s arm and
motioned for him to hang back after the others.

‘Stacey,’ whispered the inspector authoritatively as he loudly
knocked out the ashes from his pipe as an excuse for delaying, ‘watch
out to-night. One of those men may try to leave. See that he doesn’t
quit the house, understand?’

‘You think that----’

‘I’m givin’ orders, not answerin’ questions, an’ if you know what’s
good for you, do as I say. Play up, now.’

Before the surprised Stacey could speak again, Sewell pushed him into
the hall toward the smoking-room.

‘What shall it be, gentlemen?’ asked the doctor as he shuffled the
cards.

‘Know whist? It’s kind of takin’ advantage of you, my bein’ the
champion of the county, but it’s about the only game I know.’

The others assented and the evening passed quickly. Ezra proved
his boast convincingly, the while flavoring the conversation with
stories and sly jokes so that no one had time to revert to the
mystery. A little after eleven, he stretched, yawned frankly, and
pushed back his chair.

‘You night owls can play as long as you want, but I’m goin’ to bed.’

‘Want me to drive you down, Sewell?’ asked Judge Brant.

‘No. Walkin’ will make me all the sleepier. Thanks for the game.’ He
smiled and jingled some newly acquired silver in his pocket.

At the doorway he addressed the doctor over his shoulder.

‘By the way, do you think there’s a chance of Miss Hawley bein’ up
to-morrow when the detective comes? I’m expectin’ him by the first
train.’

‘Rather doubtful, I’m afraid, Sewell.’

‘Wish she might. There’ll be questions to ask.’

‘Well, Mrs. Bascom said she was still--er--asleep when I stopped at
her door. That doesn’t look very hopeful.’

Sewell nodded and beckoned. While he had been speaking, he stepped
into the hall, the doctor naturally following him. Now they were out
of sight of the others and Ezra leaned close to whisper in his ear.

‘Can’t explain now, but I want those two men watched. Don’t let
either of ’em slip out of the house to-night. It’s important.’

Dr. New grabbed at Sewell to restrain him for a question, but the
latter eluded him and, placing a finger on his lips, hurried down the
hall, calling back aloud as he stood in the open door, ‘Good-night,
gentlemen, an’ thanks for a mighty pleasant an’ profitable evenin’.’

This remark was largely for the benefit of his two watchers, Ruel and
Pete, who should be on duty by now. As Ezra walked toward the road,
he glanced sharply about for them, but neither was visible, so he
nodded contentedly to himself, walked away out of sight of the house,
then cut into the field and circled back until he was in front of it
again. Here he squatted in the grass near a tree and waited, his eyes
fixed on the lighted hall.

A half-hour passed, and he began to nod gently, bringing his head
up with a jerk every now and then. After repeating this painful
process several times, he jabbed his thumb into his ribs viciously
and muttered, ‘Here you, Ezra, wake up. You’re a fine specimen of a
guard, you are.’

He glanced quickly at the house again. All the upper rooms were dark,
but the hall light burned brightly and a reflected light on the
bushes at one side showed the smoking-room was still illumined.

‘Hump! Wonder if they’re takin’ my remark serious an’ are goin’ to
play all night?’

He chuckled, and looked at his radium-dialed watch.

‘Gettin’ on to midnight. I never should have stayed up so late last
night. It ain’t good trainin’ for guard duty, leastwise not for an
old man like me.’

With a yawn he got stiffly to his feet, knowing it was too dark to be
seen from the house, stretched his arms and legs luxuriously, then
began to chuckle to himself heartily. A moment later he slapped his
leg softly, then slunk away in the general direction of the village.

Twenty minutes later he let himself into the dark jail and slipped
into his room, undressing without turning on the light. As he sank
back between the sheets of his bed, he snickered again.

‘Yep, you’re deliberately desertin’ your post, Ezra Sewell, but no
one will ever know it an’ you’ve earned a good night’s rest. Besides,
ain’t it a sign of a big man who can get others to do his work for
him?--Ha! I’d like to be a skeeter on the wall of that room so I
could see those three men, each watchin’ the other an’ suspectin’
him!--As to Ruel, it’ll do him good, havin’ a night out, an’ it’ll
keep him out of mischief, playin’ watch-dog hero. An’ Pete--well,
it’s time that lazy critter earned his salt. Yes, you take a good
snooze, Ezra, an’ to-morrow----’

The rest was lost in a yawn, quickly followed by rhythmic breathing
and a whistling snore.

 CHAPTER XVIII

 THE WHOLE TRUTH


When Ruel and Pete entered the jail the following morning at seven
o’clock as Sewell had instructed, the latter had breakfast ready and
waiting. His invitation to partake of it needed no repetition.

‘Peaceful night, eh? I didn’t see a soul go out, did you?’ the
inspector questioned them as they sat down at the table.

‘Nary a sign of one,’ said Pete.

‘Nor I,’ reported Ruel. ‘Where were you, Ez?’

‘Oh, I circled back a bit after I left the house,’ responded the
inspector vaguely.

‘Those three men sat up half the night playing cards. I could watch
them through the window,’ added Ruel as he sipped his coffee.

‘I thought they might. Well, stock up, boys, an’ then rest a bit till
we meet Detective Bartrum on the train an’ go up to King’s.’

‘Rest? Not I, Ez. Do we all go together?’

‘Yep, that is, providin’ you’ll obey orders an’ behave,’ nodded
Sewell. ‘An’ for my part, I’ll promise to clean up the mystery.’

Ruel’s eyes pleaded for more information, but he stifled his
questions when the inspector shook his head.

Breakfast over, the three men made short work of cleaning and
putting away the dishes, then Ezra set about tidying up the already
neat room. It proved a rather futile task. Ruel kept walking about
nervously, moving a chair as soon as Ezra had carefully straightened
it or absently picking up some object and replacing it in the wrong
spot. At first, Sewell merely grunted and hastened to set them to
rights again, but at length he gave it up when it appeared to be
rapidly becoming a sort of endless game of checkers--a series of
moves and jumps without purpose.

He sat down to smoke, but Ruel’s nervousness had proved infectious.
His pipe would not draw well, puffed out ashes unexpectedly; the
smoke bit his tongue. He glanced repeatedly at his watch. At last, he
got to his feet, procured the clues from the safe, and started for
the door. Here he collided violently with Ruel, who apologized in a
breathless tone.

‘Take it easy,’ admonished Sewell kindly. ‘You might expend some of
your superfluous energy fillin’ the radiator with water, if you’ve a
mind to, son.’

This humble duty accomplished, Ruel sprang into the seat beside Ezra,
Pete in the rear, and they drove quickly to the station. Of course
the train was late, but at last it puffed into sight, tooted, and
panted to a stop at the small platform. Two men got down after the
conductor and turned toward the main street. Both were natives of the
village.

Ezra looked up and down the platform expectantly, but the train
pulled out again, and Bartrum, the detective, had not arrived.

‘Guess he slipped up somehow,’ explained Sewell, bolstering up his
own disappointment. ‘I’d kind of figured on his help a bit, but with
a little changin’ of plans, we can get along without him, all right.
Just as well, maybe, at that.’

He took this mishap philosophically, but the ride to King’s house was
a silent one as he appeared to be thinking seriously. They picked
up Dr. Smith on the way, but he, too, sensed Ezra’s abstraction, so
remained mute after a friendly greeting. When they drew up before the
house, Sewell gave a low command to his henchmen.

‘We’ll meet in King’s den. Ruel, stay by the door; Pete, by the
window. You, Doctor, watch out for any tricks.’

They entered the hall just as Mrs. Bascom appeared at the top of the
stairs and looked questioningly at Ezra. He shook his head in the
negative, and she disappeared silently.

A little pile of luggage--four suit-cases, a brief-case, and a small
handbag--lay on the floor near the hatrack. These Ezra recognized as
belonging to Judge Brant and Dr. New. One of them he picked up and
handed to Basil, whispering, ‘Take it to the den an’ kind of put it
out of sight. Wait there.’

His order obeyed, Sewell called out briskly, ‘Hey there, anybody to
home?’

A door opened somewhere above and Judge Brant appeared in the upper
hall. Dr. New came almost simultaneously from the rear hall. Each
seemed on the point of asking an eager question when he noticed the
other and stopped.

‘’Mornin’, gentlemen. Have a good game after I left?’

‘We did play quite a while,’ yawned the judge, glancing sidewise at
Dr. New.

‘Sure. Gettin’ ready to leave, were you?’ He nodded toward the
luggage.

‘Temporarily,’ answered Dr. New. ‘Judge Brant offered me a ride and
we both have to go for the day, at least, so decided it would be a
good chance to take most of our things with us. We thought that as
soon as this new man had asked any questions he might want to put,
we’d be off. Of course we’ll come back, if necessary.’

‘Guess that man ain’t comin’--just yet.’

‘No? That’s unfortunate, as I really must be in town by noon at the
latest.’

‘Oh, that’s all right. I think we can fix things so nobody much need
wait around longer. But how about your patient, Doctor?’

‘Miss Hawley? I was going to speak to you about her. There’s really
nothing I can do that your village doctor can’t attend to quite well.
Of course, it’s an interesting case and I shall hope to follow it
closely, but I can leave directions and all that quite easily.’

‘I brought him along, thinkin’ he might prove handy,’ said Ezra.
‘Glad I did now. Then you think her condition ain’t really serious?’

‘I hope not. She’s still unconscious, you know,’ he added for the
benefit of the other doctor. ‘She hasn’t been able to speak and
explain her symptoms, so for that reason I may be mistaken in my
diagnosis.’

‘Glad to hear you say so, Doctor,’ Sewell gravely answered. ‘Where’s
Stacey?’

‘Around somewhere. Shall I find him?’

‘Wish you would, Judge, an’ bring him along to the den.’

Ezra did not seem to notice the judge’s gesture which sought a
private word with him. As soon as he had gone on his errand, Sewell
moved away toward the den and Dr. New seized his opportunity
to remark in a low tone, ‘We played cards rather late, and
I--er--followed your directions.’

‘Good. No trouble?’

‘None, though both men seemed keyed up, watchful and nervous, rather
furtive at times. But what does it mean? This detective----’

‘Far as I know he’ll be along sometime, but I guess it don’t make any
difference. I don’t need him after all.’

‘What? You mean----’

‘Yep, I know who killed King, how an’ why,’ drawled the inspector.
‘Ah, here’s Stacey. How are you, Basil?’

The latter nodded as he entered the den with the judge. He seemed
surprised by the cordiality of Sewell’s tone. Then he noticed Ruel,
rather tense and silent, by the door and greeted him with an effort
at heartiness.

‘Glad to see you again, Ruel. Out on parole or--well, what?’

‘For good,’ answered Sewell succinctly. ‘Now we’re all here, let’s
get down to horses an’ sheep. No, hold on a bit. Ellen B. ain’t here.
I’ll call her.’

‘Too bad Miss Hawley can’t join the group,’ added Judge Brant.

Ezra disregarded this remark, disappearing into the hall. They heard
him call out to Mrs. Bascom. As he passed Ruel in returning to the
room, he whispered, ‘Hold hard, son, she’s comin’,’ then walked to
the desk, where he stood staring silently at Dr. New. The latter
returned his gaze questioningly, but his lips twitched slightly as
the silence remained unbroken, save for Mrs. Bascom’s advancing
footsteps, curiously slow and halting.

It was one of those peculiarly electric moments when, without a word
spoken, every one seemed to feel a tenseness in the air, to expect
something neither definite nor understandable. Mrs. Bascom’s approach
seemed somehow significant, ominous, yet there was no reason for such
a feeling. The doctor’s eyes shifted nervously toward the doorway;
every one’s gaze, except Sewell’s, riveted there.

Mrs. Bascom appeared in the doorway, gravely paused, half turned
about, held out her hand, and Sylvia stepped to her side! The latter
was dressed in a becoming frock and her face, though a trifle wan,
was flushed with excitement.

Ruel started forward eagerly, but after one step, drew himself back
to his post. For once, hard as it was, he obeyed orders, though his
eyes spoke to Sylvia’s even more expressively than his actions could
have done. In silence Mrs. Bascom led her to a chair, holding her
hand encouragingly as she stood beside her. Dr. New’s face became
suddenly pallid as he leaned forward, winking to clear his vision.

‘Miss Hawley,’ he muttered in an unnatural tone, ‘what are you
doing--up--like this? I thought----’

‘Yes, your diagnosis was wrong, eh, Doctor? She came to quick enough
once we discovered what ailed her--dope!’

‘Dope?’ The doctor whirled toward Ezra. He was now completely master
of himself again through an heroic effort, his expression one of
incredulity. ‘You mean to insinuate I drugged her?’

‘She was your patient.’

‘But who told you that--said she was drugged?’

‘I did,’ answered Dr. Smith levelly. ‘She was not only doped with
medicine, but had been given a hypodermic injection, cleverly
concealed under the band of her wristwatch.’ Their eyes met, Dr.
Smith’s frankly accusing and hard, Dr. New’s snapping with anger.

‘Then you’re a poor specimen of a medical man!’ sneered the latter.
‘The idea is sheer idiocy. Why should I----But wait. It’s just
possible I might have confused those pills. I happened to have two
unlabeled bottles, though of different shapes, and one held a mild
drug. Of course I may have seized the wrong one in a hurry----’

‘And I might requote your epithet on that score, Doctor! Besides, who
gave the hypodermic?’ Again their eyes met, and this time there was
anger in both pairs.

‘Look here, Sewell,’ blustered Dr. New, deliberately turning his back
on his accuser, ‘do you mean to stand there and let me be insulted by
a mere country quack?’

‘Yep, an’ to add a country constable’s insult, let me expand the
accusation of druggin’ to breakin’ with the intent to steal, an’
murder!’

Dr. New sprang toward the inspector, swinging his clenched fist back
to strike, only to have it seized firmly by the other physician. His
eyes blazed, and his breath whistled through his compressed lips as
he tugged to free his hand. But the physical action seemed to calm
